# WILEY SERIES IN PROBABILITY AND STATISTICS

Established by *Walter A. Shewhart and Samuel S. Wilks*

Editors: *David J. Balding, Noel A. C. Cressie, Garrett M. Fitzmaurice, Geof H. Givens, Geert Molenberghs, David W. Scott, Adrian F. M. Smith, Ruey S. Tsay*

Editors Emeriti: *Harvey Goldstein, J. Stuart Hunter, Iain M. Johnstone, Joseph B. Kadane, Jozef L. Teugels*

The ***Wiley Series in Probability and Statistics*** is well established and authoritative. It covers many topics of current research interest in both pure and applied statistics and probability theory. Written by leading statisticians and institutions, the titles span both state‐of‐the‐art developments in the field and classical methods.

Reflecting the wide range of current research in statistics, the series encompasses applied, methodological and theoretical statistics, ranging from applications and new techniques made possible by advances in computerized practice to rigorous treatment of theoretical approaches. This series provides essential and invaluable reading for all statisticians, whether in academia, industry, government, or research.

A complete list of titles in this series can be found at [http://www.wiley.com/go/wsps](http://www.wiley.com/go/wsps)

# Preface

This book is the result of a long‐standing collaboration among the three authors, which began when Carla Moreira was a PhD student under the supervision of Jacobo de Uña‐Álvarez. Carla successfully defended her thesis, entitled ‘The Statistical Analysis of Doubly Truncated Data: New Methods, Software Development, and Biomedical Applications’, at the Universidade de Vigo in July 2010\. At that time, only a small number of people seemed to be aware of the importance of random double truncation. Research papers on this topic were scarce before 2010, with the contribution by Bradley Efron and Vahe Petrosian in 1999 as the most relevant one. And, of course, no software was available. So, for us, it was a risky and exciting research exercise to embrace such an initiative.

We launched version 1.1 of our `R` package `DTDA` in September 2009\. To our knowledge this was the first software library implementing the Efron–Petrosian estimator. The package included Efron and Petrosian's data on quasar luminosities, and we are very thankful to both scientists for sharing them. `DTDA` has been downloaded more than 45 thousand times up to now. We have taken the opportunity of writing this book to update and enhance `DTDA`, feeding it with new illustrative real datasets and enabling new functions and capabilities. We are confident in that the update of the package and the guidance provided by this book will exponentially increase the applications involving doubly truncated data, and also raise awareness about the implications of double truncation on inferential procedures.

Over these years, several researchers have collaborated with us in the fascinating adventure of investigating double truncation. Among them, we would like to mention Ingrid Van Keilegom, Micha Mandel, Rebecca Betensky, Luis Meira‐Machado and Roel Braekers. We have enjoyed co‐authoring a number of research papers with them. We also learned a lot about double truncation by studying real data problems posed by applied researchers; here we thank María José Bento, David Keith Simon, Zhi‐Sheng Ye, Ana Cristina Santos and Henrique Barros for fruitful discussions and cooperation.

Nowadays, there is a considerable statistical community doing research on exploratory and inferential methods for doubly truncated data, partly motivated by new emerging applications in Biomedicine, Economics and Engineering, among other fields. At the time of writing the activity in this area of research is much more intense than ever before, as is evident from the number of papers on the topic published in the last couple of years. And the interest in double truncation is growing faster and faster!

This book aims to serve as a companion for those ones interested in learning about doubly truncated data analysis and inference, presenting a wide range of tools for estimating distribution and regression models. All the methods presented in this book are accompanied by real data and simulated examples and, at the end of each chapter, the reader will find the *do‐it‐yourself* code, mostly based on the `DTDA` package. This book is not written with the aim of being just read: its main purpose is to invite the reader to think, explore and experience.

This volume is also self‐contained, providing a general overview on the main results. Further technical details and some omitted proofs can be consulted in the original references. It is also in our intention to leave several take‐home messages. First, that the correction of the potential sampling bias arising from double truncation may be critical in estimation and inference. Second, that, even when the Efron–Petrosian estimator is conceptually complicated and its asymptotic theory may be overwhelming, its practical application is relatively simple from the available software packages and the good performance of resampling algorithms. Third, that external information on the sampling bias should be used whenever available, since the Efron–Petrosian estimator may be very noisy or even non‐existing, particularly when the sample size is small to moderate.

We frankly hope that the reader will enjoy (and experience!) the book, at least as much as we have enjoyed writing it! Comments and suggestions from the readers on this edition are welcome; please send them to [`jacobo@uvigo.es`](mailto:jacobo@uvigo.es) to help us to improve the book.

Parts of this book were written while the authors were supported by the Grants MTM2017‐89422‐P (MINECO/AEI/FEDER, UE) (first author), UIDB/00013/2020 and UIDP/00013/2020 (second author), and MTM2016‐76969‐P (MINECO/AEI/FEDER, UE) (third author). This is acknowledged.

| May   2021 |      | *Jacobo de Uña‐Álvarez, Carla Moreira and Rosa M. Crujeiras Vigo, V. N. Famalicão and Santiago de Compostela* |

# 1
Introduction

## 1.1 Random Truncation

Random truncation generally refers to a situation in which a number of individuals of the target population cannot be sampled because a certain random event precludes them. When this random event is unrelated to the variables of interest standard statistical methods apply, with the only inconvenience of using a smaller sample size. In many practical cases, however, the truncation event is related to the variables under study, and specific methods to overcome the sampling bias must be considered.

This book is focused on random truncation phenomena that arise (usually, but not only) when sampling time‐to‐event data. That is, the variable of interest is the time ![upper X](images/c01-i0001.png) elapsed from a well‐defined origin to another well‐defined end point. In this setting, a truncated sample of ![upper X](images/c01-i0002.png) is a set of independent and identically distributed (iid) random variables ![upper X 1 comma ellipsis comma upper X Subscript n Baseline](images/c01-i0003.png) with the conditional distribution of ![upper X](images/c01-i0004.png) given ![upper X element-of upper B](images/c01-i0005.png), where ![upper B](images/c01-i0006.png) is a random set. Since the truncation event ![left-brace upper X element-of upper B right-brace](images/c01-i0007.png) is obviously related to ![upper X](images/c01-i0008.png), standard statistical methods applied to the truncated sample may be systematically biased. For example, the ordinary empirical cumulative distribution function (ecdf) of ![upper X 1 comma ellipsis comma upper X Subscript n Baseline](images/c01-i0009.png) at point ![x](images/c01-i0010.png), ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0011.png), converges to ![upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x vertical-bar upper X element-of upper B right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0012.png) rather than to the target cumulative distribution function (cdf) ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0013.png). This problem has received remarkable attention since the seminal paper by Turnbull ([1976](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0017)). Special forms of truncation when sampling time‐to‐event data are reviewed in [Sections 1.2](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0002) and [1.3](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0007).

Time‐to‐event data are relevant in fields like Survival Analysis and Reliability Engineering, in which random truncation often occurs. Random truncation is found in Astronomy too, where ![upper X](images/c01-i0014.png) represents the luminosity of an stellar object that is subject to observation limits. Examples from these areas will be introduced and analysed throughout this book.

## 1.2 One‐sided Truncation

### 1.2.1 Left‐truncation

Left‐truncation is a common feature when sampling time‐to‐event data. A left‐truncation time for the target ![upper X](images/c01-i0015.png) is defined as a random variable ![upper U](images/c01-i0016.png) such that ![upper X](images/c01-i0017.png) is observed only when ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X](images/c01-i0018.png), determining the random set ![upper B](images/c01-i0019.png) in the previous section.

Left‐truncation occurs, for example, with cross‐sectional sampling, where the sampled individuals are those being between the origin and the end point at a certain calendar time, which is the cross‐section date (Wang, [1991](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0018)). That is, the observer arrives at the process at a given date, being allowed to observe the time‐to‐event ![upper X](images/c01-i0020.png) and the left‐truncation time ![upper U](images/c01-i0021.png) for the individuals 'in progress' by that date. With cross‐sectional sampling, the variable ![upper U](images/c01-i0022.png) is simply defined as the time from onset to the cross‐section date. This sampling procedure is often applied because it entails relatively little effort to reach a pre‐specified sampling size. In medical research, such a design leads to the sampling of the so‐called prevalent cases: patients already diagnosed from a certain disease of interest who survived beyond the cross‐section date. Clearly, such a sampling design implies an observational bias, in the sense that individuals with longer survival (the ![upper X](images/c01-i0023.png) value) will be observed with a relatively large probability. There exist well investigated proposals to overcome such a bias, based on the simple idea of taking the observed left‐truncation times into account to define suitable risk sets. For this purpose, independence between ![upper X](images/c01-i0024.png) and ![upper U](images/c01-i0025.png) has been traditionally assumed. This independence assumption states that the time‐to‐event distribution remains unchanged along time, being unrelated to the date of onset. A classical example of left‐truncation are the Channing House data, where the age at death is measured for people living in that retirement centre; in this case, the target variable is left‐truncated by the age when entering the residence (Klein and Moeschberger, [2003](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0010)).

Another feature leading to left‐truncation is the delayed entry into study. This happens when the individuals enter the study only at some random time ![upper U](images/c01-i0026.png) after onset. For example, diagnosis of a certain disease may not be ascertained until the first visit to the hospital. If the 'end‐of‐disease' event occurs before the potential date of visit, the time‐to‐event of such a patient will be never known, with the resulting difficulty in observing relatively small event times. Beyersmann et al. ([2012](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0003)) provide an illustrative example of this issue in the investigation of abortion times.

### 1.2.2 Right‐truncation

In some particular settings, the target variable of ultimate interest ![upper X](images/c01-i0027.png) is observed only for the individuals who experience the event before a certain calendar time ![d 1](images/c01-i0028.png). A typical example of such a situation is the investigation of the incubation (or induction) times for AIDS; see for example Klein and Moeschberger ([2003](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0010)). The incubation time is defined as the time elapsed between the date of HIV infection, ![d Subscript normal upper I](images/c01-i0029.png) say, and the development of AIDS. If ![upper X](images/c01-i0030.png) stands for the incubation time and ![upper V equals d 1 minus d Subscript normal upper I](images/c01-i0031.png), then the incubation times of individuals developing AIDS prior ![d 1](images/c01-i0032.png) follow the distribution of ![upper X](images/c01-i0033.png) conditionally on ![upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c01-i0034.png). Here, ![upper V](images/c01-i0035.png) is called the right‐truncation time. An immediate effect of right‐truncation is that large values of ![upper X](images/c01-i0036.png) are sampled with a relatively small probability.

### 1.2.3 Truncation vs. Censoring

At this point, the reader may be curious about the difference between truncation and censoring. Right‐censoring is a very well known phenomenon in Survival Analysis and reliability studies, among other fields. It happens when the follow‐up of a given individual stops before the event of interest has taken place. In such a case, the observer only knows that the target variable is larger than the registered value, which is referred to as censoring time. A sample made up of real and censored values is typically analysed by the Kaplan–Meier estimator (Kaplan and Meier, [1958](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0009)), which corrects for the fact that some of the recorded values for ![upper X](images/c01-i0037.png) are smaller than the true ones. With truncated data, every value in the sample corresponds to a true observation of ![upper X](images/c01-i0038.png); however, the distribution of the observed values may be shifted with respect to the true one due to the truncation event. This difference between truncation and censoring suggests that specific methods to estimate the target distribution under random truncation should be employed. Indeed, Woodroofe ([1985](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0019)) provides a deep analysis of one‐sided truncation, introducing the original idea of Lynden–Bell ([1971](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0011)) as a nonparametric maximum likelihood estimator (NPMLE) of the probability distribution in that setting. The estimator in Woodroofe ([1985](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0019)) is a particular case of the estimator corresponding to doubly truncated data, on which this book is focused.

## 1.3 Double Truncation

A variable of interest ![upper X](images/c01-i0039.png) is said to be doubly truncated by a couple of random variables ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0040.png) if the observation of ![upper X](images/c01-i0041.png) is possible only when ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c01-i0042.png) occurs. In such a case, ![upper U](images/c01-i0043.png) and ![upper V](images/c01-i0044.png) are called left‐ and right‐truncation variables respectively. Double truncation reduces to left‐truncation when ![upper V](images/c01-i0045.png) degenerates at ![plus infinity](images/c01-i0046.png), while it corresponds to right‐truncation when ![upper P left-parenthesis upper U equals negative infinity right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c01-i0047.png). This book is focused on the problem of estimating the distribution of ![upper X](images/c01-i0048.png), and other related curves, from a set of iid triplets with the distribution of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0049.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c01-i0050.png).

There are several scenarios where double truncation appears in practice. One setting leading to double truncation is that of interval sampling, where the sample is restricted to the individuals with event between two specific dates ![d 0](images/c01-i0051.png) and ![d 1](images/c01-i0052.png) (Zhu and Wang, [2012](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0022)). Then, the right‐truncation time is ![upper V equals d 1 minus d Subscript normal upper I](images/c01-i0053.png), where ![d Subscript normal upper I](images/c01-i0054.png) denotes the date of onset for the time‐to‐event, and the left‐truncation time is ![upper U equals d 0 minus d Subscript normal upper I Baseline equals upper V minus final sigma](images/c01-i0055.png), where ![final sigma equals d 1 minus d 0](images/c01-i0056.png) is the interval width. The Childhood Cancer Data in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009) is an example of data obtained through interval sampling.

With interval sampling the variable ![upper V minus upper U](images/c01-i0057.png) is degenerated at ![final sigma](images/c01-i0058.png). This occurs in other sampling schemes too, in which ![upper U](images/c01-i0059.png) and ![upper V](images/c01-i0060.png) are certain subject‐specific event dates. An illustrative example is given by the Parkinson's Disease Data, see [Section 1.4.5](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0013), where ![upper V](images/c01-i0061.png) is the individual age at blood sampling. When ![upper V minus upper U](images/c01-i0062.png) is constant, the couple ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0063.png) falls on a line, and its joint density does not exist, even when the truncating variables may be continuous.

In other situations, the truncating variables ![upper U](images/c01-i0064.png) and ![upper V](images/c01-i0065.png) are not linked through the linear equation ![upper V equals upper U plus final sigma](images/c01-i0066.png). For example, ![upper U](images/c01-i0067.png) and ![upper V](images/c01-i0068.png) could represent some random observation limits beyond which the variable of interest ![upper X](images/c01-i0069.png) can not be sampled or detected. Situations like this occur for example in Astronomy, as it is illustrated in [Section 1.4.4](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0012).

With random double truncation, both large and small values of ![upper X](images/c01-i0070.png) are observed in principle with a relatively small probability. However, the real observational bias for ![upper X](images/c01-i0071.png) varies from application to application, depending on the joint distribution of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0072.png). We will see, for example, that the probability of sampling a value ![x](images/c01-i0073.png), namely ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to upper V vertical-bar upper X equals x right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0074.png), may be roughly constant, inducing no observational bias; or that it may be roughly decreasing, indicating the dominance of the right‐truncation bias relative to the left‐truncation bias.

Another issue of relevance is that of the identifiability of the distribution of ![upper X](images/c01-i0075.png). Intuitively it is clear that with doubly truncated data it is only possible to estimate the distribution of ![upper X](images/c01-i0076.png) conditional on ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper V](images/c01-i0077.png), where ![a Subscript upper U](images/c01-i0078.png) and ![b Subscript upper V](images/c01-i0079.png) denote respectively the lower and upper endpoints of the supports of ![upper U](images/c01-i0080.png) and ![upper V](images/c01-i0081.png) (see [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) for details). This may have important practical consequences, as we will see. On the other hand, in applications with doubly truncated survival data the estimates correspond to the susceptible population for which the terminal event of interest is sure. This is in contrast to the standard analysis of survival times where a portion of the individuals may belong to the so‐called *cured fraction*, or immunes. This should be taken into account when interpreting the results from the analysis.

An important difference of double truncation when compared to one‐sided truncation is that, with doubly truncated data, the NPMLE of the probability distribution has no explicit form. In fact, the NPMLE may be non‐unique and even non‐existing (Xiao and Hudgens, [2019](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0021)); see [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml). Several iterative algorithms that have been proposed to compute the NPMLE in practice (Efron and Petrosian, [1999](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0007); Shen, [2010](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0016)) will be reviewed in this book, and simulated and real data examples will be analysed with existing libraries of the software `R`. Semiparametric and parametric alternatives to the NPMLE will be introduced too; these approaches avoid some of the aforementioned potential issues of non‐uniqueness or non‐existence of the NPMLE, also reducing the variance at the price of introducing some bias in estimation. Also, resampling procedures, testing problems, smoothing methods, regression models and multi‐state data analysis under double truncation will be presented.

## 1.4 Real Data Examples

In this section we introduce the datasets that will be used throughout the book for illustration purposes. All of them suffer from double truncation. These examples are available within the last update of the `DTDA` package (Moreira et al., [2021a](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0014)).

### 1.4.1 Childhood Cancer Data

The Childhood Cancer Data were gathered from the IPO (*Instituto Português de Oncologia*) of Porto, Portugal, by the RORENO (Registro Oncológico do Norte) service. The information corresponds to all children diagnosed from cancer between 1 January 1999 (![d 0](images/c01-i0082.png)) and 31 December 2003 (![d 1](images/c01-i0083.png)) in the region of North Portugal, which includes five districts: Porto, Braga, Bragança, Vila Real and Viana do Castelo. The variable of main interest ![upper X](images/c01-i0084.png) is the age at diagnosis which, by definition of childhood cancer, is supported on the ![left-bracket 0 comma 15 right-bracket](images/c01-i0085.png) interval (time in years). The number of cases was 409\. However, for three cases the value of ![upper X](images/c01-i0086.png) was not available, so we only consider the ![n equals 406](images/c01-i0087.png) children who report complete information.

Because of the interval sampling, the age at diagnosis ![upper X](images/c01-i0088.png) is doubly truncated by the pair ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0089.png), where the right‐truncation variable ![upper V](images/c01-i0090.png) is the time in years from birth (date of onset, ![d Subscript normal upper I](images/c01-i0091.png)) to 31 December 2003, and ![upper U equals upper V minus 5](images/c01-i0092.png). The ![n equals 406](images/c01-i0093.png) triplets ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0094.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c01-i0095.png), with the values observed for ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0096.png) were reported in Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez (2010), while de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0006)) included the cancer group in the statistical analysis. Ordinary descriptive statistics can be applied to the information gathered along this 5 year long window to compute, for instance, the average age at cancer diagnosis. However, if the goal is to describe the population of children eventually developing cancer, the double truncation issue should be acknowledged and properly corrected, so potential biases are avoided.

Interestingly, the observed values for ![upper U](images/c01-i0097.png) range between ![negative 4.5](images/c01-i0098.png) and 14.5 (years); equivalently, the observed values for ![upper V equals upper U plus 5](images/c01-i0099.png) range between 0.5 and 19.5\. This means that the lower and upper endpoints of ![upper U](images/c01-i0100.png) and ![upper V](images/c01-i0101.png) satisfy ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to 0](images/c01-i0102.png) and ![b Subscript upper V Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to 15](images/c01-i0103.png). Thus, in this case, the target variable ![upper X](images/c01-i0104.png) is observable on its whole support ![left-bracket 0 comma 15 right-bracket](images/c01-i0105.png), and there are no identification issues for ![upper F](images/c01-i0106.png), the cdf of ![upper X](images/c01-i0107.png). Information on ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0108.png) is summarized in [Table 1.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-tbl-0001).

| Group |  | ![n](images/c01-i0110.png)  | Mean (SD) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| All |  | 406 | 6.47 (4.50) |
| By gender | Female | 178 | 6.43 (4.51) |
|  | Male | 228 | 6.51 (4.51) |
| By ICCC Group | Leukemia | 107 | 6.30 (4.15) |
|  | Lymphoma | 57 | 8.66 (4.39) |
|  | N. System Tumour |  94 | 6.38 (4.29) |
|  | Neuroblastoma |  38 | 3.16 (3.47) |
|  | Other | 105 | 6.87 (4.70) |
|  | Missing |   5 | 3.92 (5.18) |

[Table 1.1](#c01.xhtml_R_c01-tbl-0001) Descriptive statistics for Childhood Cancer Data: sample size ![n](images/c01-i0109.png) and mean (and standard deviation, SD) for the age at diagnosis (years).

This dataset is used in [Chapters 2](#c02.xhtml), [3](#c03.xhtml) and [5](#c05.xhtml) and is accessible in the `DTDA` package in `ChildCancer`.

### 1.4.2 AIDS Blood Transfusion Data

Kalbfleish and Lawless ([1989](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0008)) reported 494 cases of transfusion‐related AIDS, corresponding to individuals diagnosed prior to 1 July 1986 (![d 1](images/c01-i0111.png)). The variable of ultimate interest ![upper X](images/c01-i0112.png) is the induction or incubation time, which is the time elapsed from HIV infection to AIDS. Importantly, HIV was unknown before 1982 (![d 0](images/c01-i0113.png)); this implies that cases developing AIDS prior to this date were not reported. Let ![upper V](images/c01-i0114.png) denote the time from HIV infection to 1 July 1986 (in months), and introduce ![upper U equals upper V minus 54](images/c01-i0115.png); then, due to the interval sampling, only triplets ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper X comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0116.png) satisfying ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c01-i0117.png) were observed (Bilker and Wang, [1996](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0004)). We restrict our analysis to the ![n equals 295](images/c01-i0118.png) cases with consistent data, for which the infection could be attributed to a single transfusion or a short series of transfusions. This dataset is fully reported in Kalbfleish and Lawless ([1989](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0008)), p. 361.

The observed values of ![upper X](images/c01-i0119.png) range from 0.5 to 89 (months), while ![upper U](images/c01-i0120.png) ranges from ![negative 48.5](images/c01-i0121.png) to 45.5\. This suggests that the lower limit of the support of ![upper U](images/c01-i0122.png) is about ![negative 48.5](images/c01-i0123.png), while the upper limit of the support of ![upper V](images/c01-i0124.png) is about 99.5\. As discussed in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml), in such a case the distribution of the incubation time ![upper X](images/c01-i0125.png) is identifiable on the interval ![left-bracket 0 comma 99.5 right-bracket](images/c01-i0126.png) (months). The AIDS Blood Transfusion Data also includes information on the age of the individual at infection; see [Table 1.2](#c01.xhtml_c01-tbl-0002).

| Age group | ![n](images/c01-i0128.png)  | Mean (SD) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![less-than](images/c01-i0129.png)30 years |  56 | 27.09 (18.28) |
| 30–60 years | 104 | 33.80 (18.95) |
| ![greater-than](images/c01-i0130.png)60 years | 135 | 32.46 (16.74) |

[Table 1.2](#c01.xhtml_R_c01-tbl-0002) Descriptive statistics for the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data: sample size ![n](images/c01-i0127.png) and mean (and standard deviation, SD) for the incubation time (months) by age at infection.

This dataset is used in [Chapters 2](#c02.xhtml), [3](#c03.xhtml), [4](#c04.xhtml) and [5](#c05.xhtml) and can be obtained from `AIDS.DT` in `DTDA` package.

### 1.4.3 Equipment‐ `S` Rounded Failure Time Data

Companies are often interested in estimating the time to failure of their devices after installation. For doing this, maintenance departments may register events of failure between two specific dates ![d 0](images/c01-i0131.png) and ![d 1](images/c01-i0132.png) for the units installed in the field. This field lifetime distribution is, however, doubly truncated because of the interval sampling. The Equipment‐ `S` data (Ye and Tang, [2016](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0020)) concern ![n equals 174](images/c01-i0133.png) failures of a certain device (details are not given due to confidentiality issues) recorded between 1996 and 2011, a 15 year long observational window. Information on the date of installation and the date of failure, rounded to years, was obtained by digitizing Figure 2 in the referred paper. This dataset is therefore a discrete version of the original data in Ye and Tang (2016). In this example the right‐truncation time ![upper V](images/c01-i0134.png) is the number of years between installation and 2011, while the left‐truncation time is just ![upper U equals upper V minus 15](images/c01-i0135.png). In [Table 1.3](#c01.xhtml_c01-tbl-0003) the Equipment‐ `S` failure times are summarized.

The observable range for the Equipment‐ `S` failure times goes from zero to 34 years, which is the maximum observed value for the right‐truncating variable ![upper V](images/c01-i0136.png); hence, estimation of the reliability can only be done conditional on failure within the first 34 years of operation.

`EqSRounded` in the `DTDA` package contains this dataset, which is used in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml).

### 1.4.4 Quasar Data

A classical motivating example of doubly truncated data, introduced by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0007)), is found in cosmology when registering the luminosity of quasars. Quasars are observed only if the luminosity lies within a certain interval, bounded at both ends that are determined by detection limits of observation devices, so the data suffer from double truncation. The original dataset studied by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0007)) comprises ![n equals 210](images/c01-i0137.png) triplets ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0138.png), where ![upper X Subscript i](images/c01-i0139.png) is the luminosity in the log‐scale, obtained from a transformation model based on the redshift and the apparent magnitude of the ![i](images/c01-i0140.png)th quasar. See Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0007)) for further details on the transformation model. Due to experimental constraints, the distribution of each luminosity in the log‐scale is truncated to a known interval ![left-bracket upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-bracket](images/c01-i0141.png). Specifically, quasars with apparent magnitude above ![upper V Subscript i](images/c01-i0142.png) were too dim to yield dependent redshifts, and hence they were excluded from the study. In addition, the lower limit ![upper U Subscript i](images/c01-i0143.png) was used to avoid confusion with non‐quasar stellar objects. Some descriptive statistics are provided in [Table 1.4](#c01.xhtml_c01-tbl-0004).

| Years: | 0–4 | 5–9 | 10–14 | 15–19 | 20–24 | 25–29 | 30–34 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| N. units: | 1 | 26 | 26 | 51 | 44 | 14 | 12 |

[Table 1.3](#c01.xhtml_R_c01-tbl-0003) Years to failure and number of failing units for the Equipment‐ `S` Rounded Failure Time Data.

| Variable | Min | Q1 | Q2 | Mean | Q3 | Max |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| X | ![negative 2.34](images/c01-i0146.png) | ![negative 0.08](images/c01-i0147.png) |   0.39 | 0.24 | 0.71 | 2.08 |
| U | ![negative 2.40](images/c01-i0148.png) | ![negative 0.63](images/c01-i0149.png) | ![negative 0.03](images/c01-i0150.png) | ![negative 0.27](images/c01-i0151.png) | 0.26 | 0.75 |
| V |   0.15 |   1.78 |   2.10 |   1.95 | 2.36 | 2.58 |

[Table 1.4](#c01.xhtml_R_c01-tbl-0004) Descriptive statistics for the Quasar Data. Luminosity in log‐scale (![upper X](images/c01-i0144.png)) and observation interval ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0145.png).

The Quasar Data are used in [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml). This classical example is also included in the `DTDA` package (dataset `Quasars`).

### 1.4.5 Parkinson's Disease Data

Clark et al. ([2011](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0005)) investigated the association of candidate single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) and age of onset of Parkinson's disease (PD). For this, genomic DNA samples from human blood samples were obtained from the National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke (NINDS) Human Genetics DNA and Cell Line Repository at the Coriell Institute for Medical Research (Camden, New Jersey). More specifically, one aim of the study was to detect association between the rs8192678 PGC‐1a and A10398G SNPs and the risk or age of onset of PD.

| Group | SNP | Alleles | ![n](images/c01-i0156.png) | Mean (SD) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Early onset | A10398G | A | 76 | 46.93 ![left-parenthesis 5.66 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0157.png) |
|  |  | G | 21 | 47.14 ![left-parenthesis 5.89 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0158.png) |
|  | PGC‐1a | A | 8 | 50.00 ![left-parenthesis 3.89 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0159.png) |
|  |  | AG | 50 | 47.18 ![left-parenthesis 5.67 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0160.png) |
|  |  | G | 39 | 46.10 ![left-parenthesis 5.86 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0161.png) |
| Late onset | A10398G | A | 73 | ![75.12](images/c01-i0162.png) ![left-parenthesis 6.23 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0163.png) |
|  |  | G | 27 | 75.33 ![left-parenthesis 4.92 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0164.png) |
|  | PGC‐1a | A | 10 | 74.00 ![left-parenthesis 4.88 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0165.png) |
|  |  | AG | 37 | 76.49 ![left-parenthesis 6.25 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0166.png) |
|  |  | G | 53 | 74.49 ![left-parenthesis 5.72 right-parenthesis](images/c01-i0167.png) |

[Table 1.5](#c01.xhtml_R_c01-tbl-0005) Parkinson's Disease Data: age of onset for genetic groups. Early onset: 35–55 years; late onset: 63–87 years. Sample size ![n](images/c01-i0155.png) and mean (and standard deviation, SD) for the age of onset (years).

In order to remove the selection bias related to survival, the study was limited to patients diagnosed from PD who had their DNA sample taken within 8 years after onset. Consequently, the age of onset ![upper X](images/c01-i0152.png) is right truncated by the age at blood sampling ![upper V](images/c01-i0153.png), and it is left truncated by ![upper U equals upper V minus 8](images/c01-i0154.png) (age in years). Clark et al. ([2011](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0005)) considered only the right‐truncation issue, but ignored left‐truncation. Both truncation limits were taken into account however in several re‐analyses of the PD data, including Austin et al. ([2014](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0002)) and Mandel et al. ([2018](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0012)).

Clark et al. ([2011](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0005)) divided the cases into an earlier age of onset group (35–55 years) with 99 patients, and a later age of onset group (63–87 years) with 100 patients. The genetic information of these cases, together with basic statistics on the age of onset, is reported in [Table 1.5](#c01.xhtml_c01-tbl-0005). The early onset group have two patients with missing data on A10398G SNP and on age at sampling, and these are excluded from the analysis.

The Parkinson's Disease Data are used in [Chapters 2](#c02.xhtml) and [4](#c04.xhtml). Access to this dataset is possible through the `DTDA` package, which includes the data frames `PDearly` and `PDlate`, for the early and late onset groups, respectively.

### 1.4.6 Acute Coronary Syndrome Data

We also consider data on the age at diagnosis of acute coronary syndrome (ACS). In Portugal, with a population of 10.3 million inhabitants, there are 38 public hospitals with resources for structured care of patients with ACS, out of which 16 have catheterization laboratory facilities. Public hospitals provide treatment for the majority of the acute coronary events and the number of patients submitted to primary percutaneous coronary intervention increased by 37.0% from 2009 to 2013, although at the regional level access to this procedure varied. The EPIHeart study (Araújo et al., [2018](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0001)) is a study assembled between August 2013 and December 2014 by the Cardiology Department of two tertiary hospitals in two regions in Northern Portugal: Hospital de São João, Porto, covering the metropolitan area of Porto in the coast; and Hospital de São Pedro, Vila Real, covering the interior, northeastern region. The inclusion criteria to the cohort were admission with confirmed diagnosis of type 1 (primary spontaneous) ACS, aged 18 years or older, and living in the catchment area of the referred hospitals (Porto, Vila Real, Bragança or Viseu). Data were collected through structured interviews within the first 48 hours after admission. Of the 1297 patients initially considered, 939 were included in the cohort due the inclusion criteria (Moreira et al., [2021b](#c01.xhtml_c01-bib-0015)). Some descriptive statistics can be found in [Table 1.6](#c01.xhtml_c01-tbl-0006).

|  |  | ![n](images/c01-i0173.png) | Mean (SD) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sex | Men | 697 | 62.86 (12.63) |
|  | Women | 242 | 69.83 (12.59) |
| Diagnosis | STEMI | 359 | 61.47 (12.82) |
|  | NSTEMI | 580 | 66.61 (12.69) |

[Table 1.6](#c01.xhtml_R_c01-tbl-0006) Descriptive statistics for the ACS Data. Sample size ![n](images/c01-i0172.png) and mean (and standard deviation, SD) for the age at diagnosis (years). STEMI: ST‐segment elevation myocardial infarction; NSTEMI: non‐ST‐segment elevation myocardial infarction.

The age at diagnosis ![upper X](images/c01-i0168.png), ranging from 29.42 to 94.09 years old, was doubly truncated by (![upper U comma upper V](images/c01-i0169.png)), where ![upper V](images/c01-i0170.png) stands for the elapsed time (in years) between birth and end of the study (December 2014), and ![upper U equals upper V minus 1.42](images/c01-i0171.png).

The ACS Data are used in [Chapters 2](#c02.xhtml) and [3](#c03.xhtml). This dataset can be obtained from `ACS` (complete dataset) or `ACSred` (reduced dataset) in the `DTDA` package.

12.  Mandel M, de Uña‐Álvarez J, Simon DK, Betensky RA 2018 Inverse probability weighted Cox regression for doubly truncated data. Biometrics **74**, 481–487.
13.  Moreira C, de Uña‐Álvarez J 2010 Bootstrapping the NPMLE for doubly truncated data. Journal of Nonparametric Statistics **22**, 567–583.
14.  Moreira C, de Uña‐Álvarez J, Crujeiras RM 2021a *DTDA: Doubly Truncated Data Analysis*. R package version 3.0.
15.  Moreira C, de Uña‐Álvarez J, Santos AC, Barros H 2021b Smoothing methods to estimate the hazard rate under double truncation. *arXiv 2103.14153*.
16.  Shen PS 2010 Nonparametric analysis of doubly truncated data. Annals of the Institute of Statistical Mathematics **62**, 835–853.
17.  Turnbull BW 1976 The empirical distribution function with arbitrarily grouped, censored and truncated data. Journal of the Royal Statistical Society Series B **38**, 290–295.
18.  Wang MC 1991 Nonparametric estimation from cross‐sectional survival data. Journal of the American Statistical Association **86**, 130–143.
19.  Woodroofe M 1985 Estimating a distribution function with truncated data. Annals of Statistics **13**, 163–177.
20.  Xiao J, Hudgens MG 2019 On nonparametric maximum likelihood estimation with double truncation. Biometrika **106**, 989–996.

# 2
One‐Sample Problems

The problem of estimating a cdf from a random sample of ![n](images/c02-i0001.png) individuals taken from a homogeneous population is addressed in this chapter. Under double truncation the sampling information includes the variable of interest ![upper X](images/c02-i0002.png) and the left‐truncation and right‐truncation variables ![upper U](images/c02-i0003.png) and ![upper V](images/c02-i0004.png), but the observable population reduces to those individuals for which ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0005.png). Therefore, we assume that the data at hand ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0006.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0007.png), are a random sample following the conditional distribution of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0008.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0009.png). Of course, ![upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i](images/c02-i0010.png) will be satisfied for ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0011.png).

We consider the sample size ![n](images/c02-i0012.png) as a non‐random quantity. Under an alternative viewpoint, the data result from a two‐step procedure. In the first step, a random sample of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0013.png) with size ![upper N](images/c02-i0014.png), ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0015.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to upper N](images/c02-i0016.png), is obtained from the target population. In the second step, the triplets violating the condition ![upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i](images/c02-i0017.png) are discarded. This entails that the size of the final sample ![n](images/c02-i0018.png) is indeed random (see Woodroofe ([1985](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0034)) for further discussions). We will proceed conditionally on ![n](images/c02-i0019.png), which is the standard approach, ignoring the additional binomial term that would arise from the randomness of ![n](images/c02-i0020.png).

The focus is the estimation of ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0021.png) along the support of the ![upper X](images/c02-i0022.png) variable. In [Section 2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0002) this problem is addressed under a nonparametric viewpoint, while in [Section 2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0033) alternative estimators based on semiparametric and parametric models are considered.

## 2.1 Nonparametric Estimation of a Distribution Function

A naive estimator of ![upper F](images/c02-i0023.png) is the ordinary ecdf of the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0024.png)'s, given by

[(2.1)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0001)![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0001.png) 

However, as discussed in [Chapter 1](#c01.xhtml), this estimator may be systematically biased. In general, the limit of ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0025.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c02-i0026.png) is ![upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x vertical-bar upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0027.png), which may not be equal to the target ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0028.png). The possible bias of ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0029.png) as an estimator of ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0030.png) is nicely illustrated through the weighting function, or biasing function, which reports, for each value of the ![upper X](images/c02-i0031.png) variable, its sampling probability. This biasing function is defined for each ![x](images/c02-i0032.png) point in the support of ![upper X](images/c02-i0033.png) as

[(2.2)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0002)![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V vertical-bar upper X equals x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0002.png) 

Note that the second equality in [(2.2)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0002) holds true if ![upper X](images/c02-i0034.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0035.png) are independent random variables. Throughout the book we assume that such an independence condition is satisfied, unless otherwise is indicated.

When there is no truncation (![upper P left-parenthesis upper U equals negative infinity right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper V equals infinity right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0036.png)), the biasing function ![upper G](images/c02-i0037.png) is constant. This indicates that, once a particular value ![x](images/c02-i0038.png) of the variable ![upper X](images/c02-i0039.png) is drawn from the population, its chances to be included in the final sample are not affected by ![x](images/c02-i0040.png). Under left‐truncation (![upper P left-parenthesis upper V equals infinity right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0041.png)), ![upper G](images/c02-i0042.png) reduces to the cdf of the left‐truncation variable, thus being monotone non‐decreasing. This non‐decreasing shape of the biasing function indicates that larger values of ![upper X](images/c02-i0043.png) have relatively more chances to be observed. The situation under right‐truncation is just the opposite; in this case, ![upper G](images/c02-i0044.png) is one minus the cdf of the right‐truncation variable (that is, the survival function of ![upper V](images/c02-i0045.png)). In the following section we introduce the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0046.png) in the general setting of double truncation. As mentioned, the NPMLE has no explicit form and, in practice, must be computed by iterative algorithms.

Nonparametric estimators of ![upper F](images/c02-i0047.png) alternative to the NPMLE in [Section 2.1.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0003) have been proposed in the related literature. For instance, Austin et al. ([2014](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0001)) proposed a nonparametric estimator under the additional assumption of independence between ![upper U](images/c02-i0048.png) and ![upper V](images/c02-i0049.png). When the truncation limits are independent, [(2.2)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0002) can be simply estimated by a product of two Lynden‐Bell estimators, and an explicit‐form estimator of ![upper F](images/c02-i0050.png) can be constructed. Unfortunately, the assumption of independence between ![upper U](images/c02-i0051.png) and ![upper V](images/c02-i0052.png) is often unrealistic and, in particular, it excludes the important scenario of interval sampling. Another non‐iterative estimator of ![upper F](images/c02-i0053.png) is the one proposed by de Uña‐Álvarez ([2018](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0008)), which estimates ![upper F](images/c02-i0054.png) recursively from left to right, by using the family of Lynden‐Bell estimators computed from the restricted samples ![StartSet i colon upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i Baseline EndSet](images/c02-i0055.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to j less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0056.png). This nonparametric estimator is well adapted to interval sampling; however, it is not efficient. None of these alternative nonparametric procedures is covered by the current manuscript.

### 2.1.1 The NPMLE

Because the target variable ![upper X](images/c02-i0057.png) and the truncation variables ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0058.png) are assumed to be independent, the contribution to the likelihood of a single observation ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0059.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0060.png), is given by

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column script upper L Subscript i Baseline left-parenthesis phi comma psi right-parenthesis 2nd Column equals 3rd Column upper P left-parenthesis upper X equals upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U equals upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V equals upper V Subscript i Baseline vertical-bar upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column equals 3rd Column alpha Superscript negative 1 Baseline d upper F left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis d upper K left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis equals alpha Superscript negative 1 Baseline phi Subscript i Baseline psi Subscript i Baseline comma EndLayout](images/c02-math-0003.png)

where ![alpha equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0061.png) is the non‐truncation probability, ![upper K](images/c02-i0062.png) is the joint cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0063.png), ![phi equals left-parenthesis phi 1 comma ellipsis comma phi Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c02-i0064.png), ![psi equals left-parenthesis psi 1 comma ellipsis comma psi Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c02-i0065.png), and ![phi Subscript i](images/c02-i0066.png) and ![psi Subscript i](images/c02-i0067.png) denote, respectively, the mass of ![upper X](images/c02-i0068.png) at ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0069.png) and that of ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0070.png) at ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0071.png).

At this point, and only for the purpose of deriving the NPMLE, it is assumed that both the target variable and the pair of truncation variables are discrete, with a probability mass concentrated on the observed data. Importantly, the searching of an NPMLE over the class of discrete distributions ![upper F](images/c02-i0072.png) and ![upper K](images/c02-i0073.png) which concentrate their masses respectively on the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0074.png)'s and the ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0075.png)'s is not restrictive. This is stated in the following proposition.

[Proposition 2.1.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0002) allows us to restrict the nonparametric maximization of the likelihood to the class of empirical cdfs that are concentrated on the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0095.png)'s. In turn, this entails that the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0096.png) is zero to the left of the minimum left‐truncation value, ![upper U Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline equals min Underscript 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n Endscripts upper U Subscript i](images/c02-i0097.png), and that it equals one to the right of ![upper V Subscript left-parenthesis n right-parenthesis Baseline equals max Underscript 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n Endscripts upper V Subscript i](images/c02-i0098.png). This is important since, in the absence of restriction ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper U Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline right-parenthesis equals 1 minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper V Subscript left-parenthesis n right-parenthesis Baseline right-parenthesis equals 0](images/c02-i0099.png), a multiplicity of NPMLEs may exist (Xiao and Hudgens, [2019](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0035)). Indeed, another key result on the NPMLE is the one that guarantees its existence and its uniqueness; see the following proposition. The proof, which is omitted, is based on the theorem in the appendix of Vardi ([1985](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0032)), later exploited by Xiao and Hudgens ([2019](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0035)).

As a consequence of [Proposition 2.1.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0006), it is easy to see that, if there exists an index ![i](images/c02-i0107.png) such that ![sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0108.png) or ![sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0109.png), then the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0110.png) does not exist or is not unique. In the following examples we illustrate the application of this result, which is somehow related to the issue of holes in the Lynden‐Bell estimation (Strzalkowska‐Kominiak and Stute, [2010](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0027)). Indeed, when ![sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0111.png), the Lynden‐Bell estimator that ignores the right‐truncation variable admits a hole at ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0112.png) and it becomes degenerate. At this point we mention that the possible non‐existence and non‐uniqueness of the NPMLE may affect simulation studies too; we have found such a situation in a small number of Monte Carlo trials drawn from the simulation scenarios introduced in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016) below. Obviously, the issue may appear when using resampling procedures too. It should be noted that for all datasets considered in this book, except for the ACS Data, the existence and uniqueness condition is satisfied. Some further insight is given in the following examples.

Now note that the non‐truncation probability is given by

[(2.3)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0007)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column alpha equals integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis 2nd Column equals integral left-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis right-parenthesis normal d upper K left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column equals integral integral Underscript u less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to v Endscripts normal d upper K left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis normal d upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndLayout](images/c02-math-0007.png) 

where ![upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than u right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0138.png) is the left‐continuous version of ![upper F](images/c02-i0139.png). When the mass is concentrated on the data, the non‐truncation probability [(2.3)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0007) can also be written as ![alpha equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi Subscript i Baseline upper J Subscript j i Baseline psi Subscript j Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts normal upper Phi Subscript j Baseline psi Subscript j](images/c02-i0140.png), where ![normal upper Phi Subscript j Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi Subscript i Baseline upper J Subscript j i](images/c02-i0141.png), and where ![upper J Subscript j i Baseline equals upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0142.png). Therefore, the full likelihood is

[(2.4)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0008)![script upper L left-parenthesis phi comma psi right-parenthesis equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts alpha Superscript negative 1 Baseline phi Subscript i Baseline psi Subscript i Baseline equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction phi Subscript i Baseline psi Subscript i Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts normal upper Phi Subscript j Baseline psi Subscript j Baseline EndFraction period](images/c02-math-0008.png) 

The likelihood [(2.4)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0008) is self‐adapted to ties in the sense that, if a value ![upper X Subscript i Baseline equals x Subscript k](images/c02-i0143.png) has multiplicity ![n Subscript k](images/c02-i0144.png), the mass attached to ![x Subscript k](images/c02-i0145.png) will be ![n Subscript k Baseline phi Subscript i](images/c02-i0146.png).

The goal is to estimate the ![2 n](images/c02-i0147.png) parameters ![left-brace phi 1 comma period period period comma phi Subscript n Baseline](images/c02-i0148.png)} and ![StartSet psi 1 comma period period period comma psi Subscript n Baseline EndSet](images/c02-i0149.png) by maximizing the full likelihood [(2.4)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0008) under the restrictions ![sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi Subscript i Baseline equals 1](images/c02-i0150.png) and ![sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts psi Subscript i Baseline equals 1](images/c02-i0151.png). At least, and taking into account that the variable of ultimate interest is ![upper X](images/c02-i0152.png), one would like to compute the vector ![phi](images/c02-i0153.png) leading to the maximum, ![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ equals left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript 1 Baseline comma ellipsis comma ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c02-i0154.png) say. To this end, we decompose the full likelihood as a product of the conditional likelihood of the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0155.png)'s given the truncation values, ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0156.png), which just depends on ![phi](images/c02-i0157.png), and the marginal likelihood of the truncation values, ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 2 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi comma psi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0158.png):

[(2.5)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0009)![script upper L left-parenthesis phi comma psi right-parenthesis equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction phi Subscript i Baseline Over normal upper Phi Subscript i Baseline EndFraction times product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction normal upper Phi Subscript i Baseline psi Subscript i Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts normal upper Phi Subscript j Baseline psi Subscript j Baseline EndFraction identical-to script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis times script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 2 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi comma psi right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0009.png) 

An interesting fact is that the conditional NPMLE, that is, the maximizer of the factor ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0159.png) in [(2.5)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0009), equals ![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚](images/c02-i0160.png). This is formalized in the following proposition; see Shen ([2010a](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0024)) for related results.

Efron and Petrosian (1999) proposed two different iterative algorithms to compute the conditional NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0190.png); these are reviewed in [Section 2.1.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0015). On its turn, the couple of vectors ![left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ comma ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0191.png), where ![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ equals left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript 1 Baseline comma ellipsis comma ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c02-i0192.png), maximizing the full likelihood [(2.4)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0008) can be computed by the numerical algorithm proposed by Shen (2010a), see also [Section 2.1.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0015).

Even when the ![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i](images/c02-i0193.png)'s are secondary interest, they can be used in practice to estimate informative curves such as the function that reports the observational bias for ![upper X](images/c02-i0194.png), see [(2.2)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0002). To be explicit, introduce the NPMLE for ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0195.png), given by

[(2.6)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0012)![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0012.png) 

The inspection of ![upper G Subscript n](images/c02-i0196.png) informs on the existing sampling bias for ![upper X](images/c02-i0197.png). Furthermore, there exists an important connection between ![upper G Subscript n](images/c02-i0198.png) and the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0199.png). Certainly, the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0200.png) at ![x](images/c02-i0201.png) is

![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0013.png)

Therefore, ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0202.png) is a non‐drecreasing step function with jump ![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i](images/c02-i0203.png) at ![x equals upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0204.png). An alternative expression for the jump is given by ![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline alpha Subscript n Baseline slash upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0205.png), where ![alpha Subscript n Baseline equals left-parenthesis n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts 1 slash upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c02-i0206.png) is a normalizing constant so ![n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts alpha Subscript n Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline equals 1](images/c02-i0207.png). This shows that the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0208.png) can be obtained as an inverse probability weighted estimator (IPWE) that upweights the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0209.png)'s that have a relatively small sampling probability ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0210.png). The validity of this representation of ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0211.png) is established in the following proposition.

Due to [Proposition 2.1.7](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0018), the NPMLE ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0222.png) satisfies

[(2.7)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0016)![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Baseline integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma alpha Subscript n Baseline equals left-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript b Subscript upper X Baseline Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline comma](images/c02-math-0016.png) 

where ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0223.png) is the ordinary ecdf of the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0224.png)'s, see [(2.1)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0001), and where ![a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0225.png) and ![b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0226.png) are the endpoints of the support of ![upper X](images/c02-i0227.png). This is not surprising, since a similar equation at population level holds. Specifically,

[(2.8)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0017)![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma alpha equals left-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript b Subscript upper X Baseline Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c02-math-0017.png) 

Note also that ![alpha Subscript n](images/c02-i0228.png), as an estimator of the probability of no truncation ![alpha](images/c02-i0229.png), itself deserves attention. Properties of ![alpha Subscript n](images/c02-i0230.png) are discussed in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

The representation of ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0231.png) as an IPWE reveals a general estimation strategy for doubly truncated data that is applicable in more complicated settings, such as regression analysis or multi‐state data. In [Chapters 4](#c04.xhtml) and [5](#c05.xhtml) the details needed for these applications will be fully developed.

Another application of the ![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i](images/c02-i0232.png)'s is found in Survival Analysis with interval sampling, where the study of the incidence process may be of interest. Here, by 'incidence' we refer to the onset of ![upper X](images/c02-i0233.png) which, in the case of interval sampling, is the information reported by the right‐truncation variable ![upper V](images/c02-i0234.png). Recall that, with interval sampling, the observed individuals are those whose events fall between two specific dates ![d 0](images/c02-i0235.png) and ![d 1](images/c02-i0236.png), and that ![upper V equals d 1 minus d Subscript normal upper I](images/c02-i0237.png) in that case, where ![d Subscript normal upper I](images/c02-i0238.png) denotes the date of onset; see [Section 1.3](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0007). Specifically, one may be interested in estimating the cdf of ![upper V](images/c02-i0239.png), given by ![upper K Subscript upper V Baseline left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper V less-than-or-equal-to v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0240.png). A natural estimator is ![ModifyingAbove upper K With caret Subscript upper V Baseline left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper V Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0241.png), which is indeed the NPMLE of ![upper K Subscript upper V Baseline left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0242.png). [Figure 2.13](#c02.xhtml_c02-fig-0013) in [Section 2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0033) depicts this estimator for the Childhood Cancer Data and the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data, revealing that they have different incidence patterns; further illustration is given in [Example 2.1.8](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0022) below. Obviously, with interval sampling the incidence process can be investigated through the cdf of the left‐truncating variable ![upper K Subscript upper U Baseline left-parenthesis u right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to u right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0243.png) too. The resulting estimator ![ModifyingAbove upper K With caret Subscript upper U Baseline left-parenthesis u right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to u right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0244.png) is equal to ![ModifyingAbove upper K With caret Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0245.png) except for a shift with magnitude the width of the sampling interval.

The NPMLEs of ![upper F](images/c02-i0246.png), ![upper G](images/c02-i0247.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0248.png) are now illustrated by using the Parkinson's Disease Data of [Section 1.4.5](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0013).

### 2.1.2 Numerical Algorithms for Computing the NPMLE

In what follows, we review three existing algorithms for the computation of the NPMLE ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0250.png) of the target distribution ![upper F](images/c02-i0251.png): the two algorithms proposed by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)) and the coupled algorithm proposed by Shen ([2010a](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0024)). This last proposal also provides an estimate for ![upper K](images/c02-i0252.png). The algorithms are iterative, so they start with an initial solution for ![upper F](images/c02-i0253.png) and then they proceed by improving it step by step until fulfilling a certain convergence criterion.

As established in [Proposition 2.1.5](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0012), the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0254.png) is given by the maximizer of the conditional likelihood ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0255.png), namely ![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ equals left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript 1 Baseline comma ellipsis comma ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c02-i0256.png). This maximizer satisfies

[(2.9)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0018)![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals left-bracket sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper J Subscript j i Baseline ModifyingAbove normal upper Phi With Ì‚ Subscript j Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-bracket Superscript negative 1 Baseline comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n comma](images/c02-math-0018.png) 

where ![ModifyingAbove normal upper Phi With Ì‚ Subscript j Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript m equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript m Baseline upper J Subscript j m](images/c02-i0257.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to j less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0258.png). This expression is used by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)) to introduce the following iterative algorithm.

The previous algorithm converges slowly, an issue that was already noticed by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)), so the authors introduced a second algorithm based on the Lynden‐Bell ([1971](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0018)) method for left‐truncated data. The Lynden‐Bell method estimates the hazard at time ![upper X Subscript j](images/c02-i0266.png), assuming no ties, by

[(2.10)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0020)![ModifyingAbove lamda With Ì‚ Subscript j Baseline equals left-bracket sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript negative 1 Baseline comma](images/c02-math-0020.png) 

![1 less-than-or-equal-to j less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0267.png), and then it proceeds to evaluate the jumps of the cdf in the usual way:

[(2.11)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0021)![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript j Baseline equals ModifyingAbove lamda With Ì‚ Subscript j Baseline exp left-bracket sigma-summation Underscript i less-than j Endscripts log left-parenthesis 1 minus ModifyingAbove lamda With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to j less-than-or-equal-to n period](images/c02-math-0021.png) 

The application of [(2.10)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0020) to doubly truncated data is not consistent because it ignores the truncation from the right. Actually, as noted by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)), [(2.10)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0020) overestimates the hazard of interest and, consequently, leads to an empirical distribution that can be only considered as an upper bound for the target ![upper F](images/c02-i0268.png). Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)) proposed a way to iteratively improve [(2.10)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0020) under double truncation based on their *Hazard Rate Theorem*, which states that

[(2.12)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0022)![ModifyingAbove lamda With Ì‚ Subscript j Baseline equals left-bracket sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis plus sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper J Subscript i j Baseline ModifyingAbove upper Q With caret Subscript i Baseline right-bracket Superscript negative 1](images/c02-math-0022.png) 

for ![1 less-than-or-equal-to j less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0269.png), where ![ModifyingAbove upper Q With caret Subscript i Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript m equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript m Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript m Baseline greater-than upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis slash ModifyingAbove normal upper Phi With Ì‚ Subscript i](images/c02-i0270.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0271.png). Specifically, the second algorithm proposed by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)) is as follows.

[Equations (2.10)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0020) and [(2.12)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0022) must be updated when ties among the ![upper X Subscript j](images/c02-i0277.png)'s are present. Specifically, the right‐hand side in these equations must be multiplied by the number of events at each ![upper X Subscript j](images/c02-i0278.png) value, that is, by ![sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline equals upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0279.png). Of course, the number of the ![lamda Subscript j](images/c02-i0280.png)'s in this case reduces to the number of distinct ![upper X Subscript j](images/c02-i0281.png)‐values.

Shen ([2010a](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0024)) introduced an algorithm to simultaneously compute the NPMLEs of ![upper F](images/c02-i0282.png) and ![upper K](images/c02-i0283.png). The algorithm is based on a couple of equations that arise from the full likelihood [(2.4)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0008). The first one is

[(2.13)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0023)![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals left-parenthesis sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove normal upper Phi With Ì‚ Subscript j Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline ModifyingAbove normal upper Phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Superscript negative 1 Baseline comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n comma](images/c02-math-0023.png) 

and relates the statement of [Proposition 2.1.5](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0012). The second equation follows from a decomposition of the likelihood analogous to [(2.5)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0009) which is obtained by interchanging the roles of the target and the truncating variables. It holds
