where ![normal upper Psi Subscript j Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript m equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts psi Subscript m Baseline upper J Subscript m j](images/c02-i0284.png). The factors ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis psi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0285.png) and ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 2 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis psi comma phi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0286.png) appearing in [(2.14)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0024) are, respectively, the conditional likelihood of the ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0287.png)'s given the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0288.png)'s, and the marginal likelihood of the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0289.png)'s. The maximizer ![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚](images/c02-i0290.png) of ![script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis psi right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0291.png), satisfies

[(2.15)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0025)![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals left-bracket sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper J Subscript i j Baseline ModifyingAbove normal upper Psi With Ì‚ Subscript j Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-bracket Superscript negative 1 Baseline comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n comma](images/c02-math-0025.png) 

where ![ModifyingAbove normal upper Psi With Ì‚ Subscript j Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript m equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript m Baseline upper J Subscript m j](images/c02-i0292.png). The solutions to [(2.9)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0018) and [(2.15)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0025) are the NPMLEs of ![upper F](images/c02-i0293.png) and ![upper K](images/c02-i0294.png), respectively, and both estimators can be obtained by simultaneously solving [(2.13)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0023) and

[(2.16)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0026)![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals left-parenthesis sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove normal upper Psi With Ì‚ Subscript j Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline ModifyingAbove normal upper Psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Superscript negative 1 Baseline comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n period](images/c02-math-0026.png) 

The resulting algorithm is the following.

Algorithm S1–S4 provides a solution for ![phi](images/c02-i0303.png) equal to that of the first Efron–Petrosian algorithm. On the other hand, the second Efron–Petrosian algorithm is based on a different set of equations and, thus, it is not obvious in principle that it will always provide the same solution for ![phi](images/c02-i0304.png). The aforementioned *Hazard Rate Theorem* of Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)) gives, however, theoretical support for the validity of L1–L3\. Indeed, as already indicated by Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)), L1–L3 is expected to result in a faster convergence when the right truncation is not severe; this is the situation of the Quasar Data of [Section 1.4.4](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0012). For the Quasar Data example, EP1–EP3 (and S1–S4) required 43 iterations, while L1–L3 stopped after only 7 iterations when applied with the same, default convergence criterion of the `DTDA` package.

### 2.1.3 Theoretical Properties of the NPMLE

In this section we describe the theoretical properties of the NPMLE ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0305.png) for doubly truncated data. This includes consistency results, but also a representation of the NPMLE as a sum of iid random variables that allows for an inspection of its sampling distribution. Apart from the results pertaining to ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0306.png) itself, we provide the properties of functionals of the form

[(2.17)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0027)![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi Baseline equals integral phi normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline phi left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Baseline StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction phi left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Over upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction comma](images/c02-math-0027.png) 

where the last equality follows from [Proposition 2.1.7](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0018). Efron–Petrosian integrals as defined in [(2.17)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0027) naturally appear when estimating location and dispersion parameters. For example, ![phi 1 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals x](images/c02-i0307.png) gives an estimator for the mean of ![upper X](images/c02-i0308.png), while by combining ![phi 1](images/c02-i0309.png) and ![phi 2 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals x squared](images/c02-i0310.png) one can estimate the variance of ![upper X](images/c02-i0311.png). The estimator ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0312.png) itself can be recovered from [(2.17)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0027) by considering ![phi Subscript x Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals upper I left-parenthesis t less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0313.png). Convergence of ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi](images/c02-i0314.png) to ![upper S Superscript phi Baseline equals integral phi normal d upper F](images/c02-i0315.png) is of course expected; the available results on this regard will be reviewed. A different version of [(2.17)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0027) appropriate for right‐censored data was proposed and investigated by Stute ([1995](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0029)) and Stute and Wang ([1993](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0030)), while Stute and Wang ([2008](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0031)) considered the setting with one‐sided truncation. The results on ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi](images/c02-i0316.png) in this section somehow extend the latter paper to the doubly truncated setup.

Due to the relevant role of ![upper G](images/c02-i0317.png) in the description of the sampling bias and the correction of double truncation effects, we also cover results for the NPMLE ![upper G Subscript n](images/c02-i0318.png). In particular, an iid representation for the normalized process ![a Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0319.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0320.png), will be given. An interesting application of ![upper G Subscript n](images/c02-i0321.png) is found when one checks if there exists an observational bias due to double truncation. Illustrative examples will be given.

For the moment ![upper F](images/c02-i0322.png) is thought as a continuous cdf with convex support ![left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0323.png), but the theoretical results apply to a wider class of distributions. Specifically, as it will be indicated, these results only require the number of discontinuity points of ![upper F](images/c02-i0324.png) to be finite. Similarly, let ![left-bracket a Subscript upper U Baseline comma b Subscript upper U Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0325.png) and ![left-bracket a Subscript upper V Baseline comma b Subscript upper V Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0326.png) be the supports of ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c02-i0327.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0328.png) respectively. As mentioned in [Chapter 1](#c01.xhtml), under double truncation one can only identify the distribution of ![upper X](images/c02-i0329.png) conditional on ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0330.png). In other words, estimation of ![upper F](images/c02-i0331.png) is only possible when ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0332.png) and ![b Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0333.png). Note that, when ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than a Subscript upper U](images/c02-i0334.png), there is no chance of sampling ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0335.png)‐values within ![left-parenthesis a Subscript upper X Baseline comma a Subscript upper U Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0336.png) and, hence, one cannot recover ![upper F](images/c02-i0337.png) in that interval. An analogous problem occurs when ![b Subscript upper V Baseline less-than b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0338.png).

In practice, the possible violations of condition ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper V Baseline](images/c02-i0339.png) must be taken into account for interpretation purposes. For example, for the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data in [Section 1.4.2](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0010), the identifiable incubation time cdf is conditional on developing AIDS within 99.5 months after HIV infection. Similarly, the age at diagnosis of acute coronary syndrome in [Section 1.4.6](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0014) is only identifiable subject to be within 29.42 and 94.09 years. As another example, the estimation of the cdf for the Equipment‐ `S` data, see [Section 1.4.3](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0011), is conditional on failure during the first 34 years of operation. In the case of the Parkinson' disease age of onset study, [Section 1.4.5](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0013), the definition of the early and late onset groups takes care of this issue, since the age limits are based on the very observed values ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0340.png). The application in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009) is different since the clinical definition of childhood cancer restricts the age at diagnosis ![upper X](images/c02-i0341.png) to the interval ![left-bracket 0 comma 15 right-bracket](images/c02-i0342.png) (years). In this case, one should only verify that ![min Underscript 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n Endscripts upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to 0](images/c02-i0343.png) and ![max Underscript 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n Endscripts upper V Subscript i Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to 15](images/c02-i0344.png) in order to exclude the possibility of any critical restriction in the observation of ![upper X](images/c02-i0345.png); both inequalities hold true. Finally, for the quasar luminosities dataset described in [Section 1.4.4](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0012), the observable distribution is conditioned by ![negative 2.40 less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to 2.58](images/c02-i0346.png). In all this discussion the theoretical limits ![a Subscript upper U](images/c02-i0347.png) and ![b Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0348.png) have been replaced by their empirical counterparts.

Identification of the bivariate cdf ![upper K](images/c02-i0349.png) of ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0350.png) deserves some words too. It is intuitively clear that, for practical purposes, one may replace ![upper K](images/c02-i0351.png) by the conditional distribution of ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0352.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0353.png), ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0354.png) and ![upper V greater-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0355.png). This is only to recognize that, due to the sampling mechanism, nothing can be said on ![upper K left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0356.png) from the data at hand when ![u greater-than v](images/c02-i0357.png), when ![u greater-than b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0358.png), or when ![v less-than a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0359.png). As for the estimation of ![upper F](images/c02-i0360.png), the interpretation of the NPMLE of ![upper K](images/c02-i0361.png) requires some care due to such support limitations. Consider the Childhood Cancer Data in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009) as an illustrative example. Since ![left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket equals left-bracket 0 comma 15 right-bracket](images/c02-i0362.png) and ![upper V equals upper U plus 5](images/c02-i0363.png) (time in years), restrictions ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0364.png) and ![upper V greater-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0365.png) mean that the cdf of ![upper V](images/c02-i0366.png) (that is, the incidence distribution) is only estimable within a 20 year long window before 31 December 2003.

According to the previous discussion, in the following we will assume that ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0367.png) denote the truncation limits for ![upper X](images/c02-i0368.png) conditional on ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0369.png), ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0370.png) and ![upper V greater-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0371.png). This implies in particular ![b Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0372.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0373.png) and ![upper P left-parenthesis upper V minus upper U greater-than-or-equal-to 0 right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0374.png). Let ![upper K Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0375.png) be the cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0376.png), and let ![left-bracket a Subscript upper U 1 Baseline comma b Subscript upper U 1 Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0377.png) and ![left-bracket a Subscript upper V 1 Baseline comma b Subscript upper V 1 Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0378.png) be the supports of ![upper U 1](images/c02-i0379.png) and ![upper V 1](images/c02-i0380.png) respectively. When establishing the asymptotic properties of the NPMLE we will refer to the following assumptions:

1.  (A1) ![upper F](images/c02-i0381.png) has only a finite number of discontinuity points and is continuous everywhere else; ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than a Subscript upper V 1](images/c02-i0382.png), ![b Subscript upper U 1 Baseline less-than b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0383.png); ![b Subscript upper X Baseline minus a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than infinity](images/c02-i0384.png); and ![upper P left-parenthesis upper V 1 minus upper U 1 greater-than-or-equal-to gamma right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0385.png) for some ![gamma greater-than 0](images/c02-i0386.png).
2.  (A2) ![inf Subscript left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis element-of supp left-parenthesis upper K Sub Superscript asterisk Subscript right-parenthesis Baseline left-bracket upper F left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis right-bracket greater-than delta 1 greater-than 0](images/c02-i0387.png).
3.  (A3) ![inf Subscript a Sub Subscript upper X Subscript less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Sub Subscript upper X Baseline upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis greater-than delta 2 greater-than 0](images/c02-i0388.png).
4.  (A4) The marginal densities of ![upper U 1](images/c02-i0389.png) and ![upper V 1](images/c02-i0390.png) are bounded on ![left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0391.png).

Conditions (A1)–(A4) are of technical nature. They can be regarded as strengthened versions of the aforementioned identifiability conditions on the supports of ![upper X](images/c02-i0392.png), ![upper U](images/c02-i0393.png) and ![upper V](images/c02-i0394.png), plus some regularity conditions that allow for specific mathematical tools in the proofs. Actually, conditions (A2) and (A3) are superfluous and can be removed provided that ![a Subscript upper U 1 Baseline less-than a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0395.png) and ![b Subscript upper X Baseline less-than b Subscript upper V 1](images/c02-i0396.png) hold. They inform, however, in a clear way on the boundedness conditions that must be fulfilled by the two relevant sampling probabilities. Note that, while the function ![upper G](images/c02-i0397.png) appearing in (A3) represents the sampling probability for a particular value ![upper X equals x](images/c02-i0398.png) of the target, the difference ![upper F left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0399.png) in (A2) is just the sampling probability for the couple of truncating outcomes ![left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0400.png). Both of them must be bounded away from zero for the theoretical results to hold.

The next result has been proved by de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0010)), who corrected and completed the theory initiated by Shen ([2010a](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0024)). The result holds indeed for any solution ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0401.png) of the equation

[(2.18)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0028)![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline StartFraction normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Over integral Underscript u less-than-or-equal-to t less-than-or-equal-to v Endscripts left-bracket upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper K Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis EndFraction comma a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X Baseline comma](images/c02-math-0028.png) 

where ![upper K Subscript n Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0402.png) is the ordinary ecdf of ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0403.png). That the NPMLE solves [(2.18)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0028) follows from [Proposition 2.1.7](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0018) and equality ![ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals alpha Subscript n Baseline n Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-bracket upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline minus right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript negative 1](images/c02-i0404.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c02-i0405.png), which is left to the reader as an exercise. Introduce ![a left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0406.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0407.png), and let ![script l Superscript infinity Baseline left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0408.png) be the space of bounded functions defined on the support of ![upper X](images/c02-i0409.png), with the norm ![double-vertical-bar h double-vertical-bar Subscript infinity Baseline equals sup Subscript a Sub Subscript upper X Subscript less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Sub Subscript upper X Baseline StartAbsoluteValue h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndAbsoluteValue](images/c02-i0410.png).

From [Theorem 2.1.9](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0024), and with some additional technical work, the following result that states the weak convergence of the process ![StartRoot n EndRoot left-parenthesis upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0415.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0416.png), can be established; see de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0010)) for the details.

[Theorem 2.1.10](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0026) implies that ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0420.png) is asymptotically distributed as a Gaussian random variable. This is interesting, for example, when the aim is the construction of a confidence interval for ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0421.png). An important issue at this point is the estimation of the standard error of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0422.png), a topic that is discussed in the next section. Unfortunately, the linear operator ![script upper A](images/c02-i0423.png) appearing in the iid representation of [Theorem 2.1.9](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0024) has no explicit form; this complicates the design of estimators for the variance. In order to illustrate the properties of consistency and asymptotic normality of ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0424.png) we introduce now two simulated scenarios, which will be also used to investigate the performance of other inferential and exploratory tools throughout this book. Specifically, we introduce a first scenario with no sampling bias for ![upper F](images/c02-i0425.png), and a second one with observational bias.

***Simulated scenario I: no sampling bias for*** ![upper F](images/c02-i0426.png)*. In this scenario, the variable of interest* ![upper X](images/c02-i0427.png) *follows a uniform distribution on the unit interval,* ![upper X tilde upper U left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0428.png)*. The left‐truncation variable* ![upper U](images/c02-i0429.png) *is drawn from a* ![upper U left-parenthesis negative 1 slash 3 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0430.png) *model, while the right‐truncation variable is computed from* ![upper U](images/c02-i0431.png) *as* ![upper V equals upper U plus 1 slash 3](images/c02-i0432.png)*. This represents an interval sampling scenario with interval width of* ![1 slash 3](images/c02-i0433.png)*, and somehow resembles the Childhood Cancer Data in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009). Identifiability support conditions are satisfied since* ![a Subscript upper U Baseline equals negative 1 slash 3 less-than-or-equal-to 0 equals a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to 0 equals a Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0434.png) *and* ![b Subscript upper U Baseline equals 1 less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X Baseline equals 1 less-than-or-equal-to 4 slash 3 equals b Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0435.png)*. Note that in this model there is no observational bias for* ![upper X](images/c02-i0436.png)*; indeed,* ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals 1 slash 4](images/c02-i0437.png) *for* ![0 less-than x less-than 1](images/c02-i0438.png)*, which gives* ![a left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals 1](images/c02-i0439.png) *for all* ![x](images/c02-i0440.png) *points*.

***Simulated scenario II: sampling bias for*** ![upper F](images/c02-i0441.png)*. Sampling bias on* ![upper X](images/c02-i0442.png) *can be easily introduced by considering a non‐uniform distribution for the left‐truncating variable. For instance, consider that the variable of interest* ![upper X](images/c02-i0443.png) *follows a uniform distribution on the unit interval,* ![upper X tilde upper U left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0444.png)*. The left‐truncation variable* ![upper U](images/c02-i0445.png) *satisfies* ![left-bracket left-parenthesis 3 slash 4 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper U plus 1 slash 3 right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline tilde upper U left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0446.png)*, while the right‐truncation variable is computed from* ![upper U](images/c02-i0447.png) *as* ![upper V equals upper U plus 1 slash 3](images/c02-i0448.png)*. In this model the sampling probability for the* ![upper X](images/c02-i0449.png)*‐values is given by* ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals left-parenthesis 3 left-parenthesis x plus 1 slash 3 right-parenthesis slash 4 right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline minus left-parenthesis 3 x slash 4 right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2](images/c02-i0450.png)*,* ![0 less-than x less-than 1](images/c02-i0451.png)*. This function is monotone decreasing, indicating relatively few chances of observation for large* ![upper X](images/c02-i0452.png)*‐values and, thus, introducing an observational bias for the target variable* ![upper X](images/c02-i0453.png).

In general, random double truncation can be easily simulated by implementing a rejection–acceptance step, so the triplets ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0454.png) violating condition ![upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i](images/c02-i0455.png) are discarded. This idea works well in practice, although the computational times in order to reach a pre‐specified sample size ![n](images/c02-i0456.png) may be lengthy when the truncation rate is high. Alternatively, one may directly simulate the truncated world, that is, the triplet ![left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0457.png) according to its joint cdf. In this setting there are at least two possible routes:

1.  (![upper X upper U upper V](images/c02-i0458.png) route) Draw ![upper X 1](images/c02-i0459.png) from ![upper F Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0460.png) first and then draw ![left-parenthesis upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0461.png) from its conditional cdf given ![upper X 1](images/c02-i0462.png).
2.  (![upper U upper V upper X](images/c02-i0463.png) route) Draw ![left-parenthesis upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0464.png) from ![upper K Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0465.png) first and then draw ![upper X 1](images/c02-i0466.png) from its conditional cdf given ![left-parenthesis upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0467.png).

For the ![upper X upper U upper V](images/c02-i0468.png) route one needs the conditional cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0469.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0470.png) and ![upper X equals x](images/c02-i0471.png), which corresponds to

![normal d upper K Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis u comma v vertical-bar x right-parenthesis equals StartFraction upper I left-parenthesis u less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to v right-parenthesis normal d upper K left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis Over upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndFraction period](images/c02-math-0032.png)

For the ![upper U upper V upper X](images/c02-i0472.png) route the conditional cdf of ![upper X](images/c02-i0473.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0474.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis equals left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0475.png) is needed; this is the cdf attached to the measure

![normal d upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar u comma v right-parenthesis equals StartFraction upper I left-parenthesis u less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to v right-parenthesis normal d upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Over upper F left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis EndFraction period](images/c02-math-0033.png)

Depending on the model to be simulated, route (a) may be simpler than (b) or vice versa. For instance, route ![upper X upper U upper V](images/c02-i0476.png) is particularly simple for the simulated scenario I above since ![upper X 1 tilde upper U left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0477.png) (because ![upper G](images/c02-i0478.png) is constant) and, given ![upper X 1 equals x](images/c02-i0479.png), ![upper U 1](images/c02-i0480.png) is uniformly distributed on the interval ![left-parenthesis x minus 1 slash 3 comma x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0481.png). It is an interesting exercise, left to the reader, to prove the latter statement.

The representation for ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0500.png) in [Theorem 2.1.9](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0024) is an extension to double truncation of a well known result in the left‐truncation setup. Specifically, de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0010)) proved that, in the particular case of left‐truncated data, it holds

[(2.19)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0034)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column script upper A left-bracket h Subscript upper X Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper U Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper V Sub Subscript i Subscript Baseline right-bracket left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis 2nd Column equals alpha left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis left-bracket StartFraction upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis Over upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis EndFraction period 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column period right-bracket minus minus times times of II left-parenthesis right-parenthesis less-than-or-equal-to less-than-or-equal-to Uix integral integral logical-or logical-or aXUi logical-and logical-and xXi times times times times times upper G left-parenthesis right-parenthesis t minus minus 1 left-parenthesis right-parenthesis minus minus 1 of FF left-parenthesis right-parenthesis t minus minus 2 d of FF left-parenthesis right-parenthesis t EndLayout](images/c02-math-0034.png) 

where ![upper G](images/c02-i0501.png) becomes here the cdf of ![upper U](images/c02-i0502.png). This is the representation for the Lynden‐Bell estimator in Stute ([1993](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0028)), Theorem 2\. Such result entails that, in the left‐truncated setting, the limit variance of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0503.png) is given by ![sigma Subscript LB Superscript 2 Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis slash n](images/c02-i0504.png), where ![sigma Subscript LB Superscript 2 Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0505.png) denotes the variance of [(2.19)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0034), which is

Right plot shows histogram of values Fn(0.5) obtained from 1000 trials with size n=250 simulated from the same model.](images/c02f004.png)

[Figure 2.4](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-fig-0004) (Simulated scenario I: no sampling bias for ![upper F](images/c02-i0489.png). [Example 2.1.11](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0028)). Left plot: NPMLE ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0490.png) computed from a sample with size ![n equals 25](images/c02-i0491.png) (grey dashed line) or ![n equals 250](images/c02-i0492.png) drawn for model ![upper X tilde upper U left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0493.png), ![upper U tilde upper U left-parenthesis negative 1 slash 3 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0494.png), ![upper V equals upper U plus 1 slash 3](images/c02-i0495.png). The red line is the target ![upper F](images/c02-i0496.png). [(2.20)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0035)![sigma Subscript LB Superscript 2 Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 2 Baseline normal d upper F left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0035.png) 

This is interesting as a particular case since, as mentioned, in general there is no explicit formula for the asymptotic variance of the NPMLE with double truncation.

The following result gives an iid representation for the normalized biasing function ![a Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0506.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0507.png). This function plays a key role in the investigation of the sampling bias and in the reconstruction of the target ![upper F](images/c02-i0508.png) from the doubly truncated data.

The proof of [Theorem 2.1.12](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0030) can be found in de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0010)). In this paper the weak convergence of the process ![StartRoot n EndRoot left-parenthesis a Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus a left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0510.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0511.png), is established too.

In practice, ties in the data may appear due to rounding or to the possible discrete nature of the target variable ![upper X](images/c02-i0554.png). We have mentioned that the likelihood function [(2.4)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0008) is self‐adapted to ties, and that the NPMLE can be computed with no further issues in this case. Of course, similarly as with random sampling, under double truncation rounding effects will result in a less accurate estimator. In the following two examples we illustrate the situation by analysing the Equipment‐S Rounded Failure Time Data of [Section 1.4.3](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0011) and also data simulated from the scenario II.

Back to the estimator ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi](images/c02-i0564.png) introduced in [(2.17)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0027), consistency and distributional convergence results can be derived now from the theorems above. Note that

![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi Baseline equals StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction phi left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Over a Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction](images/c02-math-0038.png)![Graphs depict left plot shows NPMLE for the cdf of the device lifetime (black line) and ordinary ecdf (red line). Right plot shows estimated biasing function.](images/c02f009.png)

[Figure 2.9](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-fig-0009) (Equipment‐S Rounded Failure Time Data, [Example 2.1.15](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0036)). Left plot: NPMLE for the cdf of the device lifetime (black line) and ordinary ecdf (red line). Right plot: estimated biasing function.

![Graphs depict left: NPMLE computed from the original data. Right: NPMLE computed from the rounded data. Dashed line corresponds to the target cdf F. The sample size is n=300.](images/c02f010.png)

[Figure 2.10](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-fig-0010) (Simulated scenario II with rounding, [Example 2.1.16](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0038)). Left: NPMLE computed from the original data. Right: NPMLE computed from the rounded data. Dashed line corresponds to the target cdf ![upper F](images/c02-i0562.png). The sample size is ![n equals 300](images/c02-i0563.png).

and, therefore, representation in [Theorem 2.1.12](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0030) is an essential ingredient for the asymptotic analysis. The following theorem and corollary hold. A detailed proof can be found in de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0010)).

In [Section 2.1.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0003) an estimator for the non‐truncation probability ![alpha equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0570.png) was introduced; see [Eq. (2.7)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0016). The estimator in [Eq. (2.7)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0016), given by ![alpha Subscript n Baseline equals left-parenthesis n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c02-i0571.png), can be used to estimate the size of the population before truncation, ![upper N](images/c02-i0572.png), in an obvious way: ![upper N Subscript n Baseline equals n slash alpha Subscript n](images/c02-i0573.png). The estimator ![alpha Subscript n](images/c02-i0574.png) admits the alternative representation

![alpha Subscript n Baseline equals left-brace n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts left-bracket upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline minus right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-brace Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c02-math-0042.png)

This representation is useful to state the asymptotic properties of ![alpha Subscript n](images/c02-i0575.png), since they are inherited from those of ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0576.png). Indeed, following the steps of the proof to Theorem 2.2 in de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0010)), under the conditions (A1)–(A4) above one gets

[(2.21)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0043)![alpha Subscript n Superscript negative 1 Baseline minus alpha Superscript negative 1 Baseline equals StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts eta overTilde Subscript upper X Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper U Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper V Sub Subscript i Subscript Baseline plus o Subscript upper P Baseline left-parenthesis n Superscript negative 1 slash 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-math-0043.png) 

where

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column eta overTilde Subscript upper X Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper U Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper V Sub Subscript i Subscript 2nd Column equals StartFraction 1 Over upper F left-parenthesis upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline minus right-parenthesis EndFraction minus alpha Superscript negative 1 Baseline 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column minus integral StartFraction script upper A left-bracket h Subscript upper X Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper U Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper V Sub Subscript i Subscript Baseline right-bracket left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus script upper A left-bracket h Subscript upper X Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper U Sub Subscript i Subscript comma upper V Sub Subscript i Subscript Baseline right-bracket left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis Over left-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared EndFraction normal d upper K Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis EndLayout](images/c02-math-0044.png)

are zero‐mean terms. This is similar to the iid representation for the process ![a Subscript n Baseline equals alpha Subscript n Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper G Subscript n](images/c02-i0577.png) in [Theorem 2.1.12](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0030). Representation [(2.21)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0043) ensures in particular the weak consistency of ![alpha Subscript n](images/c02-i0578.png) and the asymptotic normality of ![StartRoot n EndRoot left-parenthesis alpha Subscript n Baseline minus alpha right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0579.png).

The following example is interesting to discuss the identifiability of ![alpha](images/c02-i0580.png), an issue related to that of the identifiability of ![upper F](images/c02-i0581.png) and ![upper K](images/c02-i0582.png). In order to simplify the discussion, assume that ![upper F](images/c02-i0583.png) is identifiable; that is, ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to a Subscript upper X](images/c02-i0584.png) and ![b Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0585.png) hold. Since ![alpha equals 1 slash normal upper E left-bracket upper G left-parenthesis upper X 1 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-i0586.png), the identifiability of ![upper G](images/c02-i0587.png) ensures that ![alpha](images/c02-i0588.png) is identifiable. The following example illustrates how the consistent estimation of ![alpha](images/c02-i0589.png) may be impossible.

### 2.1.4 Standard Errors and Confidence Limits

In the previous section we have seen that the NPMLE ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0656.png) asymptotically follows a Gaussian distribution centered at the target ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0657.png), with standard deviation ![sigma left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0658.png) say. This ![sigma left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0659.png) is the asymptotic standard deviation of ![StartRoot n EndRoot upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0660.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c02-i0661.png). In other words, for large ![n](images/c02-i0662.png) the standard error of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0663.png) approximately equals ![sigma left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis slash StartRoot n EndRoot](images/c02-i0664.png). Unfortunately, there is no simple explicit formula for ![sigma left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0665.png) that may suggest a plug‐in type estimator for the standard error. Thus, alternative procedures are needed.

The bootstrap has been traditionally used for the estimation of standard errors and, more generally, sampling distributions in a variety of settings. For randomly truncated data, two different bootstrap resampling plans have been proposed and investigated: the simple bootstrap (SB), and the obvious bootstrap (OB). While the SB resamples with replacement from ![left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis comma ellipsis comma left-parenthesis upper X Subscript n Baseline comma upper U Subscript n Baseline comma upper V Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0666.png), the OB requires the preliminary estimation of ![upper F](images/c02-i0667.png) and ![upper K](images/c02-i0668.png) in order to proceed. Specifically, the OB obtains the ![upper X](images/c02-i0669.png)‐value from ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0670.png), the ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0671.png) couple is independently drawn from ![upper K Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove psi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to u comma upper V Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0672.png) and, finally, the triplet ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0673.png) is discarded if ![upper X less-than upper U](images/c02-i0674.png) or ![upper X greater-than upper V](images/c02-i0675.png) occurs. This procedure is repeated until a valid triplet satisfying ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c02-i0676.png) is obtained. Therefore, the OB gives positive weights to triplets ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript j Baseline comma upper V Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0677.png) with ![upper U Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript j](images/c02-i0678.png), where (unlike for the SB) ![i not-equals j](images/c02-i0679.png) is possible. For clarity and ease of reference these resampling plans are detailed in the following two definitions.

In the left‐truncated setting the differences between the SB and OB methods have been discussed by Gross and Lai ([1996](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0011)), who also investigated the theoretical validity of the simple bootstrap for left‐truncated and right‐censored data by using Edgeworth expansions. In their setting the application of the OB is not straightforward because it involves either smoothing or an independence condition between the truncating and the residual right‐censoring variables. Without censoring, however, such complications are not present, even when truncation occurs from both (left and right) sides. Theory for the bootstrap with doubly truncated data has not been developed yet.

The previous bootstrap approaches can be used for estimating the standard error of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0696.png) and, subsequently, for computing confidence intervals. Specifically, Emura et al. ([2015](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0006)) and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0009)) analyzed the accuracy of the standard error of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0697.png) based on the SB. The simulation studies in these papers indicate a good behavior of the method, also for sample sizes as small as ![n equals 50](images/c02-i0698.png). Therefore, even when the knowledge on the theoretical properties of the SB in the doubly truncated setting is limited and one should be cautious, we generally recommend it in applications.

In order to illustrate and compare the performance of the two bootstrap methods introduced in [Definitions 2.1.20](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0046) and [2.1.21](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0048), we report some simulation results considering the scenarios I and II described in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016), which correspond to situations without/with sampling bias for ![upper F](images/c02-i0699.png), respectively. We focus first on the estimation of the standard error, by SB and OB, and taking as a benchmark the *true* standard error, approximated from a large number of Monte Carlo replicates. In [Table 2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-tbl-0002) (scenario I) and [Table 2.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-tbl-0003) (scenario II) the average and the standard deviation of the quotients between the bootstrap standard error, ![sigma Subscript normal upper B Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0700.png), and the true one, ![sigma Subscript MC](images/c02-i0727.png), are given. Only results for three deciles (0.3, 0.5 and 0.7) are shown, although similar conclusions can be obtained for other quantiles. Both SB and OB methods perform well; the average of the ratio approaches 1 and the standard deviation of the ratio reduces as the the sample size increases. In [Table 2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-tbl-0002) we also report the value of standard error of the ordinary ecdf (denoted by ![sigma 0](images/c02-i0728.png)), which is a consistent estimator in the scenario without sampling bias, yielding values around one‐third of ![sigma Subscript MC](images/c02-i0729.png). This illustrates the variance increase due to the double truncation.

Standard errors can be seen as the simplest way to gain information about the variability of a given estimator. However, often this variability is better visualized by means of confidence intervals. With doubly truncated data SB and OB methods can be used to this aim, as we will elaborate now. Other alternative routes for producing confidence intervals under double truncation include empirical likelihood ratio (Shen, [2011](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0026)) and explicit formulae (Emura et al., [2015](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0006)).

As a first approach, one may exploit the asymptotic normality of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0730.png) and use the bootstrap resampling algorithms to compute the bootstrap standard error ![sigma Subscript normal upper B Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0731.png) and then plug it in the usual ![100 left-parenthesis 1 minus alpha right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0732.png)% confidence interval formula as follows:

[(2.22)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0045)![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis plus-or-minus z Subscript 1 minus alpha slash 2 Baseline sigma Subscript normal upper B Superscript asterisk Baseline comma](images/c02-math-0045.png) 

where ![sigma Subscript normal upper B Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0733.png) is the standard deviation of the ![upper B](images/c02-i0734.png) bootstrap values ![upper F Subscript n Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0735.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to b less-than-or-equal-to upper B](images/c02-i0736.png), and ![z Subscript q](images/c02-i0737.png) denotes ![q](images/c02-i0738.png)th quantile for a standard normal distribution.

In the censored case, Klein and Moeschberger ([2003](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0016)) refer to the improved methods introduced by Borgan and Liestøl ([1990](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0002)) for computing confidence intervals for the survival functions, based on transformations. Although other alternatives are possible (such as the consideration of an arcsin‐square root transformation), we will consider a simple log‐transform. In this case, the confidence interval is computed from a confidence interval on the log‐cumulative distribution, which after inverse transformation yields

[(2.23)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0046)![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis exp left-parenthesis plus-or-minus z Subscript 1 minus alpha slash 2 Baseline StartFraction sigma Subscript normal upper B Superscript asterisk Baseline Over upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndFraction right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0046.png) 

Both in [(2.22)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0045) and [(2.23)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0046), the bootstrap standard error ![sigma Subscript normal upper B Superscript asterisk](images/c02-i0739.png) is used in the construction of the confidence interval, but other approaches are possible. One of these alternative methods is the percentile bootstrap. The percentile bootstrap considers the collection of ![upper B](images/c02-i0740.png) bootstrap NPMLEs ![upper F Subscript n Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0741.png) and takes the corresponding ![b 1 equals left floor left-parenthesis upper B plus 1 right-parenthesis alpha slash 2 right floor](images/c02-i0742.png) and ![b 2 equals left floor left-parenthesis upper B plus 1 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis 1 minus alpha slash 2 right-parenthesis right floor](images/c02-i0743.png) quantiles; that is, the values of the ![upper F Subscript n Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0744.png) are sorted and the ones in the positions ![b 1](images/c02-i0745.png) and ![b 2](images/c02-i0746.png) are taken as limits of the confidence interval. So, the percentile bootstrap interval for ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0747.png), with a probability coverage of ![1 minus alpha](images/c02-i0748.png), is written as

[(2.24)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0047)![left-parenthesis upper F Subscript n Superscript b 1 Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma upper F Subscript n Superscript b 2 Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0047.png) 

See Davison and Hinkley ([1997](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0003)) for more details on the percentile bootstrap and other methods such as the basic bootstrap, which can be easily applied in the doubly truncated setting.

The behavior of [(2.23)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0046) and [(2.24)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0047) is illustrated in the simulation scenarios I and II in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016), see [Tables 2.4](#c02.xhtml_c02-tbl-0004) and [2.5](#c02.xhtml_c02-tbl-0005). Although other alternatives were checked (such as the normal confidence intervals or the basic bootstrap method), the best results were obtained for these two strategies. It can be seen that both the log‐transformed and the percentile confidence intervals show a very similar behavior: the empirical coverage is quite close to the nominal coverage (which was fixed to 95%), even for ![n equals 100](images/c02-i0761.png), with a considerable reduction in length (average length in parentheses) as the sample size increases. It should be also noticed that the log‐transformed approach, although showing satisfactory results in general, may be problematic for low quantiles and small sample sizes. In these cases, a null value of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0762.png) may appear and, if so, the log‐transform procedure cannot be applied. In Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010a](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0019)) further empirical evidence on the performance of the percentile method based on both SB and OB is provided.

Results for simple bootstrap (SB, top table) and obvious bootstrap (OB, bottom table), for different sample sizes (![n equals 100 comma 500](images/c02-i0756.png)) and quantiles (![0.3 comma 0.5 comma 0.7](images/c02-i0757.png)).
