The previous methods to compute confidence intervals for ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0763.png) enable the construction of pointwise confidence bands for the cdf under double truncation. One may argue that such bands do not guarantee a global coverage of the target curve. As an alternative to bootstrap methods, Emura et al. ([2015](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0006)) propose a closed‐form estimator for the asymptotic covariance function of the NPMLE, enabling the construction of global (equal precision and Kolmogorov–Smirnov type) confidence bands. Through some simulation experiments using bootstrap replicates the authors observe a slightly better performance of the equal precision bands and the expected conservative behavior of the Kolmogorov–Smirnov bands, as it happens for non‐truncated data. Actually, both approaches are inspired in the confidence bands for censored data (see Klein and Moeschberger ([2003](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0016)), [Section 4.4](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0015)), originally proposed by Nair ([1984](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0022)) for the equal prediction bands and Hall and Wellner ([1980](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0012)) for the Kolmogorov‐Smirnov bands.

The estimation of the standard error and the construction of confidence intervals are relevant for other targets too. For example, one may be interested in the estimation of the mean, which can be written as ![mu equals integral phi normal d upper F](images/c02-i0765.png) for the identity function ![phi left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals x](images/c02-i0766.png). [Corollary 2.1.18](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0042) gives conditions under which the empirical mean ![mu Subscript n Baseline equals integral x normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0767.png) is asymptotically Gaussian. In the following example we use the SB strategy in [Definition 2.1.20](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0046) and the normal approximation to provide a confidence interval for the mean lifetime of the Equipment‐S devices presented in [Section 1.4.3](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0011). The percentile bootstrap is also applied to compare. The example includes the estimation of the standard deviation too. Note that the standard deviation involves two integrals of the form ![integral phi normal d upper F](images/c02-i0768.png): the ones corresponding to the specific ![upper X](images/c02-i0769.png)‐transformations ![phi 1 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals x](images/c02-i0770.png) and ![phi 2 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals x squared](images/c02-i0771.png). The target in this case is written as ![sigma equals left-parenthesis integral x squared normal d upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus mu squared right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2](images/c02-i0772.png), and its estimator ![sigma Subscript n](images/c02-i0773.png) is obtained when replacing ![upper F](images/c02-i0774.png) by ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0775.png). The asymptotic normality of ![sigma Subscript n](images/c02-i0776.png) is easily obtained from [Theorem 2.1.17](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0040) and the multivariate central limit theorem.

![Graphs depict pointwise confidence bands at 95% level for the distribution of the age at diagnosis for Parkinson's disease, late onset group.](images/c02f011.png)

[Figure 2.11](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-fig-0011) (Parkinson's Disease Data, [Example 2.1.22](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0050)). Pointwise confidence bands at 95% level for the distribution of the age at diagnosis for Parkinson's disease, late onset group. The simple bootstrap with 199 resamples and the percentile method were used.

## 2.2 Semiparametric and Parametric Approaches

In particular applications with doubly truncated data, external information on the cdf ![upper K](images/c02-i0794.png) of the truncating couple ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0795.png) is available. Under interval sampling, for instance, ![upper K](images/c02-i0796.png) is determined by the rate of the birth process, which may fit a specific known pattern. A prominent example is the stationary case, in which the birth process follows an homogeneous Poisson model. In such a case, both the left and right truncation limits are uniformly distributed on their corresponding support intervals, so ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c02-i0797.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0798.png) are fully specified linear functions. This seems to be the situation of the Childhood Cancer Data in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009): the NPMLE of ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0799.png) for this dataset is depicted in [Figure 2.13](#c02.xhtml_c02-fig-0013), left, and it is very close to the uniform distribution on the interval ![left-bracket min Underscript 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n Endscripts upper X Subscript i Baseline comma max Underscript 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n Endscripts upper X Subscript i Baseline plus 5 right-bracket](images/c02-i0800.png). Indeed, since Portuguese demographics indicate a roughly constant number of alive births over the period 1984–2003, it is reasonable to assume that the birth process for the subpopulation developing cancer before the age of 15 is driven by a constant‐rate Poisson process. Such external information, when available, can be used to improve the estimation of the target ![upper F](images/c02-i0801.png). In other applications the information on the birth process is hardly available. For example, for the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data of [Section 1.4.2](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0010), ![upper V](images/c02-i0802.png) is essentially the infection date, and external information on the date of transfusions involving the infected blood is missing. In this case, the NPMLE of ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0803.png) is non‐uniform, see [Figure 2.13](#c02.xhtml_c02-fig-0013), right, suggesting a non‐homogeneous Poisson model for the infection process. So very different situations may appear in practice.

The semiparametric setting refers to a situation in which ![upper K](images/c02-i0806.png) is known to belong to a parametric family of bivariate cdfs, ![StartSet upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis comma theta element-of normal upper Theta EndSet](images/c02-i0807.png), where the parametric space ![normal upper Theta](images/c02-i0808.png) is a subset of the euclidean space ![script upper R Superscript d](images/c02-i0809.png). When ![normal upper Theta](images/c02-i0810.png) has a single element, one falls within the aforementioned example of a fully specified truncation distribution. More generally, ![normal upper Theta](images/c02-i0811.png) will be a convex set of possible values for the ![d](images/c02-i0812.png)‐dimensional vector ![theta equals left-parenthesis theta 1 comma ellipsis comma theta Subscript d Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c02-i0813.png). In this setting improved estimation of ![upper F](images/c02-i0814.png) is attained by means of the preliminary estimation of ![theta](images/c02-i0815.png); this approach is explored in [Section 2.2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0034). Semiparametric truncation models have been previously considered under one‐sided truncation (Wang, [1989](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0033)), and also recently extended for left‐truncated and right‐censored data (Huang et al., [2015](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0015); Heuchenne et al., [2020](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0013)).

![Graphs depict left plot shows NPMLE of KV for the childhood cancer data in Section 1.4.1\. Right plot shows NPMLE of KV for the AIDS blood transfusion data in Section 1.4.2.](images/c02f013.png)

[Figure 2.13](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-fig-0013) Left plot: NPMLE of ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0804.png) for the Childhood Cancer Data in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009). Right plot: NPMLE of ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i0805.png) for the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data in [Section 1.4.2](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0010).

An alternative semiparametric setting is that in which ![upper F](images/c02-i0816.png) is assumed to follow a certain parametric model, ![StartSet upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma right-parenthesis comma gamma element-of normal upper Gamma EndSet](images/c02-i0817.png) say, while ![upper K](images/c02-i0818.png) remains nonparametric. Since ![upper F](images/c02-i0819.png) is the target, the scenario can be called ‘parametric’ rather than 'semiparametric'. Estimation and inference procedures for this setting is considered in [Section 2.2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0041).

### 2.2.1 Semiparametric Approach

In [Section 2.1.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0003) the NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i0820.png) was represented as an IPWE, where ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0821.png) is upweighted according to its estimated sampling probability ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0822.png); see [Eq. (2.7)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0016). In the semiparametric setting, where ![upper K](images/c02-i0823.png) belongs to the distribution family ![StartSet upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis comma theta element-of normal upper Theta EndSet](images/c02-i0824.png), the sampling probability is written as

![upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta right-parenthesis equals integral Underscript u less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to v Endscripts normal d upper K left-parenthesis u comma v semicolon theta right-parenthesis period](images/c02-math-0048.png)

Hence, a natural estimator for ![upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0825.png) takes the form ![upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0826.png), where ![theta Subscript n](images/c02-i0827.png) is a consistent estimator of ![theta](images/c02-i0828.png). By using [Eq. (2.8)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0017) one comes up with

[(2.25)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0049)![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma](images/c02-math-0049.png) 

where

![alpha Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis equals left-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript b Subscript upper X Baseline Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c02-math-0050.png)

This leads to an IPWE that is different to the NPMLE in that the weight ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0829.png) is replaced by ![upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0830.png). Note that the theoretical counterpart of [(2.25)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0049) is

![upper F left-parenthesis x semicolon theta right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t semicolon theta right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma](images/c02-math-0051.png)

with

![alpha left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis equals left-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript b Subscript upper X Baseline Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t semicolon theta right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c02-math-0052.png)

In order to compute the semiparametric estimator [(2.25)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0049) a consistent estimate for ![theta](images/c02-i0831.png) is needed. Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0020)) and independently Shen ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0025)) proposed estimating ![theta](images/c02-i0832.png) by maximizing the conditional likelihood of the ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0833.png)'s given the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i0834.png)'s, which is

[(2.26)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0053)![script upper L Subscript c Baseline left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction k left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta right-parenthesis Over upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta right-parenthesis EndFraction period](images/c02-math-0053.png) 

When ![upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0835.png) is absolutely continuous, the function ![k left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0836.png) in [(2.26)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0053) denotes the bivariate density of the truncation couple. With interval sampling ![upper V minus upper U](images/c02-i0837.png) is constant, and ![k left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0838.png) becomes the first marginal density of ![upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0839.png) evaluated at ![upper U Subscript i](images/c02-i0840.png) or, equivalently, the second marginal density of ![upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0841.png) evaluated at ![upper V Subscript i](images/c02-i0842.png). When ![theta Subscript n](images/c02-i0843.png) is the maximizer of ![script upper L Subscript c Baseline left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0844.png), it is easy to prove that the couple ![left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline comma upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0845.png) indeed maximizes the full likelihood under the semiparametric model. Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0020)) showed through simulations that the semiparametric maximum likelihood estimator (SPMLE) ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0846.png) outperforms the NPMLE in the sense of the mean square error (MSE). This holds when the true truncation distribution belongs to the parametric family ![StartSet upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis comma theta element-of normal upper Theta EndSet](images/c02-i0847.png). When this parametric model is miss‐specified, the application of the SPMLE may result in a severe bias, so choosing good candidates for ![upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0848.png) is critical. In the setting with interval sampling, Austin et al. ([2014](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0001)) suggested fitting a piecewise exponential model to the truncation distribution. Their decision on the number of pieces is based on an informal, iterative application of the Kolmogorov–Smirnov test, while the location of the pieces is driven by the ordinary empirical quantiles of the truncation variable. Formal goodness‐of‐fit methods to assess the parametric truncation family were proposed and investigated by Moreira et al. ([2014](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0021)), who considered Kolmogorov–Smirnov and Cramér–von Mises distances for three couples of functions: ![left-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline comma upper G left-parenthesis dot comma theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0849.png), ![left-parenthesis upper F Subscript n Baseline comma upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0850.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper K Subscript n Baseline comma upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0851.png). The null distribution of such test statistics was approximated by the semiparametric bootstrap that resamples the target and the truncation variables independently from ![upper F Subscript n](images/c02-i0852.png) and ![upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0853.png). Simple and informative goodness‐of‐fit plots can be constructed by a joint display of the nonparametric and parametric versions of the sampling probability ![upper G](images/c02-i0854.png). In our experience, a Beta model for the truncation limits is flexible enough in applications with interval sampling; this is the model we use in the examples of this section.

Basic asymptotic theory in the semiparametric setting includes the consistency and distributional convergence for the couple of estimators ![left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline comma upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0855.png). These results can be proved by applying standard arguments, which involve the derivation of asymptotic representations for ![theta Subscript n Baseline minus theta 0](images/c02-i0856.png) and ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0857.png) as sums of zero‐mean iid terms plus negligible remainders. Here ![theta 0](images/c02-i0858.png) denotes the true, unknown value of ![theta](images/c02-i0859.png), so ![normal d upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals normal d upper F left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis theta 0 right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0860.png). The usual regularity conditions on the underlying distributions must be imposed. These conditions relate the smoothness of ![log script upper L Subscript c Baseline left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0861.png) and imply the existence and convergence of the solution of the score equation ![partial-differential log script upper L Subscript c Baseline left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis slash partial-differential theta equals 0](images/c02-i0862.png).

Specifically, the required conditions are the following, where we use the notation ![script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis equals log left-parenthesis k left-parenthesis u comma v semicolon theta right-parenthesis slash upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0863.png):

1.  (SP1) The partial derivatives up to order three with respect to ![theta](images/c02-i0864.png) of ![script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0865.png) exist in a neighborhood ![script upper N left-parenthesis theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0866.png) of ![theta 0](images/c02-i0867.png), for almost every ![left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0868.png).
2.  (SP2) There exist integrable functions ![upper H Subscript m Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0869.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to m less-than-or-equal-to 3](images/c02-i0870.png), possibly depending on ![theta 0](images/c02-i0871.png), such that, for ![1 less-than-or-equal-to j comma k comma r less-than-or-equal-to d](images/c02-i0872.png),![StartAbsoluteValue StartFraction partial-differential script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta Subscript j Baseline EndFraction EndAbsoluteValue less-than-or-equal-to upper H 1 left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis comma StartAbsoluteValue StartFraction partial-differential squared script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta Subscript j Baseline partial-differential theta Subscript k Baseline EndFraction EndAbsoluteValue less-than-or-equal-to upper H 2 left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis comma](images/c02-math-0054.png)and![StartAbsoluteValue StartFraction partial-differential cubed script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta Subscript j Baseline partial-differential theta Subscript k Baseline partial-differential theta Subscript r Baseline EndFraction EndAbsoluteValue less-than-or-equal-to upper H 3 left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis comma](images/c02-math-0055.png)hold for ![theta](images/c02-i0873.png) in ![script upper N left-parenthesis theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0874.png) and for almost every ![left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0875.png).
3.  (SP3) For ![1 less-than-or-equal-to j less-than-or-equal-to d](images/c02-i0876.png) it holds![normal upper E Subscript theta 0 Baseline left-bracket left-parenthesis period vertical-bar of partial-differential of script l script l theta left-parenthesis right-parenthesis comma comma upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 of partial-differential theta j Subscript theta equals theta 0 Baseline right-parenthesis squared right-bracket less-than infinity](images/c02-math-0056.png)and, moreover, the matrix![normal upper E Subscript theta 0 Baseline period left-bracket left-parenthesis StartFraction partial-differential script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta EndFraction right-parenthesis left-parenthesis StartFraction partial-differential script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta EndFraction right-parenthesis Superscript t Baseline vertical-bar Subscript theta equals theta 0 Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-math-0057.png)![equals minus normal upper E Subscript theta 0 Baseline period left-bracket StartFraction partial-differential squared script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta squared EndFraction vertical-bar Subscript theta equals theta 0 Baseline right-bracket](images/c02-math-0058.png)is positive definite.

Conditions (SP1)–(SP3) are standard in maximum likelihood theory. The interested reader may check the details corresponding to random sampling in Serfling ([1980](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0023)) or Lehmann and Casella ([1998](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0017)). The adaptation to the setting with doubly truncated data is straightforward. The following result is stated here without giving further details, some of which can be found in Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0020)).

The proof to [Theorem 2.2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0054) in Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0020)) reveals indeed that, as usual, ![script upper I left-parenthesis theta 0 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline minus theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0883.png) is asymptotically equivalent to the centered iid sum ![n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts partial-differential script l Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis slash partial-differential theta vertical-bar Subscript theta equals theta 0 Baseline](images/c02-i0884.png). This, together with the regularity of the function ![upper G left-parenthesis period semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0885.png), ensures the consistency and the asymptotic normality of the SPMLE. Regularity conditions for ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0886.png) for one‐sided truncation were investigated by Wang ([1989](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0033)); similar conditions are needed in the doubly truncated setting, and we will not give further details here. The following result is stated under the vague assumption that the parametric truncation family induces a *regular* ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0887.png). A sketch of the proof is included in Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0020)).

The limit variance of the SMPLE comes from the noise when fitting the parametric truncation model, ![sigma 1 squared left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0894.png), plus the variance corresponding to the estimation of ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0895.png) under the observational bias induced by ![upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0896.png), which is ![sigma 2 squared left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0897.png). Indeed, when the truncation distribution is completely specified, ![sigma squared left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0898.png) equals ![sigma 2 squared left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0899.png), which is the limit variance of ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0900.png), the natural estimator of ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0901.png) in such a case. It is interesting to note that, when ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0902.png) is constant, ![sigma 2 squared left-parenthesis x colon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0903.png) reduces to ![upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0904.png), the limit variance of the ordinary ecdf. This is not surprising since ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0905.png) is precisely the ordinary empirical cdf when ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0906.png) is flat. The limit variance of the SPMLE can be explicitly calculated for simple parametric families. This is done in the following example, which serves to illustrate the efficiency gains with respect to the NPMLE too.

In the particular case of one‐sided truncation, the analytical computation of the asymptotic efficiency of the NPMLE relative to the SPMLE is possible. This is because an explicit formula for the limit variance of the NPMLE is available in the left‐truncated setup; see [(2.20)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0035). We give now an example of such a situation.

| ![beta](images/c02-i0981.png) | ![alpha](images/c02-i0982.png) | 0.25 | 0.5 | 0.75 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 9 | 0.9 | 0.9679 | 0.9600 | 0.9650 |
| 4 | 0.8 | 0.9385 | 0.9184 | 0.9247 |
| 2 | 0.67 | 0.9138 | 0.8712 | 0.8667 |

[Table 2.7](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-tbl-0007) [Example 2.2.4](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0060). Analytical calculations for the asymptotic efficiency of the NPMLE relative to the SPMLE at the quartiles of ![upper X](images/c02-i0978.png). The non‐truncation proportion is ![alpha equals beta slash left-parenthesis beta plus 1 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i0979.png).

For some particular parametric truncation models the maximum likelihood estimator ![theta Subscript n](images/c02-i0983.png) may have an explicit form. The following example, in which the truncation variables are statistically independent, illustrates such a situation.

The estimation of the standard error of the SPMLE is easily done through plug‐in methods. Specifically, the Fisher information matrix ![script upper I left-parenthesis theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1001.png) is replaced by its empirical counterpart,

![script upper I Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis equals minus StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction partial-differential squared Over partial-differential theta squared EndFraction log period vertical-bar of kk left-parenthesis right-parenthesis comma semicolon Ui comma Vi semicolon theta of GG left-parenthesis right-parenthesis semicolon Xi semicolon theta Subscript theta equals theta Sub Subscript n Subscript Baseline comma](images/c02-math-0066.png)

the matrix ![upper W left-parenthesis x comma theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1002.png) is replaced by ![upper W Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1003.png), where

![upper W Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta right-parenthesis equals integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript b Subscript upper X Baseline Baseline StartFraction partial-differential upper G left-parenthesis t semicolon theta right-parenthesis Over partial-differential theta EndFraction StartFraction upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta right-parenthesis minus upper I left-parenthesis t less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis Over upper G left-parenthesis t semicolon theta right-parenthesis EndFraction normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t semicolon theta right-parenthesis comma](images/c02-math-0067.png)

and then an estimator for ![sigma 1 squared left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1004.png) is obtained. One proceeds similarly for ![sigma 2 squared left-parenthesis x semicolon theta 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1005.png), getting an empirical asymptotic variance for ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1006.png), ![sigma Subscript n Superscript 2 Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1007.png) say. Finally, the standard error of the SPMLE is approximated by ![sigma Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis slash StartRoot n EndRoot](images/c02-i1008.png).

In the following example, the semiparametric approach is used to analyse the Parkinson's Disease Data in [Section 1.4.5](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0013). A ![Beta left-parenthesis theta 1 comma theta 2 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1009.png) model is considered as parametric information on ![upper K Subscript upper V](images/c02-i1010.png), after a suitable transformation to map the data onto the ![left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c02-i1011.png) interval. The Beta model is appealing because of its flexibility; besides, it contains the uniform distribution as a special case, so it can be used to test for the stationarity assumption on the birth process, if necessary. Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2010b](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0020)) investigated through intensive simulation studies the practical performance of the SPMLE when based on parametric Beta information, finding relevant improvements compared to the NPMLE.

### 2.2.2 Parametric Approach

Assume now that the target ![upper F](images/c02-i1029.png) is known to fall within a parametric family of cdfs ![StartSet upper F left-parenthesis period semicolon gamma right-parenthesis comma gamma element-of normal upper Gamma EndSet](images/c02-i1030.png), but no information is available for the truncation distribution ![upper K](images/c02-i1031.png). This situation is typically referred to as parametric setting, since the target ![upper F](images/c02-i1032.png) is characterized by a finite‐dimensional parameter ![gamma](images/c02-i1033.png). From the viewpoint of the random vector ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1034.png) the model is semiparametric however; indeed, it is possible to estimate ![upper K](images/c02-i1035.png) in a semiparametric way once the ![gamma](images/c02-i1036.png) parameter has been estimated. Anyway the focus of the present section is on the estimation of, and the inference on, ![gamma](images/c02-i1037.png) and, thus, all the discussion falls under the umbrella of parametric inference. In previous sections we have seen that the NPMLE maximizes the conditional likelihood of the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c02-i1038.png)'s given the ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1039.png)'s; this conditional likelihood is

![Graphs depict left plot shows NPMLE of G (black line) and SPMLE based on the fitted beta model (red line). Right plot shows NPMLE of F (black line) and SPMLE based on the fitted beta model (red line). Early onset group.](images/c02f018.png)

[Figure 2.18](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-fig-0018) (Parkinson's Disease Data, [Example 2.2.6](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0064)). Left plot: NPMLE of ![upper G](images/c02-i1027.png) (black line) and SPMLE based on the fitted Beta model (red line). Right plot: NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c02-i1028.png) (black line) and SPMLE based on the fitted Beta model (red line). Early onset group.

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column script upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis phi right-parenthesis equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction phi Subscript i Baseline Over normal upper Phi Subscript i Baseline EndFraction equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction normal d upper F left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Over upper H left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction comma 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c02-math-0068.png)

where ![upper H left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis equals upper F left-parenthesis v right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1040.png), see [Eq. (2.5)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0009). Under the parametric model one has ![upper F left-parenthesis dot right-parenthesis equals upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1041.png) and the conditional likelihood becomes

[(2.27)](#c02.xhtml_R_c02-disp-0069)![script upper L Subscript c Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis gamma right-parenthesis equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction f left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon gamma right-parenthesis Over upper H left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline semicolon gamma right-parenthesis EndFraction](images/c02-math-0069.png) 

where ![f left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1042.png) is the pdf of ![upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1043.png), and where ![upper H left-parenthesis u comma v semicolon gamma right-parenthesis equals upper F left-parenthesis v semicolon gamma right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus semicolon gamma right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1044.png). Maximization of ![log script upper L Subscript c Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis gamma right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1045.png) with respect to ![gamma](images/c02-i1046.png) leads then to the conditional MLE of this parameter, ![gamma Subscript n](images/c02-i1047.png) say. It is important to note that the marginal likelihood of the truncation values does not provide further information on ![gamma](images/c02-i1048.png). Indeed, under the parametric model the full likelihood is maximized by ![left-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis comma upper K left-parenthesis dot comma dot semicolon gamma Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1049.png), where ![normal d upper K left-parenthesis u comma v semicolon gamma Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis proportional-to left-bracket upper F left-parenthesis v semicolon gamma Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis minus upper F left-parenthesis u minus semicolon gamma Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper K Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis u comma v right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1050.png). Thus, ![gamma Subscript n](images/c02-i1051.png) is not only a conditional MLE, but also an unconditional MLE for ![gamma](images/c02-i1052.png).

Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0005)) considered maximization of [(2.27)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0069) under a special exponential family (SEF) in which the log density of ![upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1053.png) is a normalized cubic function. Emura et al. ([2017](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0007)) carefully developed maximum likelihood theory for the SEF, and generalized the model by allowing an arbitrary polynomial for the log density; see also the computational results in Hu and Emura ([2015](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0014)). In these papers a simplified presentation of the required regularity conditions, as well as a detailed inspection of the parametric space, is achieved by considering the SEF. Dörre et al. ([2021](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0004)) followed the approach in Emura et al. ([2017](#c02.xhtml_c02-bib-0007)) to discuss the asymptotic theory in a location‐scale model for ![log left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1054.png). Such a location‐scale model includes as particular cases three popular families, among others: the Weibull model, the log‐normal model, and the log‐logistic model.

The next result establishes the consistency and distributional convergence of ![gamma Subscript n](images/c02-i1055.png) under general, mild conditions, which should be checked in practice for the particular model at hand. In the following ![gamma 0 element-of normal upper Gamma](images/c02-i1056.png) denotes the true value of the parameter ![gamma](images/c02-i1057.png), that is, ![upper F left-parenthesis dot right-parenthesis equals upper F left-parenthesis dot semicolon gamma 0 right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1058.png). It is assumed that ![normal upper Gamma](images/c02-i1059.png) is a subset of the ![d](images/c02-i1060.png)‐dimensional Euclidean space ![script upper R Superscript d](images/c02-i1061.png). Conditions (SP1)–(SP3) in [Section 2.2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0034) will be assumed here for ![script l Subscript gamma Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u comma v right-parenthesis equals log left-parenthesis f left-parenthesis x semicolon gamma right-parenthesis slash upper H left-parenthesis u comma v semicolon gamma right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c02-i1062.png).

[Theorem 2.2.7](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0066) can be proved similarly to [Theorem 2.2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0054). Note that there exists a duality between both results, in that the roles of the truncating couple and the target variable are interchanged. The next example provides an illustration of the parametric approach discussed in this section.

## 2.3 R Code for the Examples

As is collected in Appendix A, there are some packages in `R` that include functions for analysing doubly truncated data. A brief (up to date) review of the available methods will be provided in the appendix. Throughout this volume, we will make use mainly of library `DTDA`, introducing functions from other libraries (specifically, `DTDA.cif`, `DTDA.ni`, `double.truncation` and `SurvTrunc`) when necessary. We refer to Appendix A (or library help manuals) for further details on functions usage.
