# 3
Smoothing Methods

[Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) has mainly focused on the estimation of the distribution function, considering the NPMLE ([Section 2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0002)) and the semiparametric and parametric variants ([Section 2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0033)) as the reference functions for characterizing the behavior of the target variable ![upper X](images/c03-i0001.png). While the validity of such estimators is unquestionable from a theoretical point of view, the distribution function, being a cumulative probability function, may not reveal in a clear way some data features such as skewness or multimodality. These traits are usually better unveiled by smooth estimates of the density or the hazard function, which can be obtained by kernel methods.

Our aim in this chapter is to describe how kernel estimators for the pdf ([Section 3.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0003)) and the hazard function ([Section 3.6](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0022)) can be constructed in the doubly truncated setting. We also analyze their asymptotic properties and illustrate the performance of the estimators with some examples. Note that, in our particular context, the truncation pattern must be also reflected in the construction of the estimators.

Anyone familiar with kernel density and hazard estimation will be aware of the bandwidth selection issue, a non‐trivial problem that must be also accounted for. In [Section 3.4](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0011), we present some selection bandwidth procedures for kernel density estimation when ![upper X](images/c03-i0002.png) is doubly truncated. Bandwidth selection for the hazard rate will also be discussed.

## 3.1 Some Background in Kernel Estimation

We refer to Silverman ([1986](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0012)) or Wand and Jones ([1995](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0015)) for comprehensive reviews on kernel density estimation, but let us introduce some basic concepts that will be helpful for understanding the distinctive features that arise when ![upper X](images/c03-i0003.png) is doubly truncated. Specifically, in a non‐truncated scenario, consider ![upper X 1 comma ellipsis comma upper X Subscript n Baseline](images/c03-i0004.png) a random sample of ![upper X](images/c03-i0005.png), with unknown pdf ![f](images/c03-i0006.png). Denote by ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk](images/c03-i0007.png) the ecdf obtained from the previous sample. The kernel density estimator is defined as:

[(3.1)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0001)![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis equals integral upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis comma](images/c03-math-0001.png) 

where ![upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals h Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper L left-parenthesis t slash h right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0008.png) is the rescaled kernel (![upper L](images/c03-i0009.png) usually being a symmetric density function), and ![h equals h Subscript n](images/c03-i0010.png) is a deterministic bandwidth sequence such as ![h Subscript n Baseline right-arrow 0](images/c03-i0011.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0012.png). For a fixed ![x](images/c03-i0013.png), it can be seen that

[(3.2)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0002)![normal upper E left-parenthesis f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis plus StartFraction h squared Over 2 EndFraction mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis f Superscript Super Superscript double-prime Superscript Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis plus o left-parenthesis h squared right-parenthesis comma mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis equals integral t squared upper L left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis normal d t comma](images/c03-math-0002.png) 

and, if ![n h right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0014.png), then the variance can be also obtained:

[(3.3)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0003)![Var left-parenthesis f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals StartFraction upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis Over n h EndFraction f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis plus o left-parenthesis left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline right-parenthesis comma upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis equals integral upper L left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis squared normal d t period](images/c03-math-0003.png) 

The mean square error (MSE) at a fixed point ![x](images/c03-i0015.png) is immediately obtained from [(3.2)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0002) and [(3.3)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0003). From the expressions above it is clear that a value of ![h](images/c03-i0016.png) reducing the bias will necessarily enlarge the variance. Hence, the MSE, which sums squared‐bias and variance, seems like an appropriate error measure whose minimization in ![h](images/c03-i0017.png) (actually, the minimization of an approximation of the MSE) could lead to an optimal bandwidth value. However, such a bandwidth will be a local one, in the sense that it minimizes the error for a fixed ![x](images/c03-i0018.png). In order to obtain a global bandwidth, one may consider the integrated version of the MSE, namely the MISE (mean integrated square error), or even the ISE (integrated square error) as a starting point for optimal bandwidth selection.

Although the previous ideas seem reasonable, it should be noted that local and global error measurements to be minimized involve some unknown quantities depending on the density ![f](images/c03-i0019.png), which is the target function. The goal of bandwidth selectors is to provide data‐driven values for ![h](images/c03-i0020.png) that can be used in practice. Bandwidth selectors can be constructed under two perspectives. On the one hand, one may explicitly derive the expression of an optimal bandwidth and then try to approximate the unknown quantities therein; these are the so called plug‐in rules. On the other hand, given an error measure, one may directly approximate the error expression and obtain the bandwidth value where the minimum is attained, such as in cross‐validation or bootstrap procedures.

In what follows, we will adapt the kernel estimator in [(3.1)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0001) for a doubly truncated variable, and we will also introduce some smoothing selectors such as normal reference, plug‐in, cross‐validation and bootstrap bandwidths. Existing literature on kernel estimation for the density and hazard functions with different forms of incomplete or biased data includes Jones ([1991](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0006)), Cai ([1998](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0002)) and Zhou ([1999](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0016)), among many others.

## 3.2 Estimating the Density Function

Recall the expression for the kernel density estimator under a non‐truncated scenario, given in [(3.1)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0001). If the target variable ![upper X](images/c03-i0021.png) is now truncated by the pair ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0022.png), we get a sample ![left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 comma upper V 1 right-parenthesis comma ellipsis comma left-parenthesis upper X Subscript n Baseline comma upper U Subscript n Baseline comma upper V Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0023.png), where ![upper X Subscript i](images/c03-i0024.png) follows the conditional distribution of ![upper X](images/c03-i0025.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c03-i0026.png). It seems obvious that the sampling probabilities must be introduced in the estimator, re‐weighting ![upper X Subscript i](images/c03-i0027.png) according to an estimation of the sampling probability ![upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0028.png), that is, the values with less probability of being observed are receiving more mass. With this purpose, the ecdf ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk](images/c03-i0029.png) in [(3.1)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0001) must be replaced by the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE introduced in [Section 2.1.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0003). Hence, the kernel density estimator is given by

[(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0004)![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Baseline StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0004.png) 

In a semiparametric setting (see [Section 2.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0033)), the semiparametric kernel density estimator is given by

[(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0005)![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis equals integral upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis normal d upper F left-parenthesis t semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0005.png) 

It should be recalled that a constant ![upper G](images/c03-i0030.png) is possible. This happens, for instance, in the simulated scenario I of [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016), where the left‐truncation variable ![upper U](images/c03-i0031.png) is uniformly distributed and ![upper V minus upper U](images/c03-i0032.png) is degenerated. When the function ![upper G Subscript n](images/c03-i0033.png), respectively ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0034.png), is flat, the kernel density estimate [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004), respectively [(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0005), reduces to the ordinary one, given in [(3.1)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0001).

## 3.3 Asymptotic Properties

In order to analyze the asymptotic properties of ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0041.png) and ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0042.png) we introduce first the artificial estimator based on the true ![alpha](images/c03-i0043.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0044.png):

[(3.6)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0006)![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0006.png) 

Here, and throughout the rest of this chapter, we assume that the sampling bias ![upper G](images/c03-i0045.png) is a strictly positive function, so ![upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c03-i0046.png) is well defined. The artificial estimator ![f overbar Subscript h](images/c03-i0047.png) is asymptotically equivalent to ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0048.png), in the sense that ![left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline left-parenthesis f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0049.png) converges to zero in probability under appropriate conditions. Actually, the weak convergence result that is obtained from [Theorem 2.1.12](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0030) guarantees that ![alpha Subscript n Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c03-i0050.png) is ![StartRoot n EndRoot](images/c03-i0051.png)‐consistent, that is, ![sup Underscript x element-of left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket Endscripts StartAbsoluteValue alpha Subscript n Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline minus alpha upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndAbsoluteValue equals upper O Subscript upper P Baseline left-parenthesis n Superscript negative 1 slash 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0052.png), from which

![Graphs depict left plot shows nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the biasing function for the age at diagnosis. Right plot shows nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the cdf of the age at diagnosis.](images/c03f001.png)

[Figure 3.1](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-fig-0001) (Childhood Cancer Data, [Example 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0002)). Left plot: nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the biasing function for the age at diagnosis. Right plot: nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the cdf of the age at diagnosis. The semiparametric estimators were computed assuming a Beta![left-parenthesis theta 1 comma theta 2 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0039.png) model for ![upper U](images/c03-i0040.png).

[(3.7)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0007)![sup Underscript x element-of left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket Endscripts StartAbsoluteValue left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline left-parenthesis f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis EndAbsoluteValue equals upper O Subscript upper P Baseline left-parenthesis StartFraction h Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals upper O Subscript upper P Baseline left-parenthesis h Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline right-parenthesis period](images/c03-math-0007.png) 

In other words, [(3.7)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0007) says that the asymptotic distribution of ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0053.png) as ![h right-arrow 0](images/c03-i0054.png) and ![n h right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0055.png) equals that of ![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0056.png). Thus, heuristically, the asymptotic bias and the variance of ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0057.png) can be calculated from the artificial estimator, which is easily handled due to the non‐randomness of ![alpha](images/c03-i0058.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0059.png). Similar arguments hold for the semiparametric estimator ![f left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0060.png), because ![alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c03-i0061.png) is also ![StartRoot n EndRoot](images/c03-i0062.png)‐consistent. The fact that the two distinct estimators [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004) and [(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0005) may share the same asymptotic distribution could be surprising. This is explained by the nonparametric convergence rate, ![left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 slash 2](images/c03-i0063.png), which holds in kernel estimation, and which is slower than the parametric rate ![n Superscript negative 1 slash 2](images/c03-i0064.png), which corresponds to non‐smooth empiricals like ![upper G Subscript n](images/c03-i0065.png) and ![upper G left-parenthesis period semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0066.png).

Strong consistency and asymptotic normality for ![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0067.png) have been derived by Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2012](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0007)). This is stated as a theorem.

The asymptotic mean and variance of [(3.6)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0006) were provided by Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2012](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0007)) under the following standard regularity assumptions for kernel density estimation:

1.  (D1) The kernel function ![upper L](images/c03-i0082.png) is a density function with ![integral t upper L left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis normal d t equals 0](images/c03-i0083.png), ![mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis less-than infinity](images/c03-i0084.png), and ![upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis less-than infinity](images/c03-i0085.png).
2.  (D2) The sequence of bandwidths ![h equals h Subscript n](images/c03-i0086.png) satisfies ![h right-arrow 0](images/c03-i0087.png) and ![n h right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0088.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0089.png).
3.  (D3) The functions ![f](images/c03-i0090.png) and ![f slash upper G](images/c03-i0091.png) are twice continuously differentiable around ![x](images/c03-i0092.png).

When analysing the results in [Theorem 3.3.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) it can be seen that double truncation does not affect the bias, as expressions [(3.2)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0002) and [(3.8)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0009) are the same. However, truncation does influence variance since a factor ![alpha upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c03-i0094.png) multiplies now the main term in [(3.9)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0010) when compared to [(3.3)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0003). This factor enlarges the variance at those points for which the relative probability of getting ![upper X Subscript i](images/c03-i0095.png)‐values around ![x](images/c03-i0096.png), that is ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0097.png), is small.

As discussed for the ordinary kernel density estimator in [Section 3.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0002), bias and variance expressions in [Theorem 3.3.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006) are useful for deriving local and global error measures in the doubly truncated setup. Specifically, the MSE will be given by

![MSE left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals left-bracket normal upper E left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis minus f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-bracket squared plus upper V a r left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis comma](images/c03-math-0011.png)

and the MISE, as a global error measure, is just given by the integration of ![MSE left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0098.png) over ![x](images/c03-i0099.png). Removing the negligible terms, we get the asymptotic MISE:

[(3.10)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0012)![AMISE left-parenthesis f overbar Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis equals one fourth h Superscript 4 Baseline upper R left-parenthesis f Superscript double-prime Baseline right-parenthesis mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis squared plus left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline alpha upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x period](images/c03-math-0012.png) 

Note that, given the ![StartRoot n h EndRoot](images/c03-i0100.png)‐equivalence between ![upper G Subscript n](images/c03-i0101.png), ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0102.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0103.png), the same AMISE expression holds for ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0104.png) and ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0105.png) under proper conditions. Accordingly to the previous expressions of bias and variance, the difference of [(3.10)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0012) with respect to the ![AMISE](images/c03-i0106.png) in the non‐truncated scenario (Wand and Jones, [1995](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0015)) is found in the second addend, which corresponds to the variance. In this case, it should be noted that, by Hölder's inequality, ![alpha integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x greater-than-or-equal-to 1](images/c03-i0107.png). This fact indicates that the ![AMISE](images/c03-i0108.png) in the doubly truncated situation is at least as large as the one for the non‐truncated setting.

The global error expression in [(3.10)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0012) is also interesting for deriving an optimal bandwidth. By minimizing ![AMISE left-parenthesis f overbar Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0109.png) with respect to ![h](images/c03-i0110.png), we get the asymptotically optimal bandwidth:

[(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0013)![h Subscript AMISE Baseline equals left-bracket StartFraction alpha upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x Over upper R left-parenthesis f Superscript double-prime Baseline right-parenthesis mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis squared EndFraction right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 5 Baseline n Superscript negative 1 slash 5 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0013.png) 

Note that the order of the bandwidth, ![n Superscript negative 1 slash 5](images/c03-i0111.png), is the same as in the non‐truncated case. The optimal bandwidth in [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013) depends on some unknown quantities directly related to ![f](images/c03-i0112.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0113.png). These quantities must be estimated when [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013) is to be used in practice. This is discussed in [Sections 3.4.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0012) and [3.4.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0013).

It is interesting from both a theoretical and an applied viewpoint to investigate the bandwidth which minimizes the MISE, as well as the minimum achieved error. This can be done through simulations, where the unknown target ![f](images/c03-i0114.png) and the nuisance parameters ![alpha](images/c03-i0115.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0116.png) are under control. Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2012](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0007)) conducted intensive simulations to explore the aforementioned issues, and to investigate the performance of the nonparametric and semiparametric kernel density estimators. Among their findings we point out the following: (i) both [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004) and [(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0005) perform satisfactorily, reporting a MISE that decreases as the sample size increases; (ii) the semiparametric estimator is more accurate than the nonparametric estimator, provided that the parametric truncation model is properly chosen; and (iii) the semiparametric estimator requires a bandwidth smaller than that of the nonparametric estimator, according to its smaller finite‐sample variance. Here we further illustrate these points by simulating the scenarios I and II in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

In the simulations the theoretical MISE of ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0117.png) can be approximated by the average of the ISEs along the ![upper M](images/c03-i0118.png) trials,

![ModifyingAbove ISE With bar left-parenthesis f Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis equals StartFraction 1 Over upper M EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript m equals 1 Overscript upper M Endscripts integral left-parenthesis f Subscript h comma left-parenthesis m right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared normal d normal x comma](images/c03-math-0014.png)

where ![f Subscript h comma left-parenthesis m right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0119.png) denotes the kernel density estimator [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004) when computed from the ![m](images/c03-i0120.png)th trial. The ISE of the semiparametric estimator can be computed in an analogous way. For each estimator, the optimal bandwidth that minimizes the MISE, ![h Subscript MISE](images/c03-i0121.png), is approximated by the minimizer of the corresponding Monte Carlo approximation.

Even when the semiparametric estimator [(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0005) can be recommended due to its relatively better performance, it requires the preliminary selection of a parametric model for the truncation variables. As discussed in [Section 2.2.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0034), this is delicate, since an unsuitable truncation model may result in a large systematic bias of the estimator; see Moreira and de Uña‐Álvarez ([2012](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0007)) for further details and illustrations in this regard. In the following two examples we consider the estimation of the pdf for two real datasets. In both examples the Beta model fitted the truncation distribution well.

![Graphs depict Nonparametric (black line), semiparametric (red line) and naive (green line) estimators of the pdf for the incubation time, computed for two different bandwidths: h=0.04 (left plot) and h=0.10 (right plot).](images/c03f003.png)

[Figure 3.3](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-fig-0003) (AIDS Blood Transfusion Data, [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012)). Nonparametric (black line), semiparametric (red line) and naive (green line) estimators of the pdf for the incubation time, computed for two different bandwidths: ![h equals 0.04](images/c03-i0154.png) (left plot) and ![h equals 0.10](images/c03-i0155.png) (right plot).

![Graphs depict left plot shows nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the biasing function for the incubation time. Right plot shows nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the cdf of the incubation time.](images/c03f004.png)

[Figure 3.4](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-fig-0004) (AIDS Blood Transfusion Data, [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012)). Left plot: nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the biasing function for the incubation time. Right plot: nonparametric (black line) and semiparametric (red line) estimators for the cdf of the incubation time.

In the next section, we will introduce some data‐driven bandwidth selection procedures. Some of the proposals will be directly based on [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013), while others consider an approximation of an error measure to be minimized.

## 3.4 Data‐driven Bandwidth Selection

Expressions [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004) and [(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0005) require an appropriate bandwidth value ![h](images/c03-i0156.png) for their effective application in practice. As noted in the previous section, bandwidth selectors can be designed under two perspectives. On the one hand, one may take the expression of an optimal global bandwidth, such as the ![h Subscript AMISE](images/c03-i0157.png) in [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013), and replace the unknown quantities there by sample counterparts. This first group of bandwidth selectors are known as plug‐in rules. We will introduce a normal reference bandwidth, the analogue of the rule‐of‐thumb by Silverman ([1986](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0012)) in the doubly truncated case; and a plug‐in rule following the ideas by Sheather and Jones ([1991](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0011)). On the other hand, one may directly consider an (estimated) error expression and then look for the value of ![h](images/c03-i0158.png) that minimizes such an error criterion. This is the philosophy of other approaches such as the cross‐validation rules and the bootstrap bandwidth. In order to avoid unnecessary repetitions, we will restrict ourselves to the semiparametric estimator ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0159.png). The ideas in the following sections can be adapted in an obvious way for the nonparametric estimator ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0160.png).

### 3.4.1 Normal Reference Bandwidth Selection

In the expression of the optimal bandwidth [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013) two unknown quantities appear: ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0161.png) and ![alpha integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x](images/c03-i0162.png). Regarding ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0163.png), following the idea of Silverman's rule‐of‐thumb, one may assume that ![f](images/c03-i0164.png) follows a Gaussian distribution with a certain variance ![sigma squared](images/c03-i0165.png). Simple calculations using Hermite polynomials yield ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis equals three eighths pi Superscript negative 1 slash 2 Baseline sigma Superscript negative 5](images/c03-i0166.png). In addition, if a Gaussian kernel is used, then one can also compute ![upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0167.png) and ![mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0168.png) and the optimal bandwidth is given by

[(3.12)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0015)![h Subscript AMISE Baseline equals left-bracket four thirds alpha integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 5 Baseline sigma n Superscript negative 1 slash 5 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0015.png) 

The bandwidth in [(3.12)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0015) may lead to oversmoothing if the population is multimodal, as a result of ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0169.png) being large relative to the standard deviation (Silverman, [1986](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0012); Wand and Jones, [1995](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0015)). Better results can be obtained by using a robust measure of variability; for instance, by considering the interquartile range (IQR) of an underlying normal distribution, as a more robust dispersion measure:

![h Subscript AMISE Baseline equals 0.79 IQR n Superscript negative 1 slash 5 Baseline left-bracket alpha integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 5 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0016.png)

In the classical context, Silverman ([1986](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0012)) recommended the use of the smallest value between ![sigma](images/c03-i0170.png) and IQR in the computation of the ![h Subscript AMISE](images/c03-i0171.png) to reduce the risk of oversmoothing. This leads to the following bandwidth parameter:

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column ModifyingAbove h With caret Subscript NR Baseline equals left-bracket four thirds alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 5 Baseline min left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove sigma With Ì‚ comma 0.79 ModifyingAbove IQR With Ì‚ right-parenthesis n Superscript negative 1 slash 5 Baseline comma 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0017.png)

where the unknown ![alpha](images/c03-i0172.png), ![upper G](images/c03-i0173.png) and ![f](images/c03-i0174.png) have been replaced by suitable estimators. Under double truncation, ![sigma squared](images/c03-i0175.png) can be estimated by

[(3.13)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0018)![ModifyingAbove sigma With Ì‚ squared equals alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis integral left-parenthesis x minus upper M left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma](images/c03-math-0018.png) 

with ![upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk](images/c03-i0176.png) the ordinary ecdf of the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c03-i0177.png)'s, see (2.1), and

![upper M left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis integral x upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F Subscript n Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma](images/c03-math-0019.png)

while IQR is replaced by ![ModifyingAbove IQR With Ì‚ equals upper F Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis 0.75 semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis minus upper F Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis 0.25 semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0178.png). This selector provides reasonable estimates if the pdf is smooth enough, otherwise resulting in oversmoothing due to the simplification in the approximation of the *curvature* term, ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0179.png). More sophisticated approximations of this quantity lead to other plug‐in selectors, as the one described in the following section.

### 3.4.2 Plug‐in Bandwidth Selection

The idea for the normal reference bandwidth is to approximate ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0180.png) by assuming that ![f](images/c03-i0181.png) is a normal distribution with variance ![sigma squared](images/c03-i0182.png). However, a more accurate estimation of ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0183.png) can be considered by just recalling that this quantity can be seen as an expectation of the target variable ![upper X](images/c03-i0184.png) transformed by the second derivative of its pdf. The estimation of this expectation can be accomplished by means of kernel estimators. This is the proposal by Sheather and Jones ([1991](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0011)) for obtaining a plug‐in rule in the non‐truncated case, which has been adapted for double truncation by Moreira and Van Keilegom ([2013](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0009)). Complete details on the corrected plug‐in bandwidth are provided in the latter reference. We will present in what follows the basic ideas when using ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0185.png). As mentioned above, a similar route can be followed for ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0186.png).

It can be seen that, under certain smoothness conditions on ![f](images/c03-i0187.png), ![upper R left-parenthesis f Superscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline right-parenthesis equals integral f Superscript left-parenthesis 2 r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x](images/c03-i0188.png) and, hence, the unknown quantity ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0189.png) in [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013) is written as an expected value: ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis equals normal upper E left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0190.png). A semiparametric estimator of this expectation under double truncation is given by

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket 2nd Column equals 3rd Column alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts f Subscript g Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column equals 3rd Column alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis squared n Superscript negative 2 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper W Subscript g Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 EndLayout](images/c03-math-0020.png)

(Sheather and Jones, [1991](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0011); Wand and Jones, [1995](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0015)), where the bandwidth ![g](images/c03-i0191.png) and the kernel ![upper W](images/c03-i0192.png) are possibly different from ![h](images/c03-i0193.png) and ![upper L](images/c03-i0194.png). Moreira and Van Keilegom ([2013](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0009)) obtained the asymptotic bias and variance of this estimator under some regularity assumptions for the kernel ![upper W](images/c03-i0195.png), the density ![f](images/c03-i0196.png) and the function ![f slash upper G](images/c03-i0197.png), leading to an asymptotically optimal value of ![g](images/c03-i0198.png). In particular, when ![upper W](images/c03-i0199.png) is of order ![k](images/c03-i0200.png), the bandwidth ![g](images/c03-i0201.png) which minimizes the asymptotic mean square error (AMSE) of ![ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0202.png) is

[(3.14)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0021)![g Subscript AMSE Baseline equals left-bracket minus StartFraction alpha k factorial upper W Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x Over normal upper E left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 plus k right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket mu Subscript k Baseline left-parenthesis upper W right-parenthesis EndFraction right-bracket Superscript 1 slash left-parenthesis 4 plus k plus 1 right-parenthesis Baseline n Superscript negative 1 slash left-parenthesis 4 plus k plus 1 right-parenthesis Baseline comma](images/c03-math-0021.png) 

where ![mu Subscript k Baseline left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis equals integral t Superscript k Baseline upper L left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis normal d t](images/c03-i0203.png).

The plug‐in bandwidth selector in the doubly truncated case follows the basic ideas of Sheather and Jones ([1991](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0011)). Just recall the expression of the AMISE bandwidth [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013) and replace ![upper R left-parenthesis f double-prime right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0204.png) by the estimator above ![ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0205.png) and ![alpha integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis d x](images/c03-i0206.png) by ![alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0207.png) respectively, to obtain the direct plug‐in (DPI) rule

![ModifyingAbove h With caret Subscript DPI Baseline equals left-bracket StartFraction alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d upper F left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Over ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis squared EndFraction right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 5 Baseline n Superscript negative 1 slash 5 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0022.png)

This selector is ready‐to‐use as soon as ![ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0208.png) is computed, which, as mentioned, requires a pilot bandwidth ![g](images/c03-i0209.png). In this case, one may select ![g](images/c03-i0210.png) by considering [(3.14)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0021). For the sake of simplicity take ![upper W equals upper L](images/c03-i0211.png) and consider that the kernel is of second order, so ![k equals 2](images/c03-i0212.png). Then,

![g Subscript AMSE Baseline equals left-bracket minus StartFraction 2 alpha upper L Superscript left-parenthesis 4 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x Over normal upper E left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 6 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis EndFraction right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 7 Baseline n Superscript negative 1 slash 7 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0023.png)

However, the computation of ![g Subscript AMSE](images/c03-i0213.png) requires an estimate ![ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 6 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0214.png), which itself needs a suitable bandwidth. This formulation leads to an iterative procedure, which can be stopped in a first or a second step (that is, for ![ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 6 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0215.png) or for ![ModifyingAbove normal upper E With Ì‚ left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis 8 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0216.png)) by assuming in that case that ![f](images/c03-i0217.png) follows a normal distribution with variance ![sigma squared](images/c03-i0218.png). These methods will be denoted as ![DPI Subscript 1](images/c03-i0219.png) and ![DPI Subscript 2](images/c03-i0220.png) throughout this chapter. Actually, this approach allows to get a simple estimate of ![normal upper E left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0221.png) for any even integer ![r](images/c03-i0222.png), by using the equality

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column normal upper E left-bracket f Superscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-bracket equals StartFraction left-parenthesis negative 1 right-parenthesis Superscript r slash 2 Baseline r factorial Over left-parenthesis 2 sigma right-parenthesis Superscript r plus 1 Baseline left-parenthesis r slash 2 right-parenthesis factorial pi Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline EndFraction semicolon 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0024.png)

the corresponding estimator is

### 3.4.3 Least‐squares Cross‐validation Bandwidth Selection

As mentioned before, the previous proposals can be viewed as plug‐in rules, given that they consider the expression of the asymptotically optimal bandwidth, ![h Subscript AMISE](images/c03-i0225.png), and replace the unknown quantities by empirical counterparts. Another approach for getting a data‐driven bandwidth is based on the minimization of an approximated error measure. An example of such procedure is cross‐validation bandwidth selection. In order to describe the method, we consider the semiparametric estimator ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0226.png) of the target density ![f](images/c03-i0227.png); similar derivations hold for the nonparametric density estimator [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004).

The integrated square error of [(3.5)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0005) is given by

[(3.15)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0026)![ISE left-bracket f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket equals integral left-parenthesis f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis minus f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared normal d x period](images/c03-math-0026.png) 

Taking expectations in [(3.15)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0026) one gets the MISE:

[(3.16)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0027)![MISE left-bracket f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket equals normal upper E left-bracket integral f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis squared normal d x minus 2 integral f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x plus integral f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis squared normal d x right-bracket period](images/c03-math-0027.png)

[(3.17)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0028)![upper S left-bracket f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket equals normal upper E left-bracket integral f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis squared normal d x minus 2 integral f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x right-bracket period](images/c03-math-0028.png) 

The idea of the cross‐validation estimator is based on minimizing ![upper S left-bracket f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c03-i0230.png), so an appropriate estimator of [(3.17)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0028) is needed. For that purpose, let ![f Subscript h comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0231.png) be the density estimator constructed from all data points except ![upper X Subscript i](images/c03-i0232.png), that is

where ![theta Subscript n comma negative i](images/c03-i0233.png), ![upper F Subscript n comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0234.png) and ![alpha Subscript n comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0235.png) are the leave‐one‐out versions of ![theta Subscript n](images/c03-i0236.png), ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0237.png) and ![alpha Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0238.png); these are the estimators that exclude the ![i](images/c03-i0239.png)th data point when being computed.
