### 3.4.4 Smoothed Bootstrap Bandwidth Selection

Another bandwidth selector based on the minimization of an approximated error is the bootstrap bandwidth. In this procedure the MISE of ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0247.png) is estimated by means of a smoothed bootstrap since, as noticed by Silverman and Young ([1987](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0013)), a non‐smoothed bootstrap may yield inconsistent results. The bootstrap algorithm can be described as follows. For fixed ![upper B](images/c03-i0248.png) and for ![b equals 1 comma ellipsis comma upper B](images/c03-i0249.png):

1.  Let ![upper X Subscript i Superscript b](images/c03-i0250.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c03-i0251.png), be an iid sample from ![f Subscript g Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0252.png), where ![g](images/c03-i0253.png) is chosen according to DP![normal upper I 2](images/c03-i0254.png) criterion (other choices for ![g](images/c03-i0255.png) are possible), and let ![left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Superscript b Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Superscript b Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0256.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c03-i0257.png), be an iid sample from ![upper K Subscript n](images/c03-i0258.png). For each ![i equals 1 comma ellipsis comma n](images/c03-i0259.png), we keep the triplet ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Superscript b Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Superscript b Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Superscript b Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0260.png) in the resample only if the condition ![upper U Subscript i Superscript b Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Superscript b Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i Superscript b](images/c03-i0261.png) is fulfilled. If not, the same resampling procedure is repeated until a triplet for which the inequalities hold is found.
2.  Let ![theta Subscript n Superscript b](images/c03-i0262.png) and ![f Subscript h Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Superscript b Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0263.png) be the estimator of ![theta](images/c03-i0264.png) and of the density ![f](images/c03-i0265.png) respectively, obtained from the bootstrap sample ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Superscript b Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Superscript b Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Superscript b Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0266.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c03-i0267.png).

Note that in the first step above no smoothing is required for the resampling of the truncation values, as is the case for one‐sided truncation (Sánchez‐Sellero et al., [1999](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0010)). Next, let

![BMISE left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis equals upper B Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript b equals 1 Overscript upper B Endscripts integral left-parenthesis f Subscript h Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Superscript b Baseline right-parenthesis minus f Subscript g Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared normal d x comma](images/c03-math-0032.png)

which for ![upper B](images/c03-i0268.png) large will approximate well the bootstrap MISE given by

![MISE Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis equals normal upper E Superscript b Baseline left-bracket integral left-parenthesis f Subscript h Superscript b Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Superscript b Baseline right-parenthesis minus f Subscript g Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared normal d x right-bracket comma](images/c03-math-0033.png)

where ![normal upper E Superscript b](images/c03-i0269.png) denotes the expected value conditionally on the original sample. Now, define

![ModifyingAbove h With caret Subscript boot Baseline equals arg min Underscript h Endscripts BMISE left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis period](images/c03-math-0034.png)

Similar to what is considered for the cross‐validation procedure in [Section 3.4.3](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0014), the optimal bandwidth is obtained here by minimizing an estimator of the (non‐asymptotic) MISE of ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0270.png), whereas the normal reference rule in [Section 3.4.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0012) and the plug‐in bandwidth selection procedures in [Section 3.4.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0013) are based on the minimization of an appropriate estimator of the AMISE. In the following section we illustrate the practical performance of the several selectors for the kernel density bandwidth through the analysis of simulated and real data.

### 3.4.5 Bandwidth Selectors in Practice

Moreira and Van Keilegom ([2013](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0009)) widely investigated through simulations the performance of normal reference rule, plug‐in, least‐squares cross‐validation and smoothed bootstrap bandwidths. They considered sample sizes ranging from ![n equals 50](images/c03-i0271.png) to ![n equals 500](images/c03-i0272.png) and different models with double truncation. The bandwidth values reported by the selectors were generally smaller for the semiparametric estimator ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0273.png) when compared to those of the nonparametric estimator ![f Subscript h](images/c03-i0274.png). This is in agreement with [Table 3.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-tbl-0001). Importantly, the bandwidth selectors were found to perform consistently, approaching the minimizer of the MISE, ![h Subscript MISE](images/c03-i0275.png), for an increasing sample size. Also interestingly, even in situations in which the selector was not close to the optimal smoothing degree, the MISE of the resulting density estimator was only slightly larger than the minimum MISE. Regarding the relative behavior of the selectors, Moreira and Van Keilegom ([2013](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0009)) found an overdispersion for LSCV when compared to the other selectors; for this reason, the application of the LSCV criterion in practice may be risky. Another finding was the oversmoothing of the bootstrap bandwidth when compared to the plug‐in rules. Here we illustrate these issues by launching a set of simulated trials with sizes ![n equals 100](images/c03-i0276.png) and ![n equals 500](images/c03-i0277.png) from the scenarios I and II introduced in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

In [Examples 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012) and [3.4.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0016) we have seen how the naive estimator that ignores the double truncation may be grossly biased. In the following example we investigate how the naive approach may also result in a wrong decision on the optimal smoothing degree required by the corrected estimator [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004). This illustrates how corrections for double truncation are also relevant in bandwidth selection.

From the existing evidences one can conclude that the plug‐in methods DP![normal upper I 1](images/c03-i0362.png) and DP![normal upper I 2](images/c03-i0363.png) perform well in general, while LSCV shows a reasonable behavior but with a variance larger than that of the other methods. The performance of the normal reference bandwidth is also good, particularly when taking its simplicity into account. Finally, the smoothed bootstrap tends to oversmooth the density estimate.

## 3.5 Further Issues in Kernel Density Estimation

From a practical perspective, it is undoubtedly relevant to have bandwidth selection methods at practitioners' disposal, but the probability distribution of the target variable may exhibit more challenging patterns, such as presenting relatively different degrees of smoothing along the observation range. In this situation, global bandwidth selectors, which aim to provide an optimal degree of smoothing on the whole density, may not provide an adequate estimated curve.

When the underlying density requires different degrees of smoothness along the support of ![upper X](images/c03-i0367.png), the kernel density estimator in [(3.4)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0004) can be defined with local bandwidths as follows:

![f Subscript h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral upper L Subscript h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n Baseline StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0035.png)

The asymptotically equivalent version of ![f Subscript h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0368.png) is given by

![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals StartFraction alpha Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c03-math-0036.png)

As pointed out by Wand and Jones ([1995](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0015)) in their Section 2.10.1, the use of a local bandwidth ![h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0369.png) is equivalent to considering a different kernel at each location. An alternative route to local smoothing is given by the nearest‐neighbour bandwidth, when ![h left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0370.png) is defined as the distance from ![x](images/c03-i0371.png) to the ![k](images/c03-i0372.png)th closest sample point. For practical computation, the parameter ![k](images/c03-i0373.png) must be selected. A similar approach may be taken for the semiparametric estimator ![f Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0374.png).

Kernel density estimation has been often used for data exploration. A relevant question here is which observed features are genuine from the underlying distribution, and not just caused by sampling artifacts. Some kind of uncertainty measure attached to the density estimate is needed for answering that sort of question. An issue here is that the bias of the estimator, and the fact that the variance depends on the unknown underlying density ![f](images/c03-i0375.png), hinder the computation of pointwise confidence bands for the pdf.

Despite these difficulties, some methods to quantify the variability in the density estimator are available. Simonoff ([2012](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0014)) suggests the construction of a variability plot based on the bootstrap, by computing the kernel density estimates from the bootstrap resamples along a fixed grid of points. Then, for each value in the grid, the 2.5% and 97.5% bootstrap quantiles are computed to depict a variability envelope. This bootstrap approach is possible with doubly truncated data too. The simple bootstrap and the obvious bootstrap presented in [Section 2.1.4](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0028) are two appropriate resampling algorithms that could be used to this end, similar to what has been done for the cdf ![upper F](images/c03-i0376.png) attached to the density ![f](images/c03-i0377.png).

Another option is to construct a variability band, as proposed by Bowman and Azzalini ([1997](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0001)), which aims to quantify the variability of the density estimate without accounting for the bias. In the doubly truncated setting, the key results on the bias and variance are given in [Theorem 3.3.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0006). Note that the bias depends on ![f double-prime left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0378.png), as in the non‐truncated case, hampering the constructing of confidence intervals. Regarding the variance, as done by Bowman and Azzalini ([1997](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0001)), one may transform ![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0379.png) by a square‐root; from the delta method, the corresponding variance is given by

[(3.18)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0037)![upper V a r left-bracket left-parenthesis StartRoot ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndRoot right-parenthesis right-bracket almost-equals one fourth StartFraction alpha Over n h EndFraction upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis comma](images/c03-math-0037.png) 

which does not depend on ![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0380.png), although it requires the estimation of ![alpha](images/c03-i0381.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0382.png). This can be done nonparametrically from ![upper G Subscript n](images/c03-i0383.png) or, in a semiparametric setting, from ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0384.png). Then, for each fixed value ![x](images/c03-i0385.png), a 95% interval around ![ModifyingAbove f With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0386.png) is obtained in the square‐root scale by adding and substracting ![1.96 one fourth StartFraction alpha Subscript n Baseline Over n h EndFraction upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0387.png) or, in the semiparametric setting, the amount corresponding to ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0388.png). In order to produce the final variability band, this is done for a sequence of values along the support of ![upper X](images/c03-i0389.png) and, then, a transformation back to the original scale is applied. Note that such a tool does not allow us to examine hypotheses about a pdf (for instance, determining the number of modes), but it may be a useful graphical display for quantifying the variability of the estimator. In the non‐truncated setting, variability bands based on [(3.18)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0037) present a constant width along the observation range of ![upper X](images/c03-i0390.png). However, for doubly truncated data, the effect of the sampling bias ![upper G](images/c03-i0391.png) plays a role in the construction of the variability band, with wider areas located at undersampled ![x](images/c03-i0392.png)‐values.

## 3.6 Estimating the Hazard Function

The hazard function, or failure rate function, is often used in Survival Analysis and Reliability to represent the prognosis of a patient or the performance of a device along time. For a continuous variable ![upper X](images/c03-i0395.png) with cdf ![upper F](images/c03-i0396.png) and pdf ![f](images/c03-i0397.png), the hazard function at point ![x](images/c03-i0398.png) is defined as

![lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals limit Underscript white up pointing small triangle x down-arrow 0 Endscripts upper P left-parenthesis x less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than x plus white up pointing triangle x vertical-bar upper X greater-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis equals StartFraction f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x minus right-parenthesis EndFraction period](images/c03-math-0038.png)

The value ![lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0399.png) indicates the instantaneous probability of the event of interest (death, failure) for the individuals or units still alive, or working, by time ![x](images/c03-i0400.png). In our doubly truncated context the hazard function is of course of much interest. In this section we discuss how to estimate ![lamda](images/c03-i0401.png) under double truncation through smoothing methods.

![Graphs depict Kernel density estimator (solid line) based on DPI2 bandwidth, and variability band (dashed lines). Black lines correspond to the nonparametric estimator, while red lines stand for the semiparametric estimator. Left plot shows childhood cancer data. Right plot shows AIDS blood transfusion data.](images/c03f009.png)

[Figure 3.9](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-fig-0009) (Childhood Cancer Data from [Example 3.5.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0020) and AIDS Blood Transfusion Data from [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012)). Kernel density estimator (solid line) based on DP![normal upper I 2](images/c03-i0394.png) bandwidth, and variability band (dashed lines). Black lines correspond to the nonparametric estimator, while red lines stand for the semiparametric estimator. Left plot: Childhood Cancer Data. Right plot: AIDS Blood Transfusion Data.

The kernel estimator for the hazard function is given by

[(3.19)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0039)![lamda Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis d normal upper Lamda Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals integral upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis StartFraction normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Over 1 minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t minus right-parenthesis EndFraction](images/c03-math-0039.png) 

where, as in [(3.1)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0001), ![upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals h Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper L left-parenthesis t slash h right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0402.png) is a rescaled kernel function and ![h equals h Subscript n](images/c03-i0403.png) is a deterministic bandwidth sequence with ![h right-arrow 0](images/c03-i0404.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0405.png). The functions ![upper F Subscript n](images/c03-i0406.png) and ![normal upper Lamda Subscript n](images/c03-i0407.png) appearing in [(3.19)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0039) are the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE of the cdf ![upper F](images/c03-i0408.png) and the corresponding empirical cumulative hazard, which is defined as

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column normal upper Lamda Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline StartFraction normal d upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Over 1 minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t minus right-parenthesis EndFraction equals alpha Subscript n Baseline StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus right-parenthesis EndFraction period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0040.png)

In the non‐truncated scenario, where ![upper G Subscript n](images/c03-i0409.png) is constant and ![upper F Subscript n](images/c03-i0410.png) equals the ordinary ecdf, [(3.19)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0039) reduces to the ordinary kernel estimator for the hazard function (Wand and Jones, [1995](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0015)).

The semiparametric version of [(3.19)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0039), based on a plausible parametric truncation model, is defined as

[(3.20)](#c03.xhtml_R_c03-disp-0041)![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column lamda Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis 2nd Column equals 3rd Column integral upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus t right-parenthesis StartFraction normal d upper F left-parenthesis t semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis t minus semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column equals 3rd Column alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction period EndLayout](images/c03-math-0041.png) 

Similar to the density estimation, [(3.20)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0041) is expected to outperform [(3.19)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0039) whenever the parametric truncation model is chosen in a reasonable way. These two different kernel estimators of the hazard function were proposed and investigated by Moreira et al. ([2021](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0008)).

Following the arguments in [Section 3.3](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0005), the nonparametric and semiparametric hazard smoothers are asymptotically equivalent, and their asymptotic distribution coincides with that of the artificial estimator based on the true, unknown ![alpha](images/c03-i0411.png) and ![upper G](images/c03-i0412.png). This is given by

![ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals alpha StartFraction 1 Over n EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus right-parenthesis EndFraction period](images/c03-math-0042.png)

Under the usual regularity conditions, it holds that ![left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline left-parenthesis lamda Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals o Subscript upper P Baseline left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0413.png) and ![left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript 1 slash 2 Baseline left-parenthesis lamda left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis minus ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals o Subscript upper P Baseline left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0414.png) and, hence, the asymptotic properties of ![lamda Subscript h](images/c03-i0415.png) and ![lamda Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0416.png) can be derived from those of ![lamda overbar Subscript h](images/c03-i0417.png). These are given in the two following results, which were proved by Moreira et al. ([2021](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0008)). For the second result we will refer to the following conditions:

1.  (H1) The kernel function ![upper L](images/c03-i0418.png) is a density function with ![integral t upper L left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis normal d t equals 0](images/c03-i0419.png), ![mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis less-than infinity](images/c03-i0420.png), and ![upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis less-than infinity](images/c03-i0421.png).
2.  (H2) The sequence of bandwidths ![h equals h Subscript n](images/c03-i0422.png) satisfies ![h right-arrow 0](images/c03-i0423.png) and ![n h right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0424.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c03-i0425.png).
3.  (H3) The functions ![lamda](images/c03-i0426.png) and ![lamda slash left-parenthesis left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F right-parenthesis upper G right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0427.png) are twice continuously differentiable around ![x](images/c03-i0428.png).

The formulae in [Theorem 3.6.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0024) indicate that the asymptotic bias of the artificial estimator is not affected by the truncation issue, while its limit variance may be smaller or larger depending on the sampling bias and the particular point ![x](images/c03-i0444.png) at which the hazard is being estimated. In particular, the variance is larger for small values of ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0445.png), according to the corresponding undersampling of the target variable around the point ![x](images/c03-i0446.png). Such an effect vanishes when ![upper G](images/c03-i0447.png) is flat, where the usual expression for the variance is obtained. This fully resembles the situation of the kernel density estimator, previously discussed in [Section 3.3](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0005).

As happens for the density estimator, the practical application of the kernel hazard estimator requires the selection of a bandwidth parameter ![h](images/c03-i0448.png). Following the previous ideas for density estimation, an optimal AMISE bandwidth can be derived. For that purpose, consider the MISE as the global error of ![lamda overbar Subscript h](images/c03-i0449.png); this is given by

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column MISE left-parenthesis lamda overbar Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis equals integral MSE left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis normal d x comma 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0046.png)

where

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column MSE left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals left-brace normal upper E left-bracket ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-bracket minus lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-brace squared plus upper V a r left-parenthesis ModifyingAbove lamda With bar Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0047.png)

Under regularity conditions, the following asymptotic expression for the ![MISE left-parenthesis lamda overbar Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0450.png) is immediately derived from [Theorem 3.6.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0024):

![AMISE left-parenthesis lamda overbar Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis equals one fourth h Superscript 4 Baseline upper R left-parenthesis lamda Superscript double-prime Baseline right-parenthesis mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis squared plus left-parenthesis n h right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline alpha upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis integral StartFraction upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndFraction lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x period](images/c03-math-0048.png)

Here the integral is restricted to an interval strictly contained in the support of ![upper X](images/c03-i0451.png), so it is finite. Interestingly, Hölder's inequality gives ![alpha integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x greater-than-or-equal-to integral left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x](images/c03-i0452.png) and hence, similar to density estimation, double truncation implies a larger overall error. Minimization of ![AMISE left-parenthesis lamda overbar Subscript h Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0453.png) with respect to ![h](images/c03-i0454.png) leads to the asymptotically optimal bandwidth

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column h Subscript AMISE Baseline equals left-bracket StartFraction alpha upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis integral StartFraction upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis EndFraction lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x Over upper R left-parenthesis lamda Superscript double-prime Baseline right-parenthesis mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis squared EndFraction right-bracket Superscript 1 slash 5 Baseline n Superscript negative 1 slash 5 Baseline comma 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0049.png)

which is similar to [(3.11)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0013), except for the integral ![integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x](images/c03-i0455.png) appearing in that equation, which here is replaced by ![integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline lamda left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis normal d x](images/c03-i0456.png).

As it happened for the density case, the expression of ![h Subscript AMISE](images/c03-i0457.png) depends on unknown quantities that must be estimated in practice. Alternatively, the LSCV procedure proposed by Moreira et al. ([2021](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0008)) can be used. With similar derivations to those presented in [Section 3.4.3](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0014), the expression for the ![LSCV left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0458.png) criterion applied to the semiparametric hazard estimator [(3.20)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0041) is given by

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column LSCV left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis 2nd Column equals 3rd Column integral lamda Subscript h Superscript 2 Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis normal d x 2nd Row 1st Column Blank 2nd Column minus 3rd Column 2 n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts alpha Subscript n comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis lamda Subscript h comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F Subscript n comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus semicolon theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction comma EndLayout](images/c03-math-0050.png)

where

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column lamda Subscript h comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis equals alpha Subscript n comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction 1 Over n minus 1 EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript j not-equals i Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x minus upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis StartFraction upper G left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline semicolon theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over 1 minus upper F Subscript n comma negative i Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline minus semicolon theta Subscript n comma negative i Baseline right-parenthesis EndFraction 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0051.png)

is the leave‐one‐out version of ![lamda Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0459.png). The optimal bandwith is obtained by minimizing ![LSCV left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0460.png) over ![h](images/c03-i0461.png):

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column ModifyingAbove h With caret Subscript LSCV Baseline equals arg min Underscript h Endscripts LSCV left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c03-math-0052.png)

Similar to density estimation, it happens that the expectation of ![LSCV left-parenthesis h right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0462.png) is asymptotically equivalent to ![MISE left-parenthesis lamda Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0463.png) (Moreira et al., [2021](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0008)). This validates ![ModifyingAbove h With caret Subscript LSCV](images/c03-i0464.png) as an estimator for the optimal bandwidth. A similar cross‐validation approach can be introduced and justified for the nonparametric hazard estimator [(3.19)](#c03.xhtml_c03-disp-0039).

Moreira et al. ([2021](#c03.xhtml_c03-bib-0008)) thoroughly investigated through simulations the performance of the nonparametric and semiparametric kernel hazard estimators, the conclusions being similar to those already reported for the kernel density estimators ([Section 3.3](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0005)). Here we further illustrate the behavior of the hazard estimators by exploiting the simulated scenarios I and II introduced in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016), and also by analyzing real datasets.

## 3.7 R Code for the Examples

Similar to [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml), we will now show and briefly comment on the code lines that will allow the reader to reproduce the analysis in this chapter. For this purpose, the library `DTDA` will only be used, given that it is the only library (up to now) that includes smoothing tools for density and hazard rate functions.

### 3.7.1 Code for Example 3.2.1

An initial transformation of the data is required. Note that this transformation will be used in all the examples handling the Childhood Cancer Data. Note that this transformation has been used for further analysis.

A parametric estimator of the biasing function can be computed considering a Beta![left-parenthesis theta 1 comma theta 2 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0513.png) model, as follows:

A nonparametric estimation of the biasing function can be obtained, for instance, by computing the NPMLE using function `shen` and keeping the output in `biasf`.

And once we have computed the NPMLE, we can also plot it:

### 3.7.2 Code for Examples 3.3.4 and 3.3.5

The code for both examples is quite similar. Note that the Childhood Cancer Data requires a transformation, as in [Example 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0002) (it is omitted in the code below). We present the code for reproducing a nonparametric, a semiparametric and a naive (that is, uncorrected) kernel density estimators for a specific bandwidth value, ![h equals 0.04](images/c03-i0514.png). For the computation of the kernel density estimators, one may use `densityDT` in `DTDA`, considering semiparametric or naive weights ( `wx` in Code for [Example 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0002), and `wgNV`).

A joint plot can be reproduced as follows, with a suitable scale adaptation for the output.

For the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data in [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012), the following transformation is needed and the code above should be computed again with the new variables.

In order to compute a semiparametric estimator, a Beta![left-parenthesis theta 1 comma theta 2 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0515.png) model (as in [Example 3.3.4](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0010)) has been also considered, but replacing 0.25 by 0.57\. Just recall that a transformation is also required for reproducing the figures.

### 3.7.3 Code for Examples 3.4.2 and 3.4.3

We will present the code for reproducing the figures corresponding to [Example 3.4.2](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0016). The quasar data are also available at `DTDA`. Let's start by computing the NPMLE and exploring the biasing function.

Now, we will obtain the kernel density estimator for the target variable using the rules proposed above: the normal reference, plug‐in, least‐squares cross‐validation and smoothed bootstrap. The different methods are clearly identified in the argument `bw`.

Note that in all cases we have evaluated the estimator in a regular grid in ![left-parenthesis negative 2.4 comma 2.1 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0516.png) with 500 points, except for the smoothed bootstrap method, where a grid of 200 points ins considered. The results can be plotted just running the next code lines.

For the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data in [Example 3.4.3](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0018) the same code can be applied just being aware the transformation done in [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012) both for computations and plotting the results.

### 3.7.4 Code for Example 3.5.1

Variability bands (semiparametric and nonparametric) have been computed for the age at diagnosis in the Childhood Cancer Data and incubation time for the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data. Note that both variables should be transformed as in the code for [Examples 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0002) and [3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012). We will present the code just for the age at diagnosis in the Childhood Cancer Data. First, the quantity ![upper R left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0517.png) involved in the variance expression is numerically approximated.

The semiparametric weights based on the Beta![left-parenthesis theta 1 comma theta 2 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0518.png) model are obtained from `a.est` and `b.est` in the code for [Example 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0002). The parameter ![alpha left-parenthesis theta Subscript 1 comma n Baseline comma theta Subscript 2 comma n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0519.png) is obtained from the semiparametric weights `Gx` in the referred code. For the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data, the 0.25 value in the Beta![left-parenthesis theta 1 comma theta 2 right-parenthesis](images/c03-i0520.png) model should be replaced by 0.57.

The semiparametric and the nonparametric kernel density estimators with DP![normal upper I 2](images/c03-i0521.png) bandwidth and the semiparametric variance in the square‐root scale are computed below. The semiparametric weights `wx` are those in the code for [Example 3.2.1](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0002).

The density plot and the variability band for the semiparametric estimator can be depicted as follows:

The nonparametric band requires the computation of the NPMLE for estimating the biasing function, which must be evaluated at the defined sequence of points (see code below). The corresponding variability band can be obtained just replacing `var.n` in the previous code by `var.n.t` computed in the code below.

### 3.7.5 Code for Example 3.6.4

Data must be transformed as in the Code for [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012). The kernel hazard function estimator is obtained with the least‐squares cross‐validation bandwidth and including semiparametric and naive weights. Note that `wx` and `wgNV` are defined in the Code for [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012). Pointwise confidence bands (nonparametric and semiparametric) were done using ![upper B equals 500](images/c03-i0522.png) bootstrap resamples. Note that `wx` is defined in the code for the [Example 3.3.5](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0012).

The `hazardDT` function from `DTDA` can be used to compute the nonparametric estimator of the hazard function. The naive and the SP estimator are not included so far in `DTDA`, but the code below can be used to reproduce the SP hazard estimator.

### 3.7.6 Code for Example 3.6.5

In this example, the acute coronary syndrome data are considered. Note that just a reduced sample (namely `ACSred` in `DTDA`) is taken, in order to guarantee the existence and uniqueness of the NPMLE. Data must be transformed as is indicated below.

Regarding the semiparametric estimator, an analogous code to the one for [Example 3.6.4](#c03.xhtml_c03-fea-0028) can be used. For reproducing the corresponding plot, ![h](images/c03-i0523.png) must be fixed to 0.019.

## References

2.  Cai Z 1998 Kernel density and hazard rate estimation for censored dependent data. Journal of Multivariate Analysis **67**, 23–34.

5.  Efron B, Petrosian V 1999 Nonparametric methods for doubly truncated data. Journal of the American Statistical Association **94**, 824–834.
6.  Jones MC 1991 Kernel density estimation for length biased data. Biometrika **78**, 511–519.
7.  Moreira C, de Uña‐Álvarez J 2012 Kernel density estimation with doubly truncated data. Electronic Journal of Statistics **6**, 501–521.
8.  Moreira C, de Uña‐Álvarez J, Santos AC, Barros H 2021 Smoothing methods to estimate the hazard rate under double truncation. *arXiv 2103.14153*.

# 4
Regression Analysis

Doubly truncated variables may be also involved in a regression setting. In this chapter we consider the case of a doubly truncated response variable ![upper X](images/c04-i0001.png) and a vector of covariates ![upper Z](images/c04-i0002.png), which may be categorical or numerical, continuous or discrete. First, as a distinct feature from classical regression models, the effect of the random double truncation in the observational bias is analyzed in [Section 4.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0002). Two types of regression models are also presented in detail. On the one hand, [Section 4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0007) is devoted to the Cox proportional hazards model, which is the most popular model in Survival Analysis. On the other hand, the accelerated failure time model, widely used in reliability studies, is considered in [Section 4.3](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0012). The previous models rely on the specification of a parametric form but, as for the density case studied in [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml), smoothing methods can be considered for estimating nonparametrically the regression function too. This is done in [Section 4.4](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0015).

## 4.1 Observational Bias in Regression

We consider a regression scenario in which the response variable ![upper X](images/c04-i0003.png) is observed under random double truncation. As in the previous chapters, we denote by ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0004.png) the pair of truncating variables, so ![upper X](images/c04-i0005.png) is observed only when ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c04-i0006.png). Besides, a vector of covariates ![upper Z](images/c04-i0007.png) is attached to ![upper X](images/c04-i0008.png) and, naturally, it will be available to the researcher only when ![upper X](images/c04-i0009.png) is not truncated. Accordingly, the sampling information is denoted by ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline comma upper V Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0010.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c04-i0011.png); these are iid copies with the conditional distribution of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0012.png) given ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c04-i0013.png).

It is assumed that ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0014.png) is independent of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0015.png). Thus, the probability of sampling a particular value ![z](images/c04-i0016.png) of ![upper Z](images/c04-i0017.png) is given by

[(4.1)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0001)![alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V vertical-bar upper Z equals z right-parenthesis equals integral upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis normal d x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis comma](images/c04-math-0001.png) 

where ![upper F left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0018.png) stands for the cdf of ![upper X](images/c04-i0019.png) given ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0020.png), and where ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0021.png), as defined in [Eq. (2.2)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0002). [Equation (4.1)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0001) states that the sampling probability for ![z](images/c04-i0022.png) is the expected value of ![upper G left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0023.png) conditioned by the event ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0024.png). When ![upper X](images/c04-i0025.png) and ![upper Z](images/c04-i0026.png) are independent, the function ![alpha](images/c04-i0027.png) is constant because ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0028.png) does not depend on ![z](images/c04-i0029.png) and, therefore, there is no sampling bias on the covariates. This is also the case when ![upper G](images/c04-i0030.png) is constant. However, it may happen that some values of the covariate vector will be observed with a larger or a smaller probability. This should be taken into account if one estimates, for example, the marginal distribution of a certain covariate from the observed data. In its turn, the probability of sampling a value ![left-parenthesis x comma z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0031.png) of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0032.png) is just ![upper G left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0033.png) regardless of the particular ![z](images/c04-i0034.png); in other words, the covariate is unimportant for the sampling bias when conditioning on ![upper X](images/c04-i0035.png).

In order to illustrate these issues, let's consider two regression settings with a doubly truncated response ![upper X](images/c04-i0036.png): a regression model with a single discrete covariate (model RD), and a regression model with a single continuous covariate (model RC).

***RD. A regression model with a single discrete covariate**. Let* ![upper Z](images/c04-i0037.png) *be a binary covariate,* ![upper Z tilde upper B left-parenthesis p right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0038.png)*, specifically with* ![p equals 1 slash 2](images/c04-i0039.png)*. Let* ![upper X](images/c04-i0040.png) *be uniformly distributed on the unit interval when* ![upper Z equals 0](images/c04-i0041.png)*, that is,* ![upper F left-parenthesis x vertical-bar 0 right-parenthesis equals x](images/c04-i0042.png) *for* ![0 less-than x less-than 1](images/c04-i0043.png)*. For* ![upper Z equals 1](images/c04-i0044.png) *let the conditional cdf of* ![upper X](images/c04-i0045.png) *be* ![upper F left-parenthesis x vertical-bar 1 right-parenthesis equals 1 minus left-parenthesis 1 minus x right-parenthesis Superscript exp left-parenthesis beta right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0046.png)*,* ![0 less-than x less-than 1](images/c04-i0047.png)*, for some real value* ![beta](images/c04-i0048.png)*. We take* ![beta equals 1](images/c04-i0049.png) *unless otherwise is indicated*.

In [Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0001) the conditional cdfs ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 0 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0050.png) and ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 1 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0051.png) of model RD are depicted. The influence of the covariate ![upper Z](images/c04-i0052.png) on ![upper X](images/c04-i0053.png) is clearly seen: ![upper X](images/c04-i0054.png) tends to take smaller values when ![upper Z equals 1](images/c04-i0055.png) compared to ![upper Z equals 0](images/c04-i0056.png). Together with the true conditional curves, the ordinary ecdfs computed from a single sample of size ![n equals 1000](images/c04-i0057.png) are displayed. This sample was obtained under the truncation scenarios I (left plot) and II (right plot) in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016). Recall that the scenario I induces no observational bias since ![upper G](images/c04-i0058.png) is constant, while ![upper G](images/c04-i0059.png) is monotone decreasing in scenario II, giving more chances of observation to small values of ![upper X](images/c04-i0060.png). The sampling bias is clearly illustrated in [Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0001), right plot, where the ordinary ecdf overestimates both ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 0 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0061.png) and ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 1 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0062.png). In scenario II the proportion of zero ![upper Z](images/c04-i0063.png)‐values in the sample was only 0.44, compared to the true value of 0.5\. This perfectly fits the theoretical probability ![upper P left-parenthesis upper Z equals 0 vertical-bar upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0064.png) that, since ![p equals 1 slash 2](images/c04-i0065.png), equals ![alpha left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis slash left-parenthesis alpha left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis plus alpha left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals 0.44](images/c04-i0066.png).

In order to introduce a regression model with a single continuous covariate, we follow the structure in model RD and we replace the binomial distribution by a uniform distribution for ![upper Z](images/c04-i0072.png).

***RC. A regression model with a single continuous covariate**. Let* ![upper Z](images/c04-i0073.png) *be a continuous covariate, specifically* ![upper Z tilde upper U left-parenthesis 0 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0074.png)*. For* ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0075.png) *let the conditional cdf of* ![upper X](images/c04-i0076.png) *be* ![upper F left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals 1 minus left-parenthesis 1 minus x right-parenthesis Superscript exp left-parenthesis beta z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0077.png)*,* ![0 less-than x less-than 1](images/c04-i0078.png)*, for some real value* ![beta](images/c04-i0079.png)*. We take* ![beta equals 1](images/c04-i0080.png) *unless otherwise indicated*.

In model RC the transformed response ![log left-parenthesis minus log left-parenthesis 1 minus upper X right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0081.png) fits a linear regression model with predictor ![minus beta upper Z](images/c04-i0082.png) and extreme‐value error term independent of the covariate. That is, ![log left-parenthesis minus log left-parenthesis 1 minus upper X right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals minus beta upper Z plus epsilon](images/c04-i0083.png) where ![epsilon](images/c04-i0084.png) is independent of ![upper Z](images/c04-i0085.png) and ![upper P left-parenthesis epsilon greater-than t right-parenthesis equals exp left-parenthesis minus e Superscript t Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0086.png), ![negative infinity less-than t less-than infinity](images/c04-i0087.png). In other words, given ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0088.png), ![minus log left-parenthesis 1 minus upper X right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0089.png) follows an exponential distribution with rate ![exp left-parenthesis beta z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0090.png). In [Figure 4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0002) we depict the true regression line together with the one estimated by maximum likelihood from a single sample of size ![n equals 1000](images/c04-i0091.png) under the truncation scenarios I and II. Maximum likelihood here is applied ignoring the truncation issue. [Figure 4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fig-0002) shows that the naive approach may be biased when the potential sampling bias is not taken into account.

![Graphs depict the conditional cdfs F(·|0) and F(·|1) in model RD (dashed lines), and ordinary ecdf (solid lines) based on a single sample of size n=1000 from the truncation scenarios I (left plot) and II (right plot) in Section 2.1.3.](images/c04f001.png)

[Figure 4.1](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-fig-0001) Conditional cdfs ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 0 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0067.png) and ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 1 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0068.png) in model RD (dashed lines), and ordinary ecdf (solid lines) based on a single sample of size ![n equals 1000](images/c04-i0069.png) from the truncation scenarios I (left plot) and II (right plot) in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

![Graphs depict the regression line for the transformed response log(-log(1-X)) in model RC (dashed line), and ordinary maximum-likelihood estimation of the regression line (continuous red line) based on a single sample of size n=1000 from the truncation scenarios I (left plot) and II (right plot) in Section 2.1.3.](images/c04f002.png)

[Figure 4.2](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-fig-0002) Regression line for the transformed response ![log left-parenthesis minus log left-parenthesis 1 minus upper X right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0070.png) in model RC (dashed line), and ordinary maximum likelihood estimation of the regression line (continuous red line) based on a single sample of size ![n equals 1000](images/c04-i0071.png) from the truncation scenarios I (left plot) and II (right plot) in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

In model RC, the sampling bias for ![upper Z](images/c04-i0092.png) under the truncation scenario II is small. This is because the function ![alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0093.png) in [(4.1)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0001) is roughly constant along ![z element-of left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c04-i0094.png), ranging between ![alpha left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis equals 0.20](images/c04-i0095.png) and ![alpha left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis equals 0.25](images/c04-i0096.png). Therefore, not much difference will be found when comparing the ordinary ecdf of ![upper Z Subscript i](images/c04-i0097.png) and the true, uniform distribution.

Due to the potential biases that can arise from random double truncation, proper corrections of the ordinary estimators are needed. Similar to the case in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml), unbiased estimators can be constructed by downweighting the multivariate observations ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0098.png) with relatively large values of ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0099.png). For example, when ![upper Z](images/c04-i0100.png) is discrete, the conditional cdf ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0101.png) at point ![x](images/c04-i0102.png) is consistently estimated by

[(4.2)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0002)![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline equals z right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper Z Subscript i Baseline equals z right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction period](images/c04-math-0002.png) 

The numerator in ![left-parenthesis 4.2 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0103.png), when normalized through the sum of the weights ![sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0104.png), can be regarded as an estimator for the so‐called subdistribution function ![upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x comma upper Z equals z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0105.png). When ![upper Z](images/c04-i0106.png) is an event type indicator, such normalized quantity estimates a cumulative incidence function, as investigated by de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020a](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0002)). This is further explained in [Chapter 5](#c05.xhtml).

The estimator [(4.2)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0002) fully exploits the independence between ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0107.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0108.png), by using the same sequence of weights for each ![upper Z](images/c04-i0109.png)‐value. Under the weaker assumption of conditional independence between ![upper X](images/c04-i0110.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0111.png) given ![upper Z](images/c04-i0112.png) the target ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0113.png) can be still consistently estimated through the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE (introduced in [Section 2.1.2](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0015)) computed from the subsample ![StartSet i colon upper Z Subscript i Baseline equals z EndSet](images/c04-i0114.png), at the price of suffering from a larger variance. Obviously, such a conditional NPMLE can be regarded as an IPWE that makes use of some conditional weights ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0115.png). In practice, the consistency of the unconditional weights as appearing in [(4.2)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0002) can be graphically assessed by plotting the curves ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0116.png) for the several ![z](images/c04-i0117.png)‐values to see if no systematic deviation among them occurs.

For a general covariate vector ![upper Z](images/c04-i0119.png), possibly having continuous and discrete components, a general estimation strategy was investigated by de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0004)). Specifically, the estimation of the general functional ![upper S Superscript phi Baseline equals integral phi normal d upper F Subscript upper X upper Z Baseline](images/c04-i0120.png), where ![upper F Subscript upper X upper Z](images/c04-i0121.png) denotes the cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0122.png), was addressed. Consider the weighted average

[(4.3)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0003)![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi Baseline equals StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction period](images/c04-math-0003.png) ![Graphs depict conditional cdfs for the age at diagnosis depending on the genetic information provided by SNP10398 (left plot) and PGC-1a (right plot), computed from (4.2). Late onset group.](images/c04f003.png)

[Figure 4.3](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-fig-0003) (Parkinson's Disease Data, [Example 4.1.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002)). Conditional cdfs for the age at diagnosis depending on the genetic information provided by SNP10398 (left plot) and PGC‐1a (right plot), computed from [(4.2)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0002). Late onset group.

The estimator ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi](images/c04-i0123.png), referred to as the *Efron–Petrosian integral with covariates* by de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0004)), generalizes the empirical functional in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016) to the multivariate setting. When ![phi](images/c04-i0124.png) is the indicator function ![upper I left-parenthesis dot less-than-or-equal-to x comma dot less-than-or-equal-to z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0125.png), ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi](images/c04-i0126.png) provides an estimator of ![upper F Subscript upper X upper Z](images/c04-i0127.png) (*x, z*), ![upper F Subscript upper X upper Z comma n](images/c04-i0128.png) (*x, z*) say, whose margins can be used to estimate the cdfs of ![upper X](images/c04-i0129.png) and ![upper Z](images/c04-i0130.png). The first marginal of ![upper F Subscript upper X upper Z comma n](images/c04-i0131.png) is just the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml), while the second marginal overcomes the potential sampling bias on the covariate by using, precisely, the masses corresponding to that very NPMLE. An alternative way to write [(4.3)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0003) is ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper F Subscript n Baseline left-brace upper X Subscript i Baseline right-brace](images/c04-i0132.png), with ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-brace x right-brace equals upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x minus right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0133.png), or even ![upper S Subscript n Superscript phi Baseline equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis a Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0134.png); see [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) for the definition of the process ![a Subscript n](images/c04-i0135.png) and its main properties.

The following results formalize the asymptotic properties of [(4.3)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0003). The proofs can be found in de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0004)).

Immediate applications of [Theorem 4.1.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004) and [Corollary 4.1.3](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0006) are the estimation and inference for the means of ![upper X](images/c04-i0142.png) and ![upper Z](images/c04-i0143.png), the covariance between ![upper X](images/c04-i0144.png) and the components of ![upper Z](images/c04-i0145.png), the bivariate distribution ![upper F Subscript upper X upper Z](images/c04-i0146.png), the conditional cdfs of *X* corresponding to discrete components of ![upper Z](images/c04-i0147.png), and the marginal cdfs of ![upper X](images/c04-i0148.png) and ![upper Z](images/c04-i0149.png). All these quantities can be easily written as depending on functionals of type ![upper S Superscript phi](images/c04-i0150.png) by combining particular ![phi](images/c04-i0151.png) functions. The conditional cdfs have been already illustrated in [Example 4.1.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0002). Some of the other targets are considered in the following example.

## 4.2 Proportional Hazards Regression

The most widely used regression model in Survival Analysis is the Cox proportional hazards model, which makes use of the hazard function introduced in [Section 3.6](#c03.xhtml_c03-sec-0022). The Cox model, see for instance Klein and Moeschberger ([2003](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0009)), indicates that the hazard functions corresponding to different covariate values are proportional to each other. More specifically, let ![lamda 0](images/c04-i0167.png) be a baseline hazard function, and let ![beta](images/c04-i0168.png) be a vector of regression coefficients. Then, under the Cox model, the conditional hazard of ![upper X](images/c04-i0169.png) given ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0170.png) is

![Graphs depict consistent estimation of the cdf for the age at infection (black line) and ordinary ecdf (red line).](images/c04f004.png)

[Figure 4.4](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-fig-0004) (AIDS Blood Transfusion Data, [Example 4.1.4](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0008)). Consistent estimation of the cdf for the age at infection (black line) and ordinary ecdf (red line).

[(4.4)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0007)![lamda left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals lamda 0 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis exp left-parenthesis beta Superscript t Baseline z right-parenthesis period](images/c04-math-0007.png) 

Therefore, ![exp left-parenthesis beta Superscript t Baseline z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0171.png) is the hazard ratio which compares an individual with covariate ![z](images/c04-i0172.png) to an individual with baseline hazard.

Mandel et al. ([2018](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0011)) suggested the following estimating equation for the model [(4.4)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0007) under double truncation:

[(4.5)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0008)![upper U left-parenthesis beta right-parenthesis identical-to sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts left-bracket upper Z Subscript i Baseline minus StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper Z Subscript j Baseline exp left-parenthesis beta Superscript t Baseline upper Z Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts exp left-parenthesis beta Superscript t Baseline upper Z Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction right-bracket equals 0 period](images/c04-math-0008.png) 

Note that the solution to [Eq. (4.5)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0008) equals the maximizer of the usual Cox partial likelihood (Klein and Moeschberger, [2003](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0009)) when there is no observational bias, that is, when ![upper G Subscript n](images/c04-i0173.png) is constant. In the general setting of random double truncation, a justification for [(4.5)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0008) is given by the following result, which was noted by Mandel et al. ([2018](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0011)). The independence between ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0174.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0175.png) is implicitly assumed.

The asymptotic properties of the solution of [(4.5)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0008) established by Mandel et al. ([2018](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0011)) include the consistency and the distributional convergence. This is formally stated as a theorem, where ![beta 0](images/c04-i0176.png) denotes the true regression coefficient. The following conditions are used in the statement of the result:

1.  (C1) ![upper X](images/c04-i0177.png) is continuous and ![b Subscript upper X Baseline minus a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than infinity](images/c04-i0178.png).
2.  (C2) The covariates ![upper Z](images/c04-i0179.png) are bounded and linearly independent.
3.  (C3) The limiting first derivative of ![n Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper U left-parenthesis beta right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0180.png) is positive definite.

Apart from (C1)–(C3), the result in Mandel et al. ([2018](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0011)) is based on two conjectures: the uniform weak consistency of ![a Subscript n](images/c04-i0181.png), and an asymptotic representation for the process ![StartRoot n EndRoot left-parenthesis a Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis minus a left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0182.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c04-i0183.png). Interestingly, both conjectures hold under assumptions (A1)–(A4) in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016); see [Theorem 2.1.12](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0030).

Rennert and Xie ([2018](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0014)) proposed an alternative estimation approach for the proportional hazards model with doubly truncated data. Such an alternative approach weights the terms in [(4.5)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0008) by ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0186.png). These extra weights are not needed for consistency purposes, and they may result in standard errors larger than those corresponding to the solution of [(4.5)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0008), as pointed out in de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020b](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0003)); see also Vakulenko‐Lagun et al. ([2020](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0016)) for further comparative analyses.

The solution of the score equation [(4.5)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0008) can be easily computed in `R` by using the well known `coxph` function of the library `survival`, once the empirical sampling probabilities ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0187.png) have been calculated. Specifically, the transformed weights ![minus log left-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0188.png) must be included as an `offset` term within the `coxph` function. However, since the weights are random, the standard errors and the p‐values obtained in this way are no longer valid. In practice, the application of the simple bootstrap (see Definition 2.1.20) has been suggested; the simple bootstrap is used in the real data illustrations of this section.

## 4.3 Accelerated Failure Time Regression

The accelerated failure time (AFT) regression model is often introduced as a log‐linear regression model (Klein and Moeschberger, [2003](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0009)). Specifically, it is assumed that the log‐transformed response ![log left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0216.png) can be predicted by a linear equation ![beta Superscript t Baseline upper Z](images/c04-i0217.png) where the first element of the vector ![upper Z](images/c04-i0218.png) is constantly equal to 1 and, accordingly, the first element of the regression coefficient vector ![beta](images/c04-i0219.png) is an intercept. Formally, it is assumed

[(4.6)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0010)![log left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis equals beta Superscript t Baseline upper Z plus sigma epsilon](images/c04-math-0010.png) 

where ![epsilon](images/c04-i0220.png) is a (standardized) random error term independent of the covariate vector ![upper Z](images/c04-i0221.png) and ![sigma](images/c04-i0222.png) is a scale parameter. An alternative representation of the AFT model [(4.6)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0010) is ![upper S left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals upper S 0 left-parenthesis x exp left-parenthesis minus beta Superscript t Baseline z right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0223.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c04-i0224.png), where ![upper S left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0225.png) is the conditional survival function of ![upper X](images/c04-i0226.png) given ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0227.png) and ![upper S 0](images/c04-i0228.png) is the baseline survival, which is just the survival function of the transformed error ![exp left-parenthesis sigma epsilon right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0229.png). The term ![exp left-parenthesis minus beta Superscript t Baseline z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0230.png) is called the acceleration factor, and changes the timescale from the baseline timescale.

The semiparametric AFT model leaves unspecified the distribution of ![epsilon](images/c04-i0231.png) in [(4.6)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0010). Estimation of ![beta](images/c04-i0232.png) in the semiparametric setting can be performed by least‐squares. Under random double truncation, however, ordinary least squares may be inconsistent due to the sampling bias. A weighted least‐squares criterion that takes double truncation into account is given by

[(4.7)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0011)![upper S upper S Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis beta right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts left-bracket log left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis minus beta Superscript t Baseline upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-bracket squared upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period](images/c04-math-0011.png) 

Of course, this idea also applies to transformations other than the logarithm, and even for an untransformed response. The consistency and distributional convergence to a normal distribution of the minimizer of [(4.7)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0011) are immediately obtained from the results in [Section 4.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0002) when ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0233.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0234.png) are independent. This was detailed in de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0004)), who discussed extensions of the theory for a possibly nonlinear predictor and to M‐estimation too. Randomly weighted regression has been considered in other settings with incomplete information too; see for instance Stute ([1999](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0015)) for right‐censored data, or de Uña‐Álvarez ([2003](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0001)) for general biased sampling models. Each of these settings requires a specific sequence of weights replacing ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0235.png) in [(4.7)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0011).

The AFT model [(4.6)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0010) has been more traditionally used in the fully parametric setting in which the error distribution is specified. In that case, maximum likelihood estimation is possible. The details of the parametric approach under random double truncation were recently provided by Dörre et al. ([2021](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0005)). In the parametric regression setting it is assumed that the truncation variables are independent of ![upper X](images/c04-i0236.png) conditionally on the covariates ![upper Z](images/c04-i0237.png), which is weaker than the independence between ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0238.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0239.png). The conditional independence assumption is also needed for the alternative approach in Ying et al. ([2020](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0018)), who proposed a Mann–Whitney type estimating equation based on comparable pairs, so the possible truncation bias is removed. In the last few years other methods for regression analysis with a doubly truncated response have been introduced, and covering all of them would require a much longer chapter.

There exists an interesting connection between the proportional hazards model studied in [Section 4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0007) and multiple linear regression. Specifically, under [(4.4)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0007) it holds ![log left-parenthesis normal upper Lamda 0 left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis right-parenthesis equals minus beta Superscript t Baseline upper Z plus epsilon](images/c04-i0254.png), where ![normal upper Lamda 0](images/c04-i0255.png) is the cumulative baseline hazard and ![epsilon](images/c04-i0256.png) is a extreme‐value variable independent of the covariates. This general property shows in particular that the regression models RD and RC fit the linear regression setup, provided that the transformation ![log left-parenthesis minus log left-parenthesis 1 minus upper X right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0257.png) is used for the response.

![Graphs depict left plot shows regression line for the transformed response log(-log(1-X)) in model RC with β=-1 (dashed line), and ordinary maximum-likelihood estimation of the regression line (continuous red line) based on a single sample of size n=1000 from the truncation scenario II in Section 2.1.3\. Right plot shows conditional cdfs F(·|0) and F(·|1) in model RD with β=-1 (dashed lines), and ordinary ecdf (solid lines) based on a single sample of size n=1000 from the truncation scenario II in Section 2.1.3.](images/c04f005.png)

Right plot: conditional cdfs ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 0 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0295.png) and ![upper F left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar 1 right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0296.png) in model RD with ![beta equals negative 1](images/c04-i0297.png) (dashed lines), and ordinary ecdf (solid lines) based on a single sample of size ![n equals 1000](images/c04-i0298.png) from the truncation scenario II in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).
