## 4.4 Nonparametric Regression

Nonparametric regression techniques aim for the estimation of the regression function ![m left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals upper E left-bracket upper X vertical-bar upper Z equals z right-bracket](images/c04-i0309.png) without imposing any parametric or semiparametric structure on the relationship between the response variable ![upper X](images/c04-i0310.png) and the covariate vector ![upper Z](images/c04-i0311.png). Thus, they are conceptually different from the methods reviewed in [Sections 4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0007) and [4.3](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0012), which were based on a proportional hazards assumption or on an accelerated failure time structure, respectively. Nonparametric methods allow for a preliminary, or exploratory, data analysis before any reasonable model can be postulated. Under another viewpoint, nonparametric procedures serve to get conclusions on the target population without any pre‐assumed structure that may compromise the inferences. Several nonparametric regression methods exist. In this section we focus on kernel smoothing, which has been already used in [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml) to nonparametrically estimate the pdf and the hazard function of ![upper X](images/c04-i0312.png), and which can be easily adapted to estimate ![m left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0313.png) (Wand and Jones, [1995](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0017); Härdle et al., [2004](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0008)). Due to the *curse of dimensionality*, which is one of the major drawbacks of nonparametric regression, we focus on the case of a one‐dimensional (continuous) covariate ![upper Z](images/c04-i0314.png). Nonparametric regression estimators with covariates of higher dimension may converge slowly, and their application requires caution. Kernel regression methods with a left‐truncated response have been deeply investigated in the literature; see for instance Ould‐Saïd and Lemdani ([2006](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0013)), Liang et al. ([2011](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0010)) and references therein. Here we consider extensions of such procedures to the doubly truncated setup.

![Graphs depict Ordinary least squares parabolic fit (blue line) and weighted fit (red line).](images/c04f006.png)

[Figure 4.6](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-fig-0006) (AIDS Blood Transfusion Data, [Example 4.3.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0020)). Ordinary least squares parabolic fit (blue line) and weighted fit (red line).

When ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0315.png) is independent of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper Z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0316.png), the observable conditional cdf of ![upper X](images/c04-i0317.png) given ![upper Z](images/c04-i0318.png) is given by

![upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis normal d t vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-math-0012.png)

where recall that ![alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0319.png) stands for the conditional probability of no truncation, ![alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V vertical-bar upper Z equals z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0320.png). Conversely,

[(4.8)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0013)![upper F left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript x Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis normal d t vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c04-math-0013.png) 

where ![alpha left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals left-parenthesis integral Subscript a Subscript upper X Baseline Superscript b Subscript upper X Baseline Baseline upper G left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline upper F Superscript asterisk Baseline left-parenthesis normal d t vertical-bar z right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0321.png). [Equation (4.8)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0013) is the conditional analogue of [Eq. (2.8)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0017) in [Section 2.1.1](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0003), and is key to derive a consistent estimator of the regression function.

Due to [(4.8)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0013), the target ![m left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals upper E left-bracket upper X vertical-bar upper Z equals z right-bracket](images/c04-i0322.png) can be written as

[(4.9)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0014)![m left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals StartFraction upper E left-bracket upper X 1 upper G left-parenthesis upper X 1 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline vertical-bar upper Z 1 equals z right-bracket Over upper E left-bracket upper G left-parenthesis upper X 1 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline vertical-bar upper Z 1 equals z right-bracket EndFraction period](images/c04-math-0014.png) 

By plugging‐in Nadaraya–Watson type smoothers (Wand and Jones, [1995](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0017); Härdle et al., [2004](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0008)) for both the numerator and denominator in [(4.9)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0014), and ![upper G Subscript n](images/c04-i0323.png) for ![upper G](images/c04-i0324.png), one gets

[(4.10)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0015)![m Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction period](images/c04-math-0015.png) 

Here, similar to [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml), ![upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis dot right-parenthesis equals upper L left-parenthesis dot slash h right-parenthesis slash h](images/c04-i0325.png) denotes the rescaled kernel function, and ![h](images/c04-i0326.png) the bandwidth or smoothing parameter. Note that, in order to obtain [(4.10)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0015), the conditional probability of no truncation given ![upper Z equals z](images/c04-i0327.png) has been estimated by

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column alpha Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Over sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c04-math-0016.png)

When there is no truncation, or when the sampling probabilities ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0328.png) are constant, the estimator [(4.10)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0015) reduces to the standard Nadaraya–Watson estimator. In general, however, the weights ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0329.png) are needed to overcome the potential sampling bias; see Moreira et al. ([2016](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0012)) for further discussions.

The Nadaraya–Watson estimation is a special case of local polynomial regression, which proceeds by locally fitting to the data a polynomial of degree ![p](images/c04-i0330.png). Specifically, let ![left-parenthesis beta Subscript 0 comma n Baseline comma beta Subscript 1 comma n Baseline comma ellipsis comma beta Subscript p comma n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0331.png) be the minimizer of the weighted sum of squares

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus beta 0 minus beta 1 left-parenthesis upper Z Subscript i Baseline minus z right-parenthesis minus ellipsis minus beta Subscript p Baseline left-parenthesis upper Z Subscript i Baseline minus z right-parenthesis Superscript p Baseline right-parenthesis squared upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c04-math-0017.png)

Then, ![m Subscript left-parenthesis p right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals beta Subscript 0 comma n](images/c04-i0332.png) is the local polynomial estimator of ![m left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0333.png), adapted to the doubly truncated setting. When ![p equals 0](images/c04-i0334.png) the estimator ![m Subscript left-parenthesis p right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0335.png) is called the local constant estimator, and coincides with the Nadaraya–Watson [(4.10)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0015). When ![p equals 1](images/c04-i0336.png), the local linear estimator is obtained; this is explicitly given by

[(4.11)](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-disp-0018)![m Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction left-bracket s 2 left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis minus s 1 left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper Z Subscript i Baseline minus z right-parenthesis right-bracket upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over s 2 left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis s 0 left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis minus s 1 left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis squared EndFraction](images/c04-math-0018.png) 

where ![s Subscript r Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts left-parenthesis upper Z Subscript i Baseline minus z right-parenthesis Superscript r Baseline upper L Subscript h Baseline left-parenthesis z minus upper Z Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0337.png), ![0 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to 2](images/c04-i0338.png). In the standard setting of random sampling, ![m Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0339.png) is usually preferred to ![m Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0340.png) due to its smaller bias and boundary adaptability, and the same relative behavior is found under random truncation. The interested reader may find more details on local polynomial regression in Fan and Gijbels ([1996](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0006)), Wand and Jones ([1995](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0017)) or Härdle et al. ([2004](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0008)).

The asymptotic properties of [(4.10)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0015) and [(4.11)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0018) can be investigated from standard arguments when ![upper G Subscript n](images/c04-i0341.png) is replaced by the true underlying ![upper G](images/c04-i0342.png). Let ![ModifyingAbove m With bar Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0343.png) and ![ModifyingAbove m With bar Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0344.png) denote such asymptotic versions. As in [Chapter 3](#c03.xhtml), it can be argued that both ![StartRoot n h EndRoot left-parenthesis m Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis minus ModifyingAbove m With bar Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0345.png) and ![StartRoot n h EndRoot left-parenthesis m Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis minus ModifyingAbove m With bar Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0346.png) converge to zero in probability and, thus, the asymptotic bias and variance of ![ModifyingAbove m With bar Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0347.png) and ![ModifyingAbove m With bar Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0348.png) are valid measures of performance of the local constant and local linear estimators. Asymptotic expressions for these measures are given in the following result.

[Theorem 4.4.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0022) indicates that double truncation does not affect the asymptotic bias in nonparametric regression, since the formulae for the mean values are the ones corresponding to random sampling. The extra bias term appearing for the Nadaraya–Watson estimator, ![mu 2 left-parenthesis upper L right-parenthesis h squared m prime left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis f prime Subscript upper Z Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0363.png), is responsible for its poorer performance when compared to the local linear estimator. On the other hand, double truncation does affect the variance term; with random sampling, ![alpha sigma squared left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0364.png) reduces to ![sigma 0 squared left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis equals upper E left-bracket left-parenthesis upper X minus m left-parenthesis upper Z right-parenthesis right-parenthesis squared vertical-bar upper Z equals z right-bracket](images/c04-i0365.png). Interestingly, ![alpha sigma squared left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0366.png) may be smaller or larger than ![sigma 0 squared left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0367.png), indicating that more or less sampling effort around ![z](images/c04-i0368.png) is induced by the double truncation. This is similar to what has been reported throughout the book for estimators of other targets.

In practice, computation of ![m Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0369.png) and ![m Subscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis z right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0370.png) can be easily done by using standard software for nonparametric regression, once the sampling probabilities ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c04-i0371.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c04-i0372.png), have been calculated. For example, the function `sm.regression{sm}` provides the correct estimates when using ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c04-i0373.png) within its `weights` option. As for the selection of the smoothing parameter ![h](images/c04-i0374.png), Moreira et al. ([2016](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0012)) adapted the direct plug‐in approach of Härdle and Marron ([1995](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0007)) and cross‐validation ideas to the doubly truncated setting. These authors showed by simulations that the bandwidth selectors perform satisfactorily, and that their relative performance varies depending on the regression estimator being employed and the particular scenario. Both direct plug‐in and cross‐validation bandwidths are applied in the following example. Note also that, as it happens for smooth density estimation, the consideration of a global smoothing parameter may not be adequate if the regression curve exhibits different degrees of smoothness along the covariate range. In that case, locally adapted procedures, such as those based on the ideas of nearest neighbours, may be an option (see Fan and Gijbels ([1996](#c04.xhtml_c04-bib-0006)), Section 4.2.1).

Blue line: CV bandwidth. Thin black lines correspond to the naive estimators that ignore double truncation, based on the DPI bandwidths. Gray lines correspond to the estimators computed along a grid of bandwidths ranging from 5 to 25 with step 2.](images/c04f007.png)

[Figure 4.7](#c04.xhtml_R_c04-fig-0007) (AIDS Blood Transfusion Data. [Example 4.4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0024)). Nonparametric regression smoothers for the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data. Thin black lines correspond to the naive estimators that ignore double truncation, based on the DPI bandwidths. # 5
Further Topics

In this chapter we consider a number of additional topics related to the analysis of doubly truncated data. An important issue is the adaptation of classical two‐sample tests for the potential sampling bias. In [Section 5.1](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0002) we will see that ordinary methods may perform well under double truncation, particularly when the truncation pattern is the same for the groups being compared. Special settings in which the classical two‐sample test may fail will be discussed. [Section 5.2](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0006) is devoted to the competing risks setting, of much interest in Survival Analysis and Reliability, in which several event types are present. Here we deal with two relevant goals: the nonparametric estimation of the cumulative incidence functions attached to the competing risks process, and regression approaches to investigate the influence of covariates on the cause‐specific hazards, or transition intensities. In both cases the usual methods must be modified by weighting the event times so the sampling bias is corrected, similar to previous chapters.

When ![upper X](images/c05-i0001.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0002.png) are dependent, the consistency of the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE is not ensured. Then, a critical issue is the detection of, and the correction for, the possible dependence between the truncating variables and the target. These topics are covered in [Sections 5.3](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0015) and [5.4](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0018) respectively. More specifically, in [Section 5.3](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0015) quasi‐independence tests based on association measures are reviewed and applied to simulated and real data examples. On the other hand, in order to adapt the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE to possibly dependent truncation, an approach based on copula functions is followed. In [Section 5.4](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0018) we will see that handling with dependent double truncation is non‐trivial, and that the methods available at the time of writing restrict us to the scenario of interval sampling. This is an exciting area of research nowadays, in which several open problems remain; among them, the issue of copula selection, and the formal derivation of theoretical properties for the corrected NPMLE.

## 5.1 Two‐Sample Problems

In [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) appropriate tools for estimating the distribution of a target variable ![upper X](images/c05-i0003.png) under a truncation scheme were reviewed. When ![upper X](images/c05-i0004.png) is potentially influenced by a certain categorical covariate, it may be interesting to assess possible differences between distributions. We will restrict ourselves to the two‐sample problem, providing a brief literature review and illustrating through simulations and real data analysis how the classical rank‐based test may be affected by truncation.

Specifically, the goal is to test ![upper H 0 colon upper F 1 equals upper F 2](images/c05-i0005.png) vs. ![upper H Subscript a Baseline colon upper F 1 not-equals upper F 2](images/c05-i0006.png), where ![upper F Subscript j](images/c05-i0007.png) is the cdf of the target variable ![upper X](images/c05-i0008.png) in group ![j](images/c05-i0009.png), ![j equals 1 comma 2](images/c05-i0010.png). In a non‐truncated scenario, classical tests were proposed by Mann and Whitney ([1947](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0013)) and Wilcoxon ([1945](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0023)) based on observation ranks, which indicate the position of the ![upper X](images/c05-i0011.png)‐values in the combined ordered sequence. The Mann–Whitney statistic can be alternatively introduced as a weighted difference between the hazard functions pertaining to the two groups. Specifically, consider

[(5.1)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0001)![upper U Subscript n Baseline equals integral w Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis normal d left-parenthesis normal upper Lamda Subscript 1 comma n Baseline minus normal upper Lamda Subscript 2 comma n Baseline right-parenthesis left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis comma](images/c05-math-0001.png) 

where ![w Subscript n](images/c05-i0012.png) is the weighting function and ![normal upper Lamda Subscript j comma n](images/c05-i0013.png) denotes the empirical cumulative hazard computed from the ![j](images/c05-i0014.png)th group. Under random sampling this is given by ![normal upper Lamda Subscript j comma n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to t comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline equals j right-parenthesis left-parenthesis sigma-summation Underscript k equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript k Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper Z Subscript k Baseline equals j right-parenthesis right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c05-i0015.png), where ![upper Z Subscript i](images/c05-i0016.png) is the group indicator for observation ![upper X Subscript i](images/c05-i0017.png). The Mann–Whitney test corresponds to the choice ![w Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis equals product Underscript j equals 1 Overscript 2 Endscripts sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to t comma upper Z Subscript i Baseline equals j right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0018.png). [Equation (5.1)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0001) reveals that the Mann–Whitney statistic measures the departure between the hazards, paying particular attention to the ![upper X](images/c05-i0019.png)‐values at which the number of individuals at risk, ![w Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0020.png), is large.

Some adaptations of classical two‐sample tests for censored and truncated data exist. For instance, Gehan ([1965](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0009)) introduced an extension of the Mann–Whitney test for right‐censored data, by restricting the jumps of ![normal upper Lamda Subscript j comma n](images/c05-i0021.png) to the uncensored ![upper X Subscript i](images/c05-i0022.png)'s, the so‐called Nelson–Aalen estimator for the cumulative hazard. Other choices for ![w Subscript n](images/c05-i0023.png) in [(5.1)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0001) lead to alternative extensions of the Mann–Whitney procedure, like the one proposed by Peto and Peto ([1972](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0018)); or the popular log‐rank test, which is optimal to detect group differences under a proportional hazards assumption. See Klein and Moeschberger ([2003](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0010)) for alternative weights. In the same context Pepe and Fleming ([1989](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0017)) introduced as test statistic the distance between the Kaplan–Meier curves of the two groups. These ideas were also considered by Chi et al. ([2007](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0003)) for right‐truncation, specifically using an integrated weighted difference between the estimated survival functions. Also for one‐sided truncation Lagakos ([1988](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0011)) introduced a weighted log‐rank test for comparing two distributions. Gehan's idea was adapted to the left‐truncated and right‐censored scenario by Shen ([2009](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0019)).

The test statistic [(5.1)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0001) was extended for doubly truncated data by Shen ([2013](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0021)), who suggested replacing the usual Nelson–Aalen cumulative hazard ![normal upper Lamda Subscript j comma n](images/c05-i0024.png) by the one attached to the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE. One interesting question at this point is whether the standard Mann–Whitney test is outperformed in this way. On the one hand, as indicated in previous chapters, the recovery of a doubly truncated distribution typically suffers from a large variance, and this may result in a loss of statistical power when testing for differences among the groups. On the other hand, even when the standard Nelson–Aalen estimator may be systematically biased under double truncation, the limit of the distance [(5.1)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0001) could be larger for the biased hazards than for the true ones, advising against the application of any correction. Regarding the type I error note that, when the sampling probability ![upper G](images/c05-i0025.png) is shared by the groups being compared, the usual Mann–Whitney procedure is well calibrated, since the truncated cdfs coincide under the null hypothesis ![upper H 0 colon upper F 1 equals upper F 2](images/c05-i0026.png) in such a case. In order to illustrate all these aspects, in the following examples we analyze how the truncation pattern affects the calibration and power of the classical Mann–Whitney test.

As a summary of the previous examples we may conclude that, when the sampling probability ![upper G](images/c05-i0118.png) is the same for both populations, any classical two‐sample test will generally provide reasonable results. Actually, when ![upper G](images/c05-i0119.png) is shared by the two groups, there is no reason for conjecturing a better behavior of a test adapted for double truncation. As mentioned, the estimation of ![upper G](images/c05-i0120.png) introduces an extra variability which may result in a smaller statistical power. Even more, the departure between the biased cdfs ![upper F 1 Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0121.png) and ![upper F 2 Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0122.png) could be larger than the one between ![upper F 1](images/c05-i0123.png) and ![upper F 2](images/c05-i0124.png); if so, a classical test would be even more powerful than the respective correction for double truncation.

| Density ![f 2](images/c05-i0102.png): | ![f 20](images/c05-i0103.png) | ![f 21](images/c05-i0104.png) | ![f 22](images/c05-i0105.png) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Level | ![n equals 100](images/c05-i0106.png) | ![n equals 500](images/c05-i0107.png) | ![n equals 100](images/c05-i0108.png) | ![n equals 500](images/c05-i0109.png) | ![n equals 100](images/c05-i0110.png) | ![n equals 500](images/c05-i0111.png) |
| 0.01 | 0.016 | 0.013 | 0.011 | 0.072 | 0.029 | 0.144 |
| 0.05 | 0.059 | 0.071 | 0.054 | 0.180 | 0.104 | 0.302 |
| 0.10 | 0.106 | 0.135 | 0.116 | 0.280 | 0.165 | 0.423 |

Performance of the Mann–Whitney test for ![upper F 1 not-equals upper F 2](images/c05-i0094.png) for the simulation scenarios I (for *F[1]*) and II (for *F[2]*) in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016) with *f[1]* uniform in (0,1) and ![f 2](images/c05-i0095.png) proportional to ![1 slash upper G 2](images/c05-i0096.png) (left) and ![f 2](images/c05-i0097.png) far from ![1 slash upper G 2](images/c05-i0098.png), illustrating close (left) and far from ![upper H 0](images/c05-i0099.png) alternatives (middle and right), respectively. Proportions of rejections of ![upper H 0](images/c05-i0100.png) along 1000 Monte Carlo replicates for different sample sizes ![n](images/c05-i0101.png) and significance levels.

![Images described by caption.](images/c05f001.png)

[Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-fig-0001) Density functions for the two groups in [Example 5.1.2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0004), based on the simulation scenarios I (for *F[1]*) and II (for *F[2]*) in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016). Left plot: ![f 2 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals 1 slash 2 plus x](images/c05-i0112.png) (black dashed line). Right plot: ![f 2 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals 1 slash 2 plus x](images/c05-i0113.png) for ![x element-of left-parenthesis 0.1 comma 0.9 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0114.png) (dashed line) and for ![x element-of left-parenthesis 0.25 comma 0.75 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0115.png) (dotted line), and constant out of these interval. The solid black line corresponds to the normalized version of ![1 slash upper G 2](images/c05-i0116.png), and the red dashed line is the density ![f 1](images/c05-i0117.png).

Nevertheless, in our simulated scenarios we have also noted a deficient behavior of the classical Mann–Whitney test, both in terms of size and power; this applies to other ordinary two‐sample tests too. On the one hand, if the ![upper G](images/c05-i0125.png) function is not the same in both groups, the test is not correctly calibrated. On the other hand, the test may present an extremely low power if ![upper F 1 Superscript asterisk Baseline almost-equals upper F 2 Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0126.png) but ![upper F 1 not-equals upper F 2](images/c05-i0127.png); a situation that may also happen with different ![upper G](images/c05-i0128.png) functions in both groups. In these cases, it may be worth to consider a suitably adapted test for handling double truncation. The log‐rank based test in Shen ([2013](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0021)) could be used for this purpose. Unfortunately, to our knowledge this corrected test is not available in `R`. Besides, its implementation is not obvious from the details provided in the referred paper, and this is why we have not explored its performance in the previous examples. However, we applied the classical Mann–Whitney test to the scenarios simulated by Shen ([2013](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0021)), obtaining competitive or even better results, both for calibration and power. This is in well agreement with our previous considerations, given that the designs considered in Shen ([2013](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0021)) correspond to the situation of a shared truncation pattern.

To sum up, if there is no evidence for suspecting that the biasing functions are different in the two groups, then the classical Mann–Whitney test should perform correctly, and can be recommended due to its simplicity. Actually, a non‐corrected two‐sample test may provide better results, in terms of power, than a corrected procedure when the ![x](images/c05-i0129.png)‐values at which ![upper F 1 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0130.png) departs from ![upper F 2 left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0131.png) are oversampled, and this should be taken into account in practice.

In the following example we illustrate the issue of two‐sample testing under double truncation in a practical setting.

## 5.2 Competing Risks

Competing risks data are often encountered in Survival Analysis and Reliability Engineering, among other fields. Competing risks relate situations in which more than one event type is present. In Survival Analysis, for instance, this may occur due to the identification of several causes of death; in Engineering, the separate investigation of two or more types of failure leads to a competing risks setting too. With competing risk, the event time ![upper X](images/c05-i0139.png) comes with the event type indicator ![eta](images/c05-i0140.png), which takes values within the set ![StartSet 1 comma ellipsis comma upper R EndSet](images/c05-i0141.png). Here, ![upper R](images/c05-i0142.png) is the number of different event types. The event type indicator ![eta](images/c05-i0143.png) may be seen as a covariate in the sense of the vector ![upper Z](images/c05-i0144.png) in [Chapter 4](#c04.xhtml). This identification is convenient since, as we will see, much intuition for the construction of estimators is gained by applying the ideas and results in the previous chapter. However, ![eta](images/c05-i0145.png) should not be taken as a baseline covariate, because its value is only known at the event time.

| ICCC group | Number of cases (%) | Age ![upper X](images/c05-i0151.png) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| I | 107 (26.7%) | 6.30 (4.16) |
| II | 57 (14.2%) | 8.66 (4.39) |
| III | 94 (23.4%) | 6.38 (4.29) |
| IV | 38 (9.5%) | 3.16 (3.47) |
| V–XII | 105 (26.2%) | 6.87 (4.70) |

[Table 5.3](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-tbl-0003) Childhood Cancer Data. Number of cases (and %) and mean (and SD) for the age at diagnosis for the ICCC groups.

Throughout this chapter we will naturally refer to the target variable ![upper X](images/c05-i0146.png) as the lifetime or the event time. In the preceding chapters we have seen several examples in which a lifetime is doubly truncated due to interval sampling, and this may occur with competing risks too. This is the case of the Childhood Cancer Data in [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009), which we take here as an interesting example of doubly truncated competing risks data.

[Example 5.2.1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0008) is not the standard situation with competing risks data, which are typically recorded along a prospective follow‐up. It rather refers to registry data on children already diagnosed from cancer. This excludes the possibility of having a portion of the sample without the event of interest (cancer diagnosis), who would report a right‐censored value of ![upper X](images/c05-i0152.png) at the end of the follow‐up. In other words, the analysis is focused on the distribution of ![upper X](images/c05-i0153.png) (or on its subdistributions restricted by ![eta](images/c05-i0154.png)) given that one will get cancer anyway. This is the situation by force, because no information on immunes is available.

### 5.2.1 Cumulative Incidences

A competing risks process is characterized by the cumulative incidence functions (CIFs), defined as ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x comma eta equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0155.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c05-i0156.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R](images/c05-i0157.png). Under random double truncation these functions can be consistently estimated following the ideas in [Section 4.1](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0002); see comments below [Eq. (4.2)](#c04.xhtml_c04-disp-0002). This was thoroughly investigated in de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0007)), and the main ideas will be reviewed and further illustrated here. One critical point in the construction of the estimators is to assess the independence between the truncating variables ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0158.png) and the competing risks process, which is represented by the couple ![left-parenthesis upper X comma eta right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0159.png). In de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0007)) two different situations were considered:

1.  (H1) ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0160.png) and ![left-parenthesis upper X comma eta right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0161.png) are independent.
2.  (H2) ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0162.png) and ![upper X](images/c05-i0163.png) are conditionally independent given ![eta](images/c05-i0164.png).

Under the unconditional independence hypothesis (H1) the indicators ![upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x comma eta Subscript i Baseline equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0165.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c05-i0166.png), can be weighted by the same sequence of weights

[(5.2)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0002)![ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline equals StartFraction upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n comma](images/c05-math-0002.png) 

regardless of the event type ![r](images/c05-i0167.png). Note that, indeed, [(5.2)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0002) are the probability masses attached to the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE. Then, the empirical CIFs ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis dot right-parenthesis equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts ModifyingAbove phi With Ì‚ Subscript i Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to dot comma eta Subscript i Baseline equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0168.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R](images/c05-i0169.png), are consistent estimators for their population counterparts ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0170.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R](images/c05-i0171.png). This is no longer true when (H1) is violated. Hypothesis (H2) is less restrictive; the drawback is that the weights in [(5.2)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0002) must be redefined in that case, so they become ![r](images/c05-i0172.png)‐specific. Actually, these new weights correspond to the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE when computed from the subset of individuals undergoing event type ![r](images/c05-i0173.png). This sample reduction will generally result in quite larger standard errors, and may even induce existence and uniqueness issues for the weights, as explained in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml). These potential difficulties will be illustrated soon through the analysis of the Childhood Cancer Data, see [Example 5.2.4](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0014).

The empirical CIFs that are consistent merely under the conditional independence hypothesis (H2) are defined as follows:

[(5.3)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0003)![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon r right-parenthesis equals StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x comma eta Subscript i Baseline equals r right-parenthesis upper G Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript r equals 1 Overscript upper R Endscripts sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis eta Subscript i Baseline equals r right-parenthesis upper G Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R period](images/c05-math-0003.png) 

Here, ![upper G Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0174.png) is the NPMLE of ![upper G Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to upper V vertical-bar eta equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0175.png) computed from the subset ![StartSet i colon eta Subscript i Baseline equals r EndSet](images/c05-i0176.png). The following notation is convenient: ![n Subscript r Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis eta Subscript i Baseline equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0183.png),

and

It holds ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon r right-parenthesis equals alpha overbar Subscript n Baseline n Superscript negative 1 Baseline sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x comma eta Subscript i Baseline equals r right-parenthesis upper G Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c05-i0184.png).

The uniform consistency and the weak convergence of ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n](images/c05-i0185.png) and ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis dot semicolon r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0186.png) are established in the two following theorems.

[Theorem 5.2.2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0010) and [Theorem 5.2.3](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0012) are the analogue of [Theorem 2.1.9](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0024)(a) (consistency) and [Theorem 2.1.10](#c02.xhtml_c02-fea-0026) (weak convergence) when dealing with cumulative incidences rather than with a cdf. The proof of these results follows from the asymptotic theory developed by de Uña‐Álvarez and Van Keilegom ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0008)). For example, the consistency of ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0199.png) is obtained almost immediately from the theory corresponding to the Efron‐Petrosian integrals with covariates, see [Theorem 4.1.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-fea-0004). Indeed, ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0200.png) equals ![upper S Superscript phi](images/c05-i0201.png) for the special ![phi](images/c05-i0202.png)‐function ![phi Subscript x comma r Baseline left-parenthesis t comma j right-parenthesis equals upper I left-parenthesis t less-than-or-equal-to x comma j equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0203.png). The case of [(5.3)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0003) is a bit more involved and requires some care. In de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0007)) the relationship between ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0204.png) and a conditional NPMLE was pointed out. With the notation in [(5.4)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0004) and [(5.5)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0005) it holds

[(5.6)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0006)![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline left-parenthesis x semicolon r right-parenthesis equals StartFraction n Subscript r Baseline Over n EndFraction StartFraction alpha overbar Subscript n Baseline Over alpha Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline EndFraction upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar r right-parenthesis comma](images/c05-math-0006.png) 

where ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0205.png) is the NPMLE of ![upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x vertical-bar eta equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0206.png) based on the subsample ![StartSet i colon eta Subscript i Baseline equals r EndSet](images/c05-i0207.png) of size ![n Subscript r](images/c05-i0208.png). Now, by using the technicalities similar to those in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml), it is easy to see that ![alpha Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma n Baseline right-arrow upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V vertical-bar eta equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0209.png), ![alpha overbar Subscript n Baseline right-arrow upper P left-parenthesis upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0210.png) and ![upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar r right-parenthesis right-arrow upper P left-parenthesis upper X less-than-or-equal-to x vertical-bar eta equals r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0211.png) in probability as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c05-i0212.png) under (H2). Besides, by the law of the large numbers we have ![n Subscript r Baseline slash n right-arrow upper P left-parenthesis eta equals r vertical-bar upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0213.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c05-i0214.png). This, together with [(5.6)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0006), leads to the consistency of [(5.3)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0003). Further details needed when proving consistency and weak convergence for the empirical CIFs are given in the technical appendix of de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0007)).
