### 5.2.2 Regression Models for Competing Risks

Regression models with competing risks are useful to investigate the relationship between a given vector of covariates and the intensities of the several event types. Formally, the transition intensities of a competing risks model with ![upper R](images/c05-i0224.png) event types are defined as

![lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals limit Underscript white up pointing small triangle x down-arrow 0 Endscripts upper P left-parenthesis x less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than x plus white up pointing triangle x comma eta equals r vertical-bar upper X greater-than-or-equal-to x right-parenthesis equals StartFraction f Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis Over 1 minus upper F left-parenthesis x minus right-parenthesis EndFraction comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R comma](images/c05-math-0007.png)

where ![f Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0225.png) denotes the subdensity attached to the cumulative incidence ![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0226.png); see for example Klein and Moeschberger ([2003](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0010)) or Beyersmann et al. ([2012](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0002)). The transition intensities, also called cause‐specific hazards, determine the CIFs of the competing risks process through the integral equation

![upper F Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral Subscript 0 Superscript x Baseline lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis exp left-parenthesis minus sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript upper R Endscripts normal upper Lamda Subscript left-parenthesis j right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis normal d t comma](images/c05-math-0008.png)

where, for each ![r element-of StartSet 1 comma ellipsis comma upper R EndSet](images/c05-i0227.png), ![normal upper Lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis equals integral Subscript 0 Superscript x Baseline lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis t right-parenthesis normal d t](images/c05-i0228.png) stands for the cumulative transition intensity. Note that, without loss of generality, throughout this section we naturally take zero as the lower limit of the support of ![upper X](images/c05-i0229.png).

A cause‐specific hazards regression model is a model that links the ![lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0230.png)'s to a covariate vector ![upper Z](images/c05-i0231.png), whose components may be different for different values of ![r](images/c05-i0232.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R](images/c05-i0233.png). In such a regression setting we use the conditional transition intensities ![lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis dot vertical-bar z right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0234.png), which are defined conditionally on ![upper Z equals z](images/c05-i0235.png). A useful modelling approach in this setting is given through the proportional cause‐specific hazards assumption, under which the ratio of transition intensities belonging to individuals with different covariates is constant along time. Specifically, it is assumed

![lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis x vertical-bar z right-parenthesis equals lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma 0 Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis exp left-parenthesis beta Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Superscript t Baseline z right-parenthesis comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R comma](images/c05-math-0009.png)

where ![lamda Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis comma 0](images/c05-i0236.png) is the baseline transition intensity and ![beta Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0237.png) is the regression coefficient.

In the setting with no sampling bias, the regression coefficient for event type ![r](images/c05-i0238.png) is estimated by maximizing the usual Cox partial likelihood by handling the event types different from ![r](images/c05-i0239.png) as censoring. Under random double truncation, similar to the case in [Section 4.2](#c04.xhtml_c04-sec-0007), an inverse probability weighting strategy is needed for consistency purposes. When the sampling probability ![upper G](images/c05-i0240.png) is not influenced by the covariates, this can be easily done by using the same sequence of weights ![upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1](images/c05-i0241.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c05-i0242.png), to modify all the score equations. To be specific, for ![1 less-than-or-equal-to r less-than-or-equal-to upper R](images/c05-i0243.png) the coefficient ![beta Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0244.png) can be estimated by the solution of

[(5.7)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0010)![sigma-summation Underscript i colon eta Subscript i Baseline equals r Endscripts left-bracket upper Z Subscript i Baseline minus StartFraction sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts upper Z Subscript j Baseline exp left-parenthesis beta Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Superscript t Baseline upper Z Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts exp left-parenthesis beta Subscript left-parenthesis r right-parenthesis Superscript t Baseline upper Z Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline greater-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis upper G Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline EndFraction right-bracket equals 0 period](images/c05-math-0010.png) 

The theoretical properties of these weighted maximum partial likelihood estimators are analogous are those corresponding to the solution of (4.5).

## 5.3 Testing for Quasi‐independence

Throughout this book it has been assumed that the truncating couple ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0306.png) is independent of the target variable ![upper X](images/c05-i0307.png). This independence assumption is critical in the construction of the NPMLE, and also to establish the asymptotic properties of the NPMLE. In particular, the consistency of ![upper F Subscript n](images/c05-i0308.png) is not ensured when ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0309.png) and ![upper X](images/c05-i0310.png) are dependent. An alternative estimator of ![upper F](images/c05-i0311.png) that takes the possible dependence between truncation and target variables into account is reviewed in [Section 5.4](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0018).

Strictly speaking, a condition weaker than independent truncation is enough for the consistency of the NPMLE. This has been deeply investigated under one‐sided truncation (Martin and Betensky, [2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014); Vakulenko‐Lagun et al. [2019](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0022)), and some extensions for doubly truncated data have been studied too (Martin and Betensky, [2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014); Shen, [2011](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0020); Austin et al., [2014](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0001)). Actually, since the sampling information on ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0312.png) is restricted to the region ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper V](images/c05-i0313.png), there is no way to assess the independence between ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0314.png) and ![upper X](images/c05-i0315.png) on their whole supports. At maximum, one will see that the truncating couple and the target variable behave in the observable region as if they were independent in the unobservable world. This has been termed *quasi‐independence* in the literature on randomly truncated data. For one‐sided truncation Vakulenko‐Lagun et al. ([2019](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0022)) justified that the Lynden‐Bell estimator (Lynden–Bell, [1971](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0012)) is consistent merely under quasi‐independence. Moreover, they revealed and discussed a non‐identifiability aspect of quasi‐independence that had been unnoticed up to then. Similar issues appear by force under double truncation, which is an extension of the one‐sided truncation setup.

Several tests for quasi‐independence with doubly truncated data have been proposed; the literature includes Efron and Petrosian ([1999](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0005)), Martin and Betensky ([2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014)) and Shen ([2011](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0020)). In this section we briefly present the conditional Kendall's Tau method introduced by Martin and Betensky ([2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014)) and later revisited by Austin et al. ([2014](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0001)). This method is implemented in the `R` package `SurvTrunc`.

For ease of presentation we focus on interval sampling, which is the most important scenario of doubly truncated data, and where the couple ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0316.png) falls on a line with probability one. In such a case it is enough to measure the association between ![upper X](images/c05-i0317.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0318.png). The conditional Kendall's Tau is defined as

[(5.8)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0011)![tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n Baseline equals StartFraction 1 Over n Subscript upper X upper U Baseline EndFraction sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n minus 1 Endscripts sigma-summation Underscript j equals i Overscript n Endscripts sign left-brace left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline minus upper X Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline minus upper U Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis right-brace normal upper Omega Subscript i j Baseline comma](images/c05-math-0011.png) 

where ![n Subscript upper X upper U Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n minus 1 Endscripts sigma-summation Underscript j equals i Overscript n Endscripts normal upper Omega Subscript i j](images/c05-i0319.png) with ![normal upper Omega Subscript i j Baseline equals upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper V Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0320.png). The Kendall's Tau [(5.8)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0011) measures the association between ![upper X](images/c05-i0321.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0322.png) by using the ![n Subscript upper X upper U](images/c05-i0323.png) couples of observations that are comparable. In practice, ![n Subscript upper X upper U](images/c05-i0324.png) can be much smaller than the total amount of couples, ![n left-parenthesis n minus 1 right-parenthesis slash 2](images/c05-i0325.png). Importantly, due to the double truncation effects, the strength of the association that is measured through [(5.8)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0011) may be weaker than the one existing in the unobservable world. This should be taken into account when interpreting empirical results.

Under the null hypothesis of quasi‐independence, ![StartRoot n EndRoot tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n](images/c05-i0326.png) converges in distribution to a ![upper N left-parenthesis 0 comma nu right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0327.png) as ![n right-arrow infinity](images/c05-i0328.png). The asymptotic variance ![nu squared slash n](images/c05-i0329.png) of [(5.8)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0011) can be easily estimated from the data, as discussed in Section 6 of Martin and Betensky ([2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014)). A chi‐squared test statistic with one degree of freedom is thus given by ![n tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n slash nu squared](images/c05-i0330.png); that is, quasi‐independence is rejected when the ![p](images/c05-i0331.png)‐value ![upper P left-parenthesis chi 1 squared greater-than n tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n Baseline slash nu squared right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0332.png) is below the nominal level. When the truncating variables ![upper U](images/c05-i0333.png) and ![upper V](images/c05-i0334.png) do not determine each other, a chi‐squared test with two degrees of freedom can be constructed in a similar way. In such a case, the test statistic involves two conditional association measures, corresponding to ![upper X upper U](images/c05-i0335.png) and ![upper X upper V](images/c05-i0336.png) combinations (Martin and Betensky, [2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014)).

In order to illustrate the level and the power in finite samples of the conditional Kendall's Tau as implemented in the `SurvTrunc` package, we consider the following simulation scenario:

|  |  |  |  | Level |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ![theta](images/c05-i0385.png) | ![tau Subscript theta](images/c05-i0386.png) | ![p Subscript c](images/c05-i0387.png) | ![tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n](images/c05-i0388.png) | 0.01 | 0.05 | 0.1 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 0.0000 | 0.2035 | 0.0002 | 0.0000 | 0.0123 | 0.0370 |
| 1 | 0.3333 | 0.1818 | 0.0299 | 0.0000 | 0.0238 | 0.0833 |
| 2 | 0.5000 | 0.1730 | 0.0576 | 0.0118 | 0.1529 | 0.2824 |
| 4 | 0.6667 | 0.1642 | 0.1146 | 0.3140 | 0.5930 | 0.6628 |
| 8 | 0.8000 | 0.1597 | 0.2409 | 0.9880 | 0.9880 | 1.0000 |

[Table 5.5](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-tbl-0005) Simulation results for the conditional Kendall's Tau in models a) and b) (see text). Left: Clayton parameter ![theta](images/c05-i0377.png), Kendall's Tau between ![upper X](images/c05-i0378.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0379.png) ![tau Subscript theta](images/c05-i0380.png), proportion of comparable couples of ![upper X upper U](images/c05-i0381.png) observations ![p Subscript c](images/c05-i0382.png) and conditional Kendall's Tau ![tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n](images/c05-i0383.png); averaged along 1000 Monte Carlo trials. Right: proportion of rejections of the quasi‐independence assumption at nominal levels 0.01, 0.05 and 0.1 computed along 100 trials, according to the implementation of the test in `indeptestDT` function. The sample size is ![n equals 300](images/c05-i0384.png), and the truncating variables follow scenario I in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

1.  (Null hypothesis of quasi‐independence.) We consider the simulation scenario I in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016), in which ![upper X](images/c05-i0354.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0355.png) are independent. Note that independence implies quasi‐independence.
2.  (Alternative hypothesis of positive conditional Kendall's Tau.) We link ![upper X](images/c05-i0356.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0357.png) in scenario I of [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016) through a Clayton copula with parameter ![theta greater-than 0](images/c05-i0358.png) (Nelsen, [2006](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0016)). This means that the joint cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0359.png) is given by ![script upper C Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u right-parenthesis equals left-parenthesis x Superscript negative theta Baseline plus u overtilde Superscript negative theta Baseline minus 1 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 slash theta](images/c05-i0360.png), ![0 less-than x less-than 1](images/c05-i0361.png), ![negative 1 slash 3 less-than u less-than 1](images/c05-i0362.png), where ![u overtilde equals left-parenthesis 3 slash 4 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis u plus 1 slash 3 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0363.png). The usual Kendall's Tau between ![upper X](images/c05-i0364.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0365.png) in this model is given by ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals theta slash left-parenthesis theta plus 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0366.png); large values of ![tau Subscript theta](images/c05-i0367.png) correspond to relatively large ![tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n](images/c05-i0368.png)'s. See [Table 5.5](#c05.xhtml_c05-tbl-0005). An algorithm to simulate this model is the following; see exercise 4.17 in Nelsen ([2006](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0016)):

    1.  Step 1 Generate independent random variables ![upper Y 1](images/c05-i0369.png) and ![upper Y 2](images/c05-i0370.png) from an exponential model with unit rate.
    2.  Step 2 Independently generate ![upper Z 0](images/c05-i0371.png) from a gamma model with shape parameter ![1 slash theta](images/c05-i0372.png) and rate parameter 1 and compute ![upper U equals left-parenthesis 4 slash 3 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis 1 plus upper Y 2 slash upper Z 0 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 slash theta Baseline minus 1 slash 3](images/c05-i0373.png).
    3.  Step 3 Finally compute ![upper X equals left-parenthesis 1 plus upper Y 1 slash upper Z 0 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 slash theta](images/c05-i0374.png).

    Of course, couples ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0375.png) that violate the condition ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper U plus 1 slash 3](images/c05-i0376.png) are rejected.

In [Table 5.5](#c05.xhtml_c05-tbl-0005) we provide the proportion of comparable couples of ![upper X upper U](images/c05-i0394.png) observations (![p Subscript c](images/c05-i0395.png)) and the conditional Kendall's Tau averaged along 1000 Monte Carlo trials. The sample size is ![n equals 300](images/c05-i0396.png). Several values of the Clayton parameter ![theta](images/c05-i0397.png) are considered, the value ![theta equals 0](images/c05-i0398.png) corresponding to independent truncation. In [Table 5.5](#c05.xhtml_c05-tbl-0005) it is seen that ![tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n](images/c05-i0399.png) represents a small proportion of the existing association between ![upper X](images/c05-i0400.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0401.png), ranging from 9% to 30% and increasing with ![tau Subscript theta](images/c05-i0402.png). It is also evident that, for the simulated model, the proportion of comparable couples decreases as the association increases, being between 16% and 20% of the total number of couples. [Table 5.5](#c05.xhtml_c05-tbl-0005) provides the proportion of rejections of the quasi‐independence test of Martin and Betensky ([2005](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0014)) as implemented by `indeptestDT`. These proportions of rejections were calculated along only 100 trials due to the long waiting times when running `indeptestDT`. In the simulations the conditional Kendall's Tau was very conservative under quasi‐independence, and was unable to detect a real association of ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 1 slash 3](images/c05-i0403.png). Results were better for larger association degrees, the statistical power of ![tau Subscript upper X upper U comma n](images/c05-i0404.png) increasing with ![tau Subscript theta](images/c05-i0405.png) as expected. This is in agreement with the sampling distribution of the conditional Kendall's Tau as depicted in [Figure 5.9](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0009). We also mention that the application of the function `indeptestDT` was not conclusive for about 14–19% of the trials (depending on the value of ![theta](images/c05-i0406.png)), apparently due to problems with the estimation of the variance.

![Graphs depict Conditional cumulative incidences for lymphoma (solid lines) and other cancer types (dashed lines) given gender (black for males, red for females).](images/c05f009.png)

[Figure 5.9](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-fig-0009) Histograms for the conditional Kendall's Tau, corresponding to 1000 trials of the simulated models a) and b) (see text) with sample size ![n equals 300](images/c05-i0389.png). Top row: ![tau equals 0](images/c05-i0390.png) (left), ![tau equals 0.5](images/c05-i0391.png) (right). Bottom row: ![tau equals 0.67](images/c05-i0392.png) (left), ![tau equals 0.8](images/c05-i0393.png) (right).

## 5.4 Dependent Truncation

In the previous section we discussed how to assess the assumption of independent truncation. When independent truncation is rejected, or when there exists external information on the association between the truncating variables ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0407.png) and the target ![upper X](images/c05-i0408.png), methods alternative to the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE are needed. In this section we review a recent approach based on copulas for the estimation of a doubly truncated cdf under possibly dependent truncation. This extends copula‐based estimation under dependent one‐sided truncation (Emura and Wang, [2012](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0006)) to the doubly truncated setting. See Chiou et al. ([2019](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0004)) for alternative ideas to deal with dependent truncation via transformation models.

Copula functions (Nelsen, [2006](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0016)) have been often used in statistics to handle dependence in a number of settings. In particular, in Survival Analysis copulas have served to model the issues of dependent censoring and dependent truncation. The copula‐based approach starts by splitting a multivariate distribution of interest into two components: (i) the marginal distributions, and (ii) the link among the margins. How to estimate both components depends on the particular setting one is working with.

Similar to the case in [Section 5.3](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0015), for ease of presentation we consider the situation of interval sampling, in which double truncation is induced by a single truncating variable ![upper U](images/c05-i0409.png), because ![upper V equals upper U plus final sigma](images/c05-i0410.png) for a known positive constant ![final sigma](images/c05-i0411.png). This has been carefully studied by Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)). For the alternative scenario in which the couple ![left-parenthesis upper U comma upper V right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0412.png) is not concentrated on a line, a similar approach is in principle possible, with the extra complexity entailed by the multivariate nature of the truncation variable. The details of such an extension are however missing in the related literature.

A copula function is a bivariate distribution on the unit square ![normal upper I squared equals left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket times left-bracket 0 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c05-i0413.png) with uniform margins. Let ![upper F Subscript upper X upper U](images/c05-i0414.png) denote the bivariate cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0415.png), and let ![upper F](images/c05-i0416.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0417.png) be its margins. As in previous chapters we denote by ![left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket](images/c05-i0418.png) and ![left-bracket a Subscript upper U Baseline comma b Subscript upper U Baseline right-bracket](images/c05-i0419.png) the respective supports of ![upper F](images/c05-i0420.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0421.png). According to Sklar's theorem (Nelsen, [2006](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0016)), there exists a copula function ![script upper C](images/c05-i0422.png) such that

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column upper F Subscript upper X upper U Baseline left-parenthesis x comma u right-parenthesis equals script upper C left-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma upper K Subscript upper U Baseline left-parenthesis u right-parenthesis right-parenthesis comma left-parenthesis x comma u right-parenthesis element-of left-bracket a Subscript upper X Baseline comma b Subscript upper X Baseline right-bracket times left-bracket a Subscript upper U Baseline comma b Subscript upper U Baseline right-bracket period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c05-math-0012.png)

Furthermore, if ![upper F](images/c05-i0423.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0424.png) are continuous, the copula ![script upper C](images/c05-i0425.png) is unique. When ![script upper C left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis equals s 1 s 2](images/c05-i0426.png), ![0 less-than-or-equal-to s 1 comma s 2 less-than-or-equal-to 1](images/c05-i0427.png), the variables ![upper X](images/c05-i0428.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0429.png) are independent but, otherwise, they are dependent. The association between ![upper X](images/c05-i0430.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0431.png) induced by a given copula function ![script upper C](images/c05-i0432.png) is often measured through the Kendall's Tau, which is given by

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column tau Subscript script upper C Baseline equals 4 integral Underscript normal upper I squared Endscripts script upper C left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis normal d script upper C left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis period 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c05-math-0013.png)

Dependent truncation can be handled in a relatively easy way through a copula model ![script upper C Subscript theta](images/c05-i0433.png) indexed by a univariate or multivariate parameter ![theta](images/c05-i0434.png). Plenty of examples are given in Nelsen ([2006](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0016)). Here we only quote three of them for illustration purposes; in the following we put ![tau Subscript theta](images/c05-i0435.png) for ![tau Subscript script upper C Sub Subscript theta](images/c05-i0436.png):

1.  C1. Clayton copula: ![script upper C Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis equals left-bracket max left-parenthesis s 1 Superscript negative 1 slash theta Baseline plus s 2 Superscript negative 1 slash theta Baseline minus 1 comma 0 right-parenthesis right-bracket Superscript negative 1 slash theta](images/c05-i0437.png), ![theta element-of left-bracket negative 1 comma infinity right-parenthesis reverse-solidus StartSet 0 EndSet](images/c05-i0438.png). Kendall's Tau: ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals theta slash left-parenthesis theta plus 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0439.png).
2.  C2. Frank copula: ![script upper C Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis equals minus theta Superscript negative 1 Baseline log left-bracket 1 plus left-parenthesis normal e Superscript minus theta s 1 Baseline minus 1 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis normal e Superscript minus theta s 2 Baseline minus 1 right-parenthesis slash left-parenthesis normal e Superscript negative theta Baseline minus 1 right-parenthesis right-bracket](images/c05-i0440.png), ![theta element-of left-parenthesis negative infinity comma infinity right-parenthesis reverse-solidus StartSet 0 EndSet](images/c05-i0441.png). Kendall's Tau: ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 1 plus 4 left-parenthesis upper D 1 left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis minus 1 right-parenthesis slash theta](images/c05-i0442.png), where ![upper D 1 left-parenthesis theta right-parenthesis equals theta Superscript negative 1 Baseline integral Subscript 0 Superscript theta Baseline t left-parenthesis normal e Superscript t Baseline minus 1 right-parenthesis Superscript negative 1 Baseline normal d t](images/c05-i0443.png) is the Debye function of the first kind.
3.  C3. Farlie–Gumbel–Morgenstern (FGM) copula: ![script upper C Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis equals s 1 s 2 plus theta s 1 s 2 left-parenthesis 1 minus s 1 right-parenthesis left-parenthesis 1 minus s 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0444.png), ![theta element-of left-bracket negative 1 comma 1 right-bracket](images/c05-i0445.png). Kendall's Tau: ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 2 theta slash 9](images/c05-i0446.png).

Clayton, Frank and FGM copulas have the independent copula as a special (limiting) case (![theta right-arrow 0](images/c05-i0452.png)). Given a copula family ![script upper C Subscript theta](images/c05-i0453.png), the copula parameter ![theta](images/c05-i0454.png) and the margins ![upper F](images/c05-i0455.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0456.png) can be estimated by maximum likelihood principles. Details are given below.

In [Figure 5.10](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0010) we depict scatterplots of ![n equals 1000](images/c05-i0457.png) couples ![left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0458.png) simulated from the Clayton copula with three different association degrees. Dependence is hardly guessed when ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 0.2](images/c05-i0459.png), but it is clearly seen when the Kendall's Tau is 0.5 or larger. In [Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0011) the scatterplots correspond to the copula families C1 and C2; the Kendall's Tau is 0.9 in the two cases. From [Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml_c05-fig-0011) it is seen how different copula functions will induce different dependence patterns. This should be taken into account when choosing a particular copula family ![script upper C Subscript theta](images/c05-i0460.png) to model the dependent truncation.

A non‐trivial issue in copula‐based estimation from incomplete data is the identifiability of the copula. That is, before introducing an estimator for ![theta](images/c05-i0464.png), one should guarantee that two different ![theta](images/c05-i0465.png)‐values cannot generate the same distribution for ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0466.png) in the observable region ![upper U less-than-or-equal-to upper X less-than-or-equal-to upper U plus final sigma](images/c05-i0467.png). This leads to the following definition. Let ![script upper C Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0468.png) denote the second‐order derivative of ![script upper C](images/c05-i0469.png) with respect to its two arguments.

![Histograms depicting the conditional Kendall's Tau, corresponding to 1000 trials of the simulated model with sample size n=300.](images/c05f010.png)

[Figure 5.10](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-fig-0010) Scatterplot of ![n equals 1000](images/c05-i0447.png) observations ![left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0448.png) simulated from the Clayton copula. From left to right: ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 0.2](images/c05-i0449.png), ![0.5](images/c05-i0450.png) and ![0.9](images/c05-i0451.png).

![c05f011](images/c05f011.png)

[Figure 5.11](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-fig-0011) Scatterplot of ![n equals 1000](images/c05-i0461.png) observations ![left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0462.png) simulated from the Clayton copula (left plot) and the Frank copula (right plot). The Kendall's Tau is ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 0.9](images/c05-i0463.png) in both cases.

Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)) proved that the identifiability condition in [Definition 5.4.1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0024) is enough in the setting of interval sampling. In other words, they proved that, under the condition in [Definition 5.4.1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0024), two different ![left-parenthesis script upper C comma upper F comma upper K Subscript upper U Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0483.png) triplets by force induce different joint distributions for ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0484.png) in the observable region. Copula families C1–C3 above are examples of copulas that satisfy [Definition 5.4.1](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0024).

When ![upper X](images/c05-i0485.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0486.png) are linked through a copula function ![script upper C](images/c05-i0487.png), the joint pdf of the observable ![left-parenthesis upper X 1 comma upper U 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0488.png), with ![upper U 1 less-than-or-equal-to upper X 1 less-than-or-equal-to upper U 1 plus final sigma](images/c05-i0489.png), is proportional to ![script upper C Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis upper F left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma upper K Subscript upper U Baseline left-parenthesis u right-parenthesis right-parenthesis f left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis k Subscript upper U Baseline left-parenthesis u right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0490.png), ![a Subscript upper X Baseline less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper X](images/c05-i0491.png), ![a Subscript upper U Baseline less-than-or-equal-to u less-than-or-equal-to b Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0492.png), ![u less-than-or-equal-to x less-than-or-equal-to u plus final sigma](images/c05-i0493.png), where ![f](images/c05-i0494.png) and ![k Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0495.png) denote the pdf of ![upper X](images/c05-i0496.png) and ![upper U](images/c05-i0497.png), respectively. In order to derive the likelihood of the sample ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript i Baseline comma upper U Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0498.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c05-i0499.png), we proceed as in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml) by assuming that ![upper F](images/c05-i0500.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0501.png) are concentrated respectively on the ![upper X Subscript i](images/c05-i0502.png)'s and ![upper U Subscript i](images/c05-i0503.png)'s, with masses ![phi Subscript i Baseline equals upper F left-brace upper X Subscript i Baseline right-brace](images/c05-i0504.png) and ![psi Subscript i Baseline equals upper K Subscript upper U Baseline left-brace upper U Subscript i Baseline right-brace](images/c05-i0505.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c05-i0506.png). Introduce

![StartLayout 1st Row 1st Column script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline equals script upper C Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline left-parenthesis sigma-summation Underscript k equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts phi Subscript k Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper X Subscript k Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis comma sigma-summation Underscript k equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts psi Subscript k Baseline upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript k Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper U Subscript j Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis 2nd Column Blank 3rd Column Blank EndLayout](images/c05-math-0015.png)

and ![normal upper Phi Subscript j Superscript script upper C Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline phi Subscript i Baseline upper J Subscript j i](images/c05-i0507.png), where ![upper J Subscript j i Baseline equals upper I left-parenthesis upper U Subscript j Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Subscript i Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper U Subscript j Baseline plus final sigma right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0508.png). With this notation the likelihood can be written as

[(5.9)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0016)![script upper L Subscript script upper C Baseline left-parenthesis phi comma psi right-parenthesis equals product Underscript i equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts StartFraction script upper C Subscript i i Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline phi Subscript i Baseline psi Subscript i Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts normal upper Phi Subscript j Superscript script upper C Baseline psi Subscript j Baseline EndFraction comma](images/c05-math-0016.png) 

with ![phi equals left-parenthesis phi 1 comma ellipsis comma phi Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c05-i0509.png) and ![psi equals left-parenthesis psi 1 comma ellipsis comma psi Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis Superscript t](images/c05-i0510.png). For the independence copula ![script upper C left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 right-parenthesis equals s 1 s 2](images/c05-i0511.png), ![0 less-than-or-equal-to s 1 comma s 2 less-than-or-equal-to 1](images/c05-i0512.png), it holds ![script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline equals 1](images/c05-i0513.png) for ![1 less-than-or-equal-to comma i comma j less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c05-i0514.png) and, hence, the likelihood [(5.9)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0016) reduces to the one in [Chapter 2](#c02.xhtml), see [Eq. (2.4)](#c02.xhtml_c02-disp-0008).

Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)) introduced two different iterative algorithms to maximize the likelihood [(5.9)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0016) for a given parametric copula family ![script upper C Subscript theta](images/c05-i0515.png). These two algorithms are termed *simple* and *full* in the referred paper. Both algorithms iterate up to reaching a solution for the score equations that arise from the first partial derivatives of the log likelihood with respect to ![theta](images/c05-i0516.png), ![phi](images/c05-i0517.png) and ![psi](images/c05-i0518.png). The difference between the two algorithms is in the computation of the partial derivatives. The simple algorithm deals with ![script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0519.png) as if it were free of ![phi](images/c05-i0520.png) and ![psi](images/c05-i0521.png), thus leading to relatively plain score equations. In its turn, the full algorithm computes the partial derivatives by taking into account the fact that ![script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0522.png) actually involves both ![phi](images/c05-i0523.png) and ![psi](images/c05-i0524.png); the score equations are of course more involved in this case. One interesting finding in Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)) is that the simple algorithm behaves in a competitive way, while saving computational time. Both the simple and the full algorithms end with an estimator ![left-parenthesis theta Subscript n Baseline comma upper F Subscript n Baseline comma upper K Subscript upper U comma n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0525.png) of the triplet ![left-parenthesis theta comma upper F comma upper K Subscript upper U Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0526.png). The bivariate cdf of ![left-parenthesis upper X comma upper U right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0527.png) at point ![left-parenthesis x comma u right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0528.png) can be then estimated by ![script upper C Subscript theta Sub Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis upper F Subscript n Baseline left-parenthesis x right-parenthesis comma upper K Subscript upper U comma n Baseline left-parenthesis u right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0529.png).

By using the aforementioned approach of the simple algorithm, the following score equations are immediately obtained:

[(5.10)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0017)![phi Subscript i Baseline equals StartFraction 1 slash normal upper Psi Subscript i Superscript script upper C Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts 1 slash normal upper Psi Subscript j Superscript script upper C Baseline EndFraction comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n comma](images/c05-math-0017.png) 

where ![normal upper Psi Subscript i Superscript script upper C Baseline equals sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis Baseline psi Subscript j Baseline upper J Subscript j i](images/c05-i0530.png), ![1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n](images/c05-i0531.png), and

[(5.11)](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-disp-0018)![psi Subscript i Baseline equals StartFraction 1 slash normal upper Phi Subscript i Superscript script upper C Baseline Over sigma-summation Underscript j equals 1 Overscript n Endscripts 1 slash normal upper Phi Subscript j Superscript script upper C Baseline EndFraction comma 1 less-than-or-equal-to i less-than-or-equal-to n period](images/c05-math-0018.png) 

Given a parametric copula family ![script upper C Subscript theta](images/c05-i0532.png), the simple algorithm as introduced in Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)) proceeds as follows:

1.  SA1. Take ![theta Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis Baseline equals argmax Subscript theta Baseline script upper L Subscript script upper C Sub Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis phi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis Baseline comma psi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0533.png), where ![phi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0534.png) and ![psi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0535.png) are initial solutions for ![phi](images/c05-i0536.png) and ![psi](images/c05-i0537.png).
2.  SA2. Use [(5.11)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0018) to improve ![psi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0538.png) and get ![psi Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0539.png), say; that is, use ![theta Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0540.png) and ![phi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0541.png) to compute ![normal upper Phi Subscript j Superscript script upper C Super Subscript theta](images/c05-i0542.png) in [(5.11)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0018).
3.  SA3. Use [(5.10)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0017) to improve ![phi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0543.png) and get ![phi Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0544.png), say; that is, use ![theta Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0545.png) and ![psi Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0546.png) to compute ![normal upper Psi Subscript j Superscript script upper C Super Subscript theta](images/c05-i0547.png) in [(5.10)](#c05.xhtml_c05-disp-0017).
4.  SA4. Compute ![theta Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline equals argmax Subscript theta Baseline script upper L Subscript script upper C Sub Subscript theta Baseline left-parenthesis phi Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline comma psi Superscript left-parenthesis 1 right-parenthesis Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0548.png).

In steps SA2 and SA3 the ![script upper C Subscript i j Superscript left-parenthesis 1 comma 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0552.png) are computed from the same triplet ![left-parenthesis theta Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis Baseline comma phi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis Baseline comma psi Superscript left-parenthesis 0 right-parenthesis Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0553.png). Steps SA2–SA4 are repeated until reaching convergence.

In the next example we apply the simple algorithm SA1–SA4 to two real datasets: the AIDS Blood Transfusion Data of [Section 1.4.2](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0010), and the Childhood Cancer Data of [Section 1.4.1](#c01.xhtml_c01-sec-0009). Copula families C1–C3 above are used in the example. For each copula, the standard error of ![theta Subscript n](images/c05-i0558.png) is approximated by bootstrapping. For this, the copula‐based obvious bootstrap proposed by Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)) is applied. This method proceeds as follows:

![c05f012](images/c05f012.png)

[Figure 5.12](#c05.xhtml_R_c05-fig-0012) (Simulated scenario I with dependent truncation, [Example 5.4.2](#c05.xhtml_c05-fea-0026)). NPMLE of ![upper F](images/c05-i0549.png) based on single trial of size ![n equals 1000](images/c05-i0550.png) from the Clayton (left plot) and Frank (right plot) copulas. Kendall's Tau is ![tau Subscript theta Baseline equals 0.5](images/c05-i0551.png) in both cases. For comparison purposes, the Efron–Petrosian NPMLE, which is based on the independence copula, is also displayed.

1.  CB1. Draw a couple of uniform random variables ![left-parenthesis upper Y Subscript upper X Baseline comma upper Y Subscript upper U Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0559.png) from ![script upper C Subscript theta Sub Subscript n](images/c05-i0560.png).
2.  CB2. Do ![upper X Superscript normal b Baseline equals upper F Subscript n Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis upper Y Subscript upper X Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0561.png) and ![upper U Superscript normal b Baseline equals upper K Subscript upper U comma n Superscript negative 1 Baseline left-parenthesis upper Y Subscript upper U Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0562.png), where ![upper F Subscript n Superscript negative 1](images/c05-i0563.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U comma n Superscript negative 1](images/c05-i0564.png) are the quantile functions of the copula‐based NPMLEs of ![upper F](images/c05-i0565.png) and ![upper K Subscript upper U](images/c05-i0566.png).
3.  CB3. Repeat step CB2 until ![upper U Superscript normal b Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper X Superscript normal b Baseline less-than-or-equal-to upper U Superscript normal b Baseline plus final sigma](images/c05-i0567.png) is fulfilled.

Nelsen ([2006](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0016)) provides simulation algorithms for specific copula families that can be used in step CB1.

In Moreira et al. ([2021](#c05.xhtml_c05-bib-0015)) the performance of the algorithm SA1–SA4, together with that of the alternative full algorithm, was thoroughly investigated through simulations. The estimators performed accurately in the simulated scenarios in that paper, supporting the copula‐based approach for dependent double truncation. Still, numerical instabilities may appear due to the sensitiveness of the algorithm with respect to the initial solution, or the existence of local maxima. Further investigation of these issues is required in order to improve the robustness of the procedures.

### 5.5.1 Code for Example 5.1.3

In this example, the goal was to compare the distribution of the incubation time for children vs. people older than 15 years.

NPMLE estimators of both groups can be plotted for visual comparison.

A PP‐plot for the ordinary and the corrected estimators can be computed as follows:

The ordinary ecdf jointly with a pointwise band can be produced using `survdiff` and `survfit` in package `survival`.

Finally, the NPMLE with pointwise confidence bands based on a normal approximation with bootstrap standard errors are also useful for comparing the two groups.

### 5.5.5 Code for Example 5.4.3

To compute the nonparametric estimator of a distribution function under dependence, we consider the algorithm described in [Section 5.4](#c05.xhtml_c05-sec-0018). This algorithm makes use of the first order derivatives of FGM, Clayton and Frank copulas, which are available in package `VineCopula`.

For using these specific copula models, some initial solutions (![phi](images/c05-i0581.png) and ![psi](images/c05-i0582.png) in step SA1 in the algorithm) must be passed. We used ![n Superscript negative 1](images/c05-i0583.png) (being ![n](images/c05-i0584.png) the sample size) for both variables. Then, the log likelihood of ![theta](images/c05-i0585.png) is computed using the initial solutions. The log likelihood can be maximized using `optim` until convergence is reached. For that purpose, a maximum error must be fixed for ![phi](images/c05-i0586.png), ![psi](images/c05-i0587.png) and ![theta](images/c05-i0588.png).

As an example, the log likelihood for the Clayton copula can be computed as follows:

For other copulas, when using the `R` function `BiCopPDF` of `VineCopula` package, we shall change the number corresponding to the desired copula family.

# A
Packages and Functions in `R`

Throughout this book, we have mainly used the functions in package `DTDA` for data analyses. Nevertheless, there are also other packages in `R` ( `R` Core Team, [2020](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0012)) that can be useful for analysing doubly truncated data; some of them have been already mentioned in the book chapters. In this Appendix we will briefly revised the functions included in packages `DTDA.ni` (de Uña‐Álvarez and Soage, [2018](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0006)), `DTDA.cif` (de Uña‐Álvarez and Soage, [2020](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0007)), `SurvTrunc` (Rennert, [2018](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0013)) and `double.truncation` (Emura et al., [2019](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0002)), together with the functions belonging to `DTDA` that have been used in the illustrative examples in this book. See Moreira et al. ([2010](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0010)) for an introduction to `DTDA`, and Moreira et al. ([2021](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0011)) for the description of the new functions included in its latest version 3.0.

`DTDA` includes a variety of functions that allow to compute the NPMLE from doubly truncated data with different algorithms, jointly with bootstrap confidence intervals, and to obtain kernel estimates of the density and hazard functions, with suitable bandwidth selectors in this setting. An alternative estimator of the NPMLE, avoiding an iterative procedure, is implemented in `DTDA.ni` ; see de Uña‐Álvarez ([2018](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0003)) for details on this non‐iterative estimator. Package `double.truncation` includes a function for obtaining the NPMLE jointly with an asymptotic standard error (Emura et al., [2015](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0001)). Likelihood‐based methods implemented in `double.truncation` also enable the computation of parametric estimators. `SurvTrunc` also allows to get the NPMLE with bootstrap standard errors. In addition, Cox regression, as proposed by Rennert and Xie ([2018](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0014)), is also possible with `SurvTrunc`. Finally, cumulative incidence function estimation for competing risks can be done with `DTDA.cif`, providing the two different estimators proposed in de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020a](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0005)). Bootstrap standard errors for the cumulative incidences are also available from `DTDA.cif`.

In what follows, functions and packages (denoted as `function` ( `package`)) will be briefly described, organized according to the most relevant tasks when proceeding with a doubly truncated data analysis. In de Uña‐Álvarez ([2020b](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0004)) comparative studies for several of these functions can be found.

## A.1 Computing the NPMLE and Standard Errors

`efron.petrosian` ( `DTDA`): the NPMLE estimator is computed using the first algorithm by Efron and Petrosian. Pointwise confidence limits can be also computed, using a simple bootstrap method.

`lynden` ( `DTDA`): the NPMLE estimator is computed using the second algorithm by Efron and Petrosian. As for the previous function, simple bootstrap (pointwise) confidence bands can be also obtained.

`shen` ( `DTDA`): the NPMLE estimator is computed using Shen's algorithm, jointly with marginal distributions of the truncation variables. Bootstrap (with simple or obvious methods) can be also computed.

`DTDAni` ( `DTDA.ni`): this function produces a non‐iterative estimator of the cumulative distribution of a doubly truncated variable, for an interval sampling scenario with interval width `tau`. Confidence bands are not provided.

`NPMLE` ( `double.truncation`): the NPMLE is computed with Shen's algorithm and standard errors are obtained using the asymptotic variance formula in Emura et al. (2015).

`cdfDT` ( `SurvTrunc`): also using Shen's algorithm, this function provides 95% confidence limits using simple bootstrap and the normal approximation method.

## A.2 Assessing the Existence and Uniqueness of the NPMLE

A necessary condition for the existence and uniqueness of the NPMLE can be checked as follows:

Both `min(c1)` and `min(c2)` must be strictly greater than 1, otherwise the NPMLE does not exist or is not unique. The functions in `DTDA` report a warning message when `min(c1)==1` or `min(c2)==1`.

As mentioned by Xiao and Hudgens ([2019](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0015)), the package `igraph` can be used to determine subsamples for which the NPMLE exists and is unique. Actually, such subsamples are strongly connected subgraphs as defined in Xiao and Hudgens ([2019](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0015)). The following lines allow to get, describe and visualize the strongly connected subgraphs from a given doubly truncated dataset with target variable `x` and truncation limits `u` and `v` :

## A.3 Semiparametric and Parametric Estimation

`PMLE.dist` ( `double.truncation`): a series of functions performing parametric estimation. Distribution models ( `dist`) considered are the `loglogistic`, `log‐normal`, `Weibull` and special exponential families. Estimation is performed by maximum likelihood. In practice, we have found that a log‐transformation of the random variables (both target and truncation variables) is required.

Regarding the semiparametric estimation it should be noted that, although there is not a specific function in `R`, there are many functions that allow for including weights. For that purpose, it is useful to keep in a vector the parametric estimation of the biasing function ![upper G](images/bapp01-i0001.png), evaluated in the sample values. Normalized weights computed from this vector may be passed to `weighted.mean` or `lm`, among others. As a note of caution, computation of standard errors directly obtained from these functions may not be correct, since they do not incorporate the randomness of the weights.

## A.4 Kernel Estimation

`densityDT` ( `DTDA`): this function computes the kernel density estimator under double truncation. Argument `bw` allows for choosing different bandwidth selectors, specifically: normal‐reference rule ( `NR`), plug‐in in one or two stages ( `DPI1`, `DPI2`), least‐squares cross‐validation ( `LSCV`) or smoothed bootstrap ( `SBoot`).

`hazardDT` ( `DTDA`): this function computes the kernel estimator of the hazard function under double truncation, with a LSCV bandwidth.

## A.5 Regression Analysis

`coxDT` ( `SurvTrunc`): this function allows to fit a Cox proportional hazards regression model, with doubly truncated survival time. It also computes bootstrap standard errors and confidence intervals. It implements the approach in Rennert and Xie ([2018](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0014)).

Cox regression as proposed by Mandel et al. ([2018](#b01.xhtml_bapp01-bib-0009)) can be performed from the function `coxph` in package `survival` by introducing ![minus log left-parenthesis upper W right-parenthesis](images/bapp01-i0002.png) as an offset, where ![upper W](images/bapp01-i0003.png) contains the evaluations of ![upper G Subscript n](images/bapp01-i0004.png) or ![upper G left-parenthesis dot semicolon theta Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/bapp01-i0005.png) on the sample values. In this case, the standard errors given by `coxph` are not valid and must be re‐calculated by bootstrapping.

## A.6 Competing Risks

`DTDAcif` ( `DTDA.cif`): this function estimates nonparametrically the cumulative incidences of competing risks under double truncation.

Cause‐specific hazards regression can be performed from the `coxph` function by using as an offset the weights ![upper W](images/bapp01-i0006.png) in the previous section.

## A.7 Simulating Data

`rsim.DT` ( `DTDA`): random generation of doubly truncated data with two different patterns of observational bias, specified by arguments `model` and `tau` (the width of the sampling interval). The two implemented models correspond to the simulation scenarios I and II in [Section 2.1.3](#c02.xhtml_c02-sec-0016).

`simu.Weibull` ( `double.truncation`): random generation of doubly truncated data from a Weibull model. Arguments `mu` and `sigma` correspond to the Weibull parameters and `delta` controls the inclusion probability.

## A.8 Testing Quasi‐independence

`indeptestDT` ( `SurvTrunc`): this function computes a quasi‐independence tests based on the conditional Kendall's Tau.

## A.9 Dependent Truncation

`BiCopPDF` ( `VineCopula`): this function evaluates the pdf of a given bivariate copula. Argument `family` (a number) allows to consider the Clayton and FGM copulas, among others.
